Optimal Control and Dissipativity of Linear Hermitian Matrix-Valued Dynamical Systems
Corentin Briat

Abstract
We develop a unified framework for linear-cost optimal control, finite-time optimal steering, dissipativity analysis, and zero-sum differential games for linear impulsive dynamical systems whose state is a Hermitian matrix evolving in ℍ ⪰ 0 n + m \mathbb{H}^{n+m}_{\succeq 0} . The system class encompasses continuous-time (CT) and discrete-time (DT) linear systems as degenerate cases, switched systems, and includes the dynamics of second-order moments of linear (stochastic) hybrid systems as a particular instance. All results rest on three tools: a single key identity relating cost, trajectory, and a dual variable; an Extended Schur complement lemma; and a Schur inner-product decomposition. Applied identically to the flow integral and to each jump, these yield structurally uniform sufficient and necessary conditions, dual linear matrix inequality (LMI) characterizations, and explicit optimal policies across all the considered problem classes. For optimal control and dissipativity, we derive finite-horizon sufficient conditions (via a generalized impulsive Riccati equation, I-GRE), necessary conditions, and dual LMI representations, then extend all results to the infinite horizon under LTV assumptions, without assuming time invariance or periodicity, via a monotone convergence argument. For finite-time optimal steering, Lagrangian duality reduces the terminal-constraint problem to an unconstrained optimal control problem; the optimal policy is characterized by a two-point boundary value problem and computed by a forward-backward algorithm. Zero-sum games are handled via the Hamilton-Jacobi-Isaacs version of the I-GRE under Isaacs’ condition. For the three problems that admit dwell-time extensions (optimal control, dissipativity, and games), causal policies under periodic, minimum, and range dwell-time constraints are derived by combining a forward Riccati ODE over a bounded timer interval with a discrete-time Riccati equation as the boundary condition at the end of the dwell period. Examples are discussed in the related sections.



1
Introduction

Hermitian matrix-valued dynamical systems form an important class
of systems whose states are Hermitian matrices evolving in time.
They arise naturally whenever the quantity of interest is a
second-order moment, a covariance, or a Gram matrix, all quantities
of central importance in stochastic control, optimal transport,
information geometry, and signal
processing
[
59
,
20
,
4
,
2
]
.
In particular, the class of systems that leave the cone of positive
semidefinite matrices invariant is of special relevance, as it
encompasses the dynamics of state covariance and second-order moment
matrices associated e.g., with linear stochastic
systems
[
46
,
25
,
29
,
27
]
.
Stability analysis and stabilization of such systems, including
continuous-time, discrete-time, and impulsive variants, were studied
in
[
16
]
under a unified framework, extending earlier analyses
conducted separately for specific problem classes and under additional
structural assumptions
[
30
,
52
,
50
,
25
,
46
]
.
The present paper extends that work to the problems of optimal
control, optimal steering, dissipativity, and zero-sum differential
games.

The view of linear systems through the lens of second-order information
and covariance representations has a long history.
The systematic treatment of covariance control (the problem of steering
and assigning the state covariance of a stochastic system by means of
linear state feedback) was pioneered by Hotz and Skelton
[
45
]

for discrete-time systems, and subsequently placed within a broader
algebraic framework in the monograph by Skelton, Iwasaki, and
Grigoriadis
[
59
]
, which unifies the design of covariance
controllers,
H_{2}
controllers, and
H_{\infty}
controllers under a
common linear-algebra formulation involving covariance matrices and
matrix inequalities.
More recently, Gattami
[
31
]
proposed an
H_{\infty}
analysis
method based directly on the state covariance, and Bamieh
[
4
]

gave a comprehensive treatment of optimal control problems in systems and controls
using a covariance-representation approach in which LQ objectives become
linear-in-matrix semidefinite programs and LMIs emerge as the natural
conal constraints of their duals.
The present paper adopts a similar point of view but works directly with
the matrix-valued second-moment process as a dynamical system in its own
right, rather than as a representation of an underlying vector state.

The classical linear quadratic regulator (LQR) for vector-state linear
systems is characterized by the algebraic or differential Riccati
equation; see
[
1
]
for a comprehensive treatment of
matrix Riccati equations and their role in systems theory,
and
[
7
,
8
]
for the dynamic-programming
foundations of optimal control on which the necessary conditions of
this paper rest.
When the cost matrices are allowed to be indefinite, the problem becomes
significantly more involved: the generalized differential Riccati equation
and the corresponding LMI conditions were studied in depth in the series
of papers
[
51
,
53
,
52
,
50
]
,
which established solvability conditions, asymptotic behavior, and the
connection to stochastic LQ control.
For systems with periodic parameters or periodically sampled inputs, the
periodic Riccati equation plays the central role; the theory of periodic
systems and its connections to
H_{2}
and
H_{\infty}
control are surveyed
in
[
11
]
.
In all of these references, the infinite-horizon results are obtained
under a time-invariance or periodicity assumption on the system and
cost operators.

Impulsive and jump linear systems, in which a continuous-time flow is
combined with instantaneous state resets at a sequence of event times,
arise in sampled-data control
[
48
,
37
,
14
,
15
]
, networked control systems
[
48
,
40
,
22
]
, reset systems
[
5
]
and the modeling
of physical systems subject to impulses
[
18
]
.
The
H_{2}
and
H_{\infty}
theory for linear time-varying and jump systems
was developed in
[
46
]
, which established the connection
between jump systems and sampled-data systems and derived the associated
Riccati equations in the LTV setting; the
H_{\infty}
analysis of
sampled-data systems through this jump-system equivalence was developed
in
[
58
]
.
Markov jump linear systems, where the jump times and the post-jump
parameters are governed by a Markov chain, have been studied extensively;
see
[
25
,
29
]
for continuous-time and
[
24
,
28
]

for discrete-time systems.
Sampled-data control of switched and Markov jump linear systems has been
investigated in depth by Geromel and co-workers
[
35
,
36
,
60
]
.
More recent contributions, including optimal control for linear impulsive
stochastic systems and their application to sampled-data problems, can be
found in
[
27
]
.
In all these references, the treatment of infinite-horizon problems
assumes, either explicitly or implicitly, that the system parameters are
time-invariant or periodic.

Covariance steering in finite time, the problem of steering the
state second-moment of a linear stochastic system from a prescribed
initial value to a prescribed terminal one while minimizing a given
cost, was formulated and solved for continuous-time LTV systems via a
Schrödinger bridge interpretation in the series of papers by Chen,
Georgiou, and Pavon
[
20
,
21
]
, where the optimal policy
is characterized by a system of two coupled Riccati or Lyapunov equations
and shown to coincide with an LQG controller with a specific terminal cost
weight. Goldshtein and Tsiotras
[
38
]
addressed the discrete
time-varying case and provided a computational algorithm, while
Bakolas
[
3
]
treated the problem subject to integral
quadratic constraints. The extension of optimal steering to impulsive systems, where
the second-moment is subject to resets at each impulse time, has not
been addressed in the literature.

Dissipativity theory, introduced by Willems
[
63
]
, provides
a general framework for characterising the energy-like behavior of
dynamical systems in terms of supply rates and storage functions
[
55
,
61
,
19
]
.
It unifies a broad range of analysis and design questions, including
passivity,
H_{\infty}
performance, and integral quadratic
constraints
[
56
]
, under a single input-output framework.
For linear systems, dissipativity is equivalent to the existence of a
solution to a Lyapunov or Riccati inequality, and the available storage
and required supply have natural state-space characterizations.
The dissipativity of impulsive systems has been studied in several
works
[
39
]
; however, to the best of our knowledge, a treatment in the
matrix-valued setting that addresses both finite- and infinite-horizon
conditions under LTV assumptions and with indefinite supply rates is
not available in the literature.

The theory of two-player zero-sum differential games for linear systems
was developed in detail by Başar and Olsder
[
6
]
, and
the connection to
H_{\infty}
optimal control
[
26
]
, formulated
as a minimax problem over the disturbance input, is well established;
for linear time-varying systems, the corresponding state-space

H_{\infty}
and game solutions were developed in
[
54
]
.
The key role of the Hamilton–Jacobi–Isaacs equation, and its reduction
to a Riccati-type equation in the linear-quadratic case, places the
game-theoretic problem squarely within the framework of matrix Riccati
equations; see again
[
1
]
for the game-theoretic Riccati
equations and
[
6
]
for the general theory.

An important class of causal policies for impulsive systems are
those based on a
timer
variable, which
measures the elapsed time since the last
jump
[
47
,
42
,
37
]
.
For stability analysis, conditions based on minimum dwell-time (MDT), were studied by Geromel
and Colaneri
[
33
,
34
]
in the context of switched systems and were later considered for switched
[
64
]
and impulsive systems
[
17
,
14
]
where maximum dwell-time, range dwell-time (RDT), and constant dwell-time were also considered in the context of looped-functionals and timer-dependent Lyapunov functions. Analogous concepts include the average dwell-time and the reverse average dwell-time
[
41
]
. Some of those results were later extended to matrix-valued impulsive and hybrid systems in
[
16
]
.
Timer-dependent Lyapunov conditions for dwell-time analysis of
deterministic and stochastic impulsive and sampled-data systems have
been developed in
[
14
,
15
]
.
These conditions are particularly valuable because they are verifiable
on bounded time intervals even though the dwell-time sequences may be
infinite; the Geromel–Colaneri stability condition at the end of
the minimum dwell period is the key technique that converts an
infinite-horizon verification to a finite one. To date, analogous conditions in the context of optimal control, dissipativity, and games have not been developed in the matrix-valued impulsive setting.

The present paper fills these gaps.
We address the linear-cost optimal control problem, optimal steering,
dissipativity analysis, and minimax optimal control for the class of
linear Hermitian impulsive matrix-valued systems introduced
in
[
16
]
, covering both finite- and infinite-horizon
problems, and developing dwell-time extensions for all three problems.
The paper is built around a single
key identity
that holds for
any admissible trajectory and any dual variable, together with two Schur
complement lemmas; these three tools apply identically to the flow integral
and to each jump summand, giving a structurally uniform proof method
throughout.

A central feature is that all infinite-horizon results are derived for

linear time-varying
systems and cost operators under suitable
stabilizability and detectability conditions, without assuming
time-invariance or periodicity, in contrast to the treatments
in
[
46
,
27
,
25
,
1
,
11
]
.
Specifically, a monotone convergence argument shows that the
horizon-
T
optimal cost is non-decreasing in
T
and converges to
the infinite-horizon value; the time-varying case requires this
functional-analytic approach in place of the algebraic Riccati
equations that characterize the LTI case. Examples are considered in the related sections.

The paper is structured as follows.
Notation, Schur complement lemmas, and the system class are collected
in Section
2
.
Linear-cost optimal control, including dwell-time extensions, is covered
in Section
3
.
Finite-time optimal steering, the two-point boundary value problem,
and the forward-backward algorithm are in Section
4
.
Dissipativity analysis is in Section
5
, and minimax control
in Section
6
.

2
Preliminaries

2.1
Notation

Throughout,
n,m\geq 1
are fixed integers. The set of Hermitian matrices, positive semidefinite Hermitian, and positive definite Hermitian matrices of dimension
n
are denoted by
\mathbb{H}^{n}
,
\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
, and
\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succ 0}
. We denote the conjugate transpose of a matrix
A
by
A^{*}
, and we write
\mathrm{Her}[A]:=A+A^{*}
. With a slight abuse of notation, the dual of an operator
\mathcal{A}
by
\mathcal{A}^{*}
. For a matrix

X=\begin{bmatrix}X_{1}&X_{2}^{*}\\
X_{2}&X_{3}\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{H}^{n+m}_{\succeq 0},

we set
X_{1}:=EXE^{*}\in\mathbb{H}^{n}
,

X_{2}:=E_{\bot}XE^{*}\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times n}
, and

X_{3}:=E_{\bot}XE_{\bot}^{*}\in\mathbb{H}^{m}_{\succeq 0}
, where
E:=\bigl[\begin{smallmatrix}I_{n}&0\end{smallmatrix}\bigr]\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times(n+m)}
and

E_{\bot}:=\bigl[\begin{smallmatrix}0&I_{m}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr]\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times(n+m)}
. For a function
f
defined on
\mathbb{R}
, the left limit at
t
is defined as
f(t^{-}):\lim_{s\uparrow t}f(s)
while the right limit at
t
is denoted by
f(t^{+}):\lim_{s\downarrow t}f(s)
.

2.2
Inner product and nuclear norm

Definition 2.1

(
[
16
, Def. 2.2]
)
.

The
Frobenius inner product
on
\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
is

\langle A,\,B\rangle:=\mathrm{trace}(AB^{*}),\qquad A,B\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}.

(1)

For
A\in\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
the nuclear norm equals the trace:

\|A\|_{*}:=\mathrm{trace}(A)
.

Proposition 2.2

(
[
16
, Prop. 2.3]
)
.

Let
P\in\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succ 0}
,
Q\in\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
,
R\in\mathbb{H}^{n}
. Then:

(a)

\langle P,\,Q\rangle=0
if and only if
Q=0
.

(b)

\langle R,\,Q\rangle\leq 0
for all
Q\succeq 0
,
Q\neq 0
, if and only if

R\preceq 0
.

(c)

\lambda_{\min}(P)\|Q\|_{*}\leq\langle P,\,Q\rangle\leq\lambda_{\max}(P)\|Q\|_{*}
for all

Q\succeq 0
.

2.3
Schur complements

Definition 2.3

(Generalized Schur complement)
.

For
\mathcal{L}=\bigl[\begin{smallmatrix}\mathcal{L}_{1}&\mathcal{L}_{2}\\
\mathcal{L}_{2}^{*}&\mathcal{L}_{3}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr]\in\mathbb{H}^{n+m}
,
the
generalized Schur complement
of
\mathcal{L}_{3}
in

\mathcal{L}
is

\mathcal{L}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{L}_{3}:=\mathcal{L}_{1}-\mathcal{L}_{2}{\mathcal{L}_{3}}^{\dagger}\mathcal{L}_{2}^{*}.

(2)

When
\mathcal{L}_{3}
is invertible this coincides with the ordinary
Schur complement. The same notation is used for any partitioned
Hermitian matrix
\mathcal{L}
, regardless of context.

The following two lemmas are used in every proof of this paper.

Lemma 2.4

(Extended Schur lemma,

[
12
]
)
.

Let
M=M^{*}
,
N
,
R=R^{*}
have compatible dimensions.
The following are equivalent:

(i)

M-N{R}^{\dagger}N^{*}\succeq 0
,
R\succeq 0
, and
N(I-{R}^{\dagger}R)=0
.

(ii)

\begin{bmatrix}M&N\\
N^{*}&R\end{bmatrix}\succeq 0
.

Lemma 2.5

(Schur inner-product decomposition)
.

Let
\mathcal{L}\in\mathbb{H}^{n+m}
with
\mathcal{L}_{3}\succeq 0
and

X\in\mathbb{H}^{n+m}_{\succeq 0}
. Write
X=UU^{*}
with

U=[U_{1}^{*}\;U_{2}^{*}]^{*}\in\mathbb{C}^{(n+m)\times r}
,

r=\mathrm{rank}(X)
. Suppose the
compatibility condition

\mathcal{L}_{2}(I-{\mathcal{L}_{3}}^{\dagger}\mathcal{L}_{3})=0\quad\bigl(\text{equivalently,\;}\operatorname{Im}(\mathcal{L}_{2}^{*})\subseteq\operatorname{Im}(\mathcal{L}_{3})\bigr)

(3)

holds. Set
W:={\mathcal{L}_{3}}^{\dagger}\mathcal{L}_{2}^{*}U_{1}+U_{2}
.
Then

\langle\mathcal{L},\,X\rangle=\langle\mathcal{L}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{L}_{3},\,U_{1}U_{1}^{*}\rangle+\langle\mathcal{L}_{3},\,WW^{*}\rangle.

(4)

In particular, whenever
\mathcal{L}\succeq 0
,
condition (
3
) holds automatically
by Lemma
2.4
.

2.4
Dynamical systems

Definition 2.6

(Impulsive system,

[
16
, Sec. 5.1]
)
.

The
impulsive
matrix-valued system combines a
continuous-time flow with instantaneous jumps at times

t_{1}<t_{2}<\cdots
,
t_{k}\to\infty
:

\displaystyle E\dot{X}(t)E^{*}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}(t,X(t)),\quad t\neq t_{k},\quad t\geq t_{0},

(5a)

\displaystyle EX(t_{k}^{+})E^{*}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{J}(k,X(t_{k}^{-})),\quad k\geq 1,

(5b)

with
EX(t_{0})E^{*}=X_{1}^{0}\in\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
.
The
flow operator

\mathcal{F}:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{H}^{n+m}_{\succeq 0}\to\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathbb{H}^{n}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hskip 0.0pt

is linear, bounded, continuous in
t
, leaves
\mathbb{H}^{n+m}_{\succeq 0}

invariant
[
16
]
, and has adjoint

\mathcal{F}^{*}(t,\cdot):\mathbb{H}^{n}\to\mathbb{H}^{n+m}
satisfying

\langle P,\,\mathcal{F}(t,X)\rangle=\langle\mathcal{F}^{*}(t,P(t)),\,X\rangle
.
The
jump operator

\mathcal{J}(k,\cdot):\mathbb{H}^{n+m}_{\succeq 0}\to\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathbb{H}^{n}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hskip 0.0pt

is linear, bounded, leaves
\mathbb{H}^{n+m}_{\succeq 0}
invariant, and has adjoint

\langle P,\,\mathcal{J}(k,X)\rangle=\langle\mathcal{J}^{*}(k,P(t)),\,X\rangle
.

Definition 2.7

(Continuous-time system,

[
16
, Sec. 3.1]
)
.

The
continuous-time
(CT) system is the impulsive
system (
5
) with no jump times (
N_{T}=0
):

E\dot{X}(t)E^{*}=\mathcal{F}(t,X(t)),\quad t\geq t_{0},\qquad EX(t_{0})E^{*}=X_{1}^{0}.

(6)

Definition 2.8

(Discrete-time system,

[
16
, Sec. 4.1]
)
.

The
discrete-time
(DT) system is the impulsive
system (
5
) with
\mathcal{F}(t,X)=0
on each
interval
(t_{k-1},t_{k})
, and jump times
t_{k}=k_{0}+k
:

EX(k+1)E^{*}=\mathcal{J}(k,X(k)),\quad k\geq k_{0},\qquad EX(k_{0})E^{*}=X_{1}^{0}.

(7)

3
Linear-Cost Optimal Control

This section develops the linear-cost optimal control theory for the
impulsive matrix-valued system (
5
). The term

linear-cost
refers to the fact that the cost functional is
linear in the trajectory
X(\cdot)\in\mathbb{H}^{n+m}_{\succeq 0}
; the classical
linear-quadratic (LQ) terminology is recovered when
X
represents
the second-order moment matrix of an underlying stochastic process,
but no such interpretation is required here.

The central analytical tool is a single algebraic

key identity
(Proposition
3.1
) that holds for any
admissible trajectory and any piecewise-
C^{1}
dual variable. All
sufficiency, necessity, and duality results follow by one application
of this identity together with the Schur complement
Lemmas
2.4
–
2.5
.
The finite-horizon theory is in Section
3.1
, the
infinite-horizon theory under LTV conditions in
Section
3.2
, and causal dwell-time policies in
Section
3.2.5
. Continuous-time and discrete-time
corollaries appear throughout.

3.1
Finite-horizon

We study the optimal control problem on the bounded horizon

[t_{0},t_{0}+T]
for a fixed
T>0
. The decision variable is the full
trajectory
X(\cdot)\in\mathbb{H}^{n+m}_{\succeq 0}
, which accounts simultaneously for
the constrained state block
X_{1}
and the free input blocks

X_{2}
,
X_{3}
. We define the cost, the composite operators, and the
key identity in Section
3.1.1
, the I-GRE and optimal
sets in Section
3.1.2
, and the sufficient, necessary,
and dual conditions in
Theorems
3.5
–
3.8
.

3.1.1
Cost function, decision variable, and operators

Let
\{t_{k}\}_{k=1}^{N_{T}}
be the jump times in
(t_{0},t_{0}+T)
, with
the convention
t_{0}^{+}:=t_{0}
and
t_{N_{T}+1}^{-}:=t_{0}+T
.
For cost weight matrices
Z:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{H}^{n+m}
with

Z(t)\succeq 0
,
Z_{k}\in\mathbb{H}^{n+m}_{\succeq 0}
, and terminal weight
Z_{T}\in\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
,
the finite-horizon cost is

J_{T}(X,X_{1}^{0}):=\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\langle Z(t),\,X(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\sum_{k=1}^{N_{T}}\langle Z_{k},\,X(t_{k}^{-})\rangle+\langle Z_{T},\,EX(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\rangle.

(8)

The three terms represent, respectively, the accumulated flow cost, the
total jump cost, and the terminal penalty on the constrained block.
The cost is
linear
in
X(\cdot)\in\mathbb{H}^{n+m}_{\succeq 0}
: when
X
is the
second-moment matrix of a stochastic process with state
x(t)
and
input
u(t)
, the inner product
\langle Z(t),\,X(t)\rangle=\mathrm{tr}(Z(t)X(t))

equals the expected value
\mathbb{E}[{[x^{*}\;u^{*}]}\,Z(t)\,[x^{*}\;u^{*}]^{*}]
,
recovering the LQ expected cost. The lower-right blocks

Z_{3}(t)\succeq 0
and
Z_{k,3}\succeq 0
, which correspond to the
input-channel cost, are allowed to be
singular
, encompassing
free-input problems and singular optimal control.

We consider now the following optimization problem

\underset{X(\cdot)\in\mathbb{H}^{n+m}_{\succeq 0}}{\mathrm{minimize}}\quad J_{T}(X,X_{1}^{0})\quad\text{subject to \eqref{eq:sys:IMP} and }EX(t_{0})E^{*}=X_{1}^{0}

(9)

with optimal cost

J_{T}^{\star}(X_{1}^{0}):=\underset{X(\cdot)\in\mathbb{H}^{n+m}_{\succeq 0}}{\inf}J_{T}(X,X_{1}^{0}),

where the
decision variable
is the full trajectory

X(\cdot)\in\mathbb{H}^{n+m}_{\succeq 0}
subject to (
5
) and

EX(t_{0})E^{*}=X_{1}^{0}
. Observe that in this abstract setting,
X_{1}(\cdot)=EX(\cdot)E^{*}
is the
constrained
block governed by the flow (
5a
) and the jumps (
5b
) dynamics, while the blocks

X_{2}(\cdot)=E_{\bot}X(\cdot)E^{*}
and
X_{3}(\cdot)=E_{\bot}X(\cdot)E_{\bot}^{*}
are the
free
blocks. The constraint
X(\cdot)\in\mathbb{H}^{n+m}_{\succeq 0}
ensures that
X(\cdot)
remains a
valid positive semidefinite matrix throughout.

The composite operators below are the fundamental quantities in all
subsequent results. They absorb the cost weights, the dynamics, and
the dual variable
P
into a single symmetric matrix whose
semidefiniteness is the key optimality condition.
For a piecewise-
C^{1}
function
P:[t_{0},t_{0}+T]\to\mathbb{H}^{n}
, define
the
composite flow operator
1
1

1

In proof steps, we write
\mathcal{C}_{Z}(t,P)
as shorthand for

\mathcal{C}_{Z}(t,P(t))
, and
X
for
X(t)
, when the time point is fixed;
in theorem statements and definitions the argument
P(t)
is always explicit.

\mathcal{C}_{Z}(t,P(t)):=E^{*}\dot{P}(t)E+\mathcal{F}^{*}(t,P(t))+Z(t)\in\mathbb{H}^{n+m},

(10)

partitioned as

\bigl[\begin{smallmatrix}(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{1}&(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{2}\\
(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{2}^{*}&(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr]
,
and the
jump operator
at
t_{k}

\mathcal{D}_{Z}\bigl(k,P(t_{k}^{+}),P(t_{k}^{-})\bigr):=\mathcal{J}^{*}\bigl(k,P(t_{k}^{+})\bigr)-E^{*}P(t_{k}^{-})E+Z_{k}\in\mathbb{H}^{n+m},

(11)

partitioned as
\bigl[\begin{smallmatrix}(\mathcal{D}_{Z})_{1}&(\mathcal{D}_{Z})_{2}\\
(\mathcal{D}_{Z})_{2}^{*}&(\mathcal{D}_{Z})_{3}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr]
.

Intuitively,
\mathcal{C}_{Z}(t,P(t))
captures the time derivative of

t\mapsto\langle P(t),\,EX(t)E^{*}\rangle
augmented by the running cost
Z(t)
,
while
\mathcal{D}_{Z}(k,P(t_{k}^{+}),P(t_{k}^{-}))
captures the jump in

\langle P,\,EXE^{*}\rangle
augmented by the jump cost
Z_{k}
.
Both are symmetric
(n+m)\times(n+m)
matrices, partitioned into

(n\times n)
,
(m\times n)
, and
(m\times m)
blocks corresponding
to the state, cross-term, and input channels.

The following identity is the single most important tool of the
paper. It holds for
any
piecewise-
C^{1}
function
P
and

any
admissible trajectory, regardless of whether
P
solves
the I-GRE. By choosing
P
to be the I-GRE solution, all subsequent
optimality conditions reduce to signed comparisons of its terms.

Proposition 3.1

(Impulsive key identity)
.

For any piecewise-
C^{1}

P:[t_{0},t_{0}+T]\to\mathbb{H}^{n}
and any
trajectory
X(\cdot)\in\mathbb{H}^{n+m}_{\succeq 0}
satisfying (
5
)
with
EX(t_{0})E^{*}=X_{1}^{0}
,

\displaystyle J_{T}(X,X_{1}^{0})

\displaystyle=\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle+\langle Z_{T}-P(t_{0}+T),\,EX(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\rangle

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\langle\mathcal{C}_{Z}(t,P(t)),\,X(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\sum_{k=1}^{N_{T}}\langle\mathcal{D}_{Z}(k,P(t_{k}^{+}),P(t_{k}^{-})),\,X(t_{k}^{-})\rangle.

(12)

When
P(t_{0}+T)=Z_{T}
the second term vanishes:

J_{T}(X,X_{1}^{0})=\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle+\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\langle\mathcal{C}_{Z}(t,P(t)),\,X(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\sum_{k=1}^{N_{T}}\langle\mathcal{D}_{Z}(k,P(t_{k}^{+}),P(t_{k}^{-})),\,X(t_{k}^{-})\rangle.

(13)

Proof.

On the flow interval
(t_{k-1},t_{k})
, differentiating

\langle P(t),\,EX(t)E^{*}\rangle
along (
5a
) and
integrating yields

\displaystyle\langle P(t_{k}^{-}),\,EX(t_{k}^{-})E^{*}\rangle-\langle P(t_{k-1}^{+}),\,EX(t_{k-1}^{+})E^{*}\rangle

\displaystyle\;=-\int_{t_{k-1}}^{t_{k}}\langle Z(t),\,X(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{t_{k-1}}^{t_{k}}\langle\mathcal{C}_{Z}(t,P(t)),\,X(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t.

(14)

At each jump
t_{k}
, using (
5b
) and the adjoint gives

\displaystyle\langle P(t_{k}^{+}),\,EX(t_{k}^{+})E^{*}\rangle-\langle P(t_{k}^{-}),\,EX(t_{k}^{-})E^{*}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle P(t_{k}^{+}),\,\mathcal{J}(k,X(t_{k}^{-}))\rangle-\langle P(t_{k}^{-}),\,EX(t_{k}^{-})E^{*}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\mathcal{J}^{*}(k,P(t_{k}^{+}))-E^{*}P(t_{k}^{-})E,\,X(t_{k}^{-})\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\mathcal{D}_{Z}(k,P(t_{k}^{+}),P(t_{k}^{-})),\,X(t_{k}^{-})\rangle-\langle Z_{k},\,X(t_{k}^{-})\rangle.

(15)

Summing (
3.1.1
) over all
N_{T}+1
flow intervals and
(
15
) over all
N_{T}
jumps, the terms telescope to
give
\langle P(t_{0}+T),\,EX(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\rangle-\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
on the
left. Rearranging yields (
12
).
∎

3.1.2
Impulsive GRE and optimal sets

With the composite operators in hand, we now define the central object
of the theory: the
impulsive generalized Riccati equation

(I-GRE). This is a coupled flow-jump terminal-value problem for
the dual variable
P(\cdot)
with
P(t_{0}+T)=Z_{T}
. The I-GRE
imposes that
\mathcal{C}_{Z}(t,P(t))\succeq 0
with vanishing generalized
Schur complement on each flow interval, and the analogous
condition on
\mathcal{D}_{Z}
at each jump. By
Lemma
3.4
, these are equivalent to a single
condition per interval:
\mathcal{C}_{Z}\succeq 0
and

\mathcal{C}_{Z}\,\widetilde{/}\,\allowbreak(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}=0
.
The I-GRE generalizes the classical differential Riccati equation
to the impulsive setting and reduces to it in the regular case

(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}\succ 0
.

Definition 3.2

(Impulsive GRE)
.

The
impulsive generalized Riccati equation
(I-GRE) for
piecewise-
C^{1}

P:[t_{0},t_{0}+T]\to\mathbb{H}^{n}
with
P(t_{0}+T)=Z_{T}

consists of:

•

Flow conditions, for a.e.
t
between jumps:

\displaystyle(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{1}(t,P(t))-(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{2}(t,P(t))\,{(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}(t,P(t))}^{\dagger}\,(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{2}(t,P(t))^{*}=0,

(16a)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}(t,P(t))\,{(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}(t,P(t))}^{\dagger}\,(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{2}(t,P(t))^{*}=(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{2}(t,P(t))^{*},

(16b)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}(t,P(t))\succeq 0.

(16c)

•

Jump conditions, at each
t_{k}

(
k=1,\ldots,N_{T}
):

Writing
P_{k}^{+}:=P(t_{k}^{+})
and
P_{k}^{-}:=P(t_{k}^{-})
:

\displaystyle(\mathcal{D}_{Z})_{1}(k,P_{k}^{+},P_{k}^{-})-(\mathcal{D}_{Z})_{2}(k,P_{k}^{+},P_{k}^{-})\,{(\mathcal{D}_{Z})_{3}(k,P_{k}^{+},P_{k}^{-})}^{\dagger}\,(\mathcal{D}_{Z})_{2}(k,P_{k}^{+},P_{k}^{-})^{*}=0,

(17a)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{D}_{Z})_{3}(k,P_{k}^{+},P_{k}^{-})\,{(\mathcal{D}_{Z})_{3}(k,P_{k}^{+},P_{k}^{-})}^{\dagger}\,(\mathcal{D}_{Z})_{2}(k,P_{k}^{+},P_{k}^{-})^{*}=(\mathcal{D}_{Z})_{2}(k,P_{k}^{+},P_{k}^{-})^{*},

(17b)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{D}_{Z})_{3}(k,P_{k}^{+},P_{k}^{-})\succeq 0.

(17c)

By Lemma
3.4
, each triple is equivalent to
the respective operator being
\succeq 0
with vanishing
generalized Schur complement.
The
flow optimal set
and
jump optimal set
are

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{c}(t,P(t))

\displaystyle:=\{K\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times n}\mid(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}(t,P(t))K^{*}+(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{2}(t,P(t))^{*}=0\},

(18)

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{d}(k,P_{k}^{+},P_{k}^{-})

\displaystyle:=\{K\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times n}\mid(\mathcal{D}_{Z})_{3}(k,P_{k}^{+},P_{k}^{-})\,K^{*}{}+(\mathcal{D}_{Z})_{2}(k,P_{k}^{+},P_{k}^{-})^{*}=0\},

(19)

whose general element is

K={(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}(t,P(t))}^{\dagger}(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{2}(t,P(t))^{*}+F\bigl(I-(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}(t,P(t)){(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}(t,P(t))}^{\dagger}\bigr)

and

K={(\mathcal{D}_{Z})_{3}(k,P_{k}^{+},P_{k}^{-})}^{\dagger}(\mathcal{D}_{Z})_{2}(k,P_{k}^{+},P_{k}^{-})^{*}+F\bigl(I-(\mathcal{D}_{Z})_{3}(k,P_{k}^{+},P_{k}^{-}){(\mathcal{D}_{Z})_{3}(k,P_{k}^{+},P_{k}^{-})}^{\dagger}\bigr)

for
\mathcal{K}_{c}
and
\mathcal{K}_{d}
, respectively, and where
F\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times n}

[
52
]
.

Remark 3.3
.

When
(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}\succ 0
and
(\mathcal{D}_{Z})_{3}\succ 0

(in particular when
Z_{3}\succ 0
and
Z_{k,3}\succ 0
), the
pseudoinverses become ordinary inverses and the compatibility
conditions (
16b
), (
17b
) hold
trivially. The I-GRE then reduces to classical Riccati equations.

The result below plays an essential role in the derivation of the main results in this section and is essentially a limiting case of the Schur complement lemma (Lemma
2.4
):

Lemma 3.4

(Schur equality lemma)
.

Let
\mathcal{L}=\bigl[\begin{smallmatrix}\mathcal{L}_{1}&\mathcal{L}_{2}\\
\mathcal{L}_{2}^{*}&\mathcal{L}_{3}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr]\in\mathbb{H}^{n+m}
.
The following are equivalent:

(i)

\mathcal{L}\succeq 0
and

\mathcal{L}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{L}_{3}=0
.

(ii)

\mathcal{L}_{1}-\mathcal{L}_{2}{\mathcal{L}_{3}}^{\dagger}\mathcal{L}_{2}^{*}=0
,
\mathcal{L}_{3}{\mathcal{L}_{3}}^{\dagger}\mathcal{L}_{2}^{*}=\mathcal{L}_{2}^{*}
, and
\mathcal{L}_{3}\succeq 0
.

Proof.

(
i
)
\Rightarrow
(
ii
).
Since
\mathcal{L}\succeq 0
, Lemma
2.4
gives

\mathcal{L}_{3}\succeq 0
,

\mathcal{L}_{1}-\mathcal{L}_{2}{\mathcal{L}_{3}}^{\dagger}\mathcal{L}_{2}^{*}\succeq 0
,
and the compatibility

\mathcal{L}_{2}(I-{\mathcal{L}_{3}}^{\dagger}\mathcal{L}_{3})=0
.
Combined with
\mathcal{L}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{L}_{3}=0
, we get

\mathcal{L}_{1}-\mathcal{L}_{2}{\mathcal{L}_{3}}^{\dagger}\mathcal{L}_{2}^{*}=0
.
The compatibility condition reads

\mathcal{L}_{2}=\mathcal{L}_{2}{\mathcal{L}_{3}}^{\dagger}\mathcal{L}_{3}
;
taking conjugate transposes gives

\mathcal{L}_{2}^{*}=\mathcal{L}_{3}{\mathcal{L}_{3}}^{\dagger}\mathcal{L}_{2}^{*}
.

(
ii
)
\Rightarrow
(
i
).
From the first condition and Definition
2.3
,

\mathcal{L}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{L}_{3}=0
.
The second condition is the compatibility

\operatorname{Im}(\mathcal{L}_{2}^{*})\subseteq\operatorname{Im}(\mathcal{L}_{3})
,
so Lemma
2.4
applies with

M=\mathcal{L}_{1}=\mathcal{L}_{2}{\mathcal{L}_{3}}^{\dagger}\mathcal{L}_{2}^{*}
.
Since
\mathcal{L}_{3}\succeq 0
we have
{\mathcal{L}_{3}}^{\dagger}\succeq 0
,
so
\mathcal{L}_{1}=\bigl(({\mathcal{L}_{3}}^{\dagger})^{1/2}\mathcal{L}_{2}^{*}\bigr)^{*}\bigl(({\mathcal{L}_{3}}^{\dagger})^{1/2}\mathcal{L}_{2}^{*}\bigr)\succeq 0
,
giving
\mathcal{L}\succeq 0
.
∎

3.1.3
Sufficient condition

Theorem
3.5
below shows that any piecewise-
C^{1}

solution
P\succeq 0
of the I-GRE is the unique solution, and that

\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
is both the optimal cost and the value attained
by any trajectory with gains in the optimal sets
\mathcal{K}_{c}

and
\mathcal{K}_{d}
in (
18
) and (
19
). The proof uses the key identity in
Proposition
3.1
to decompose the cost into a fixed
initial term
\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
and non-negative remainder terms
that vanish exactly on the optimal trajectory.

Theorem 3.5

(Sufficiency of the I-GRE)
.

Let
T>0
. Suppose there exists a piecewise-
C^{1}
solution

P:[t_{0},t_{0}+T]\to\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
with
P(t_{0}+T)=Z_{T}
satisfying the
I-GRE (
16
)–(
17
). Then:

(a)

The optimal cost is
J_{T}^{\star}(X_{1}^{0})=\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
.

(b)

The optimum is attained at any
X^{\star}
with

K(t)\in\mathcal{K}_{c}(t,P(t))
on flow intervals and

K_{k}\in\mathcal{K}_{d}(k,P(t_{k}^{+}),P(t_{k}^{-}))
at each jump.

(c)

The solution
P
is unique.

Proof.

Lower bound.

By the I-GRE,
\mathcal{C}_{Z}(t,P(t))\succeq 0
with

\mathcal{C}_{Z}\,\widetilde{/}\,\allowbreak(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}=0
on flow, and

\mathcal{D}_{Z}(k,P(t_{k}^{+}),P(t_{k}^{-}))\succeq 0
with

\mathcal{J}\,\widetilde{/}\,(\mathcal{D}_{Z})_{3}=0

at each jump (Lemma
2.4
).
Let
X
be any trajectory of (
5
) with

EX(t_{0})E^{*}=X_{1}^{0}
.
The key identity (
13
) gives

J_{T}(X,X_{1}^{0})=\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle+\underbrace{\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\langle\mathcal{C}_{Z}(t,P(t)),\,X(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t}_{=:\,I_{f}}+\underbrace{\sum_{k=1}^{N_{T}}\langle\mathcal{D}_{Z}(k,P(t_{k}^{+}),P(t_{k}^{-})),\,X(t_{k}^{-})\rangle}_{=:\,I_{j}}.

(20)

Write
X(t)=U(t)U(t)^{*}
with
U=[U_{1}^{*}\;U_{2}^{*}]^{*}
.
Since
\mathcal{C}_{Z}(t,P(t))\succeq 0
, compatibility (
3
)
holds (Lemma
2.4
), and
Lemma
2.5
gives, for a.e.
t
,

\langle\mathcal{C}_{Z}(t,P(t)),\,X(t)\rangle=\underbrace{\langle\mathcal{C}_{Z}\,\widetilde{/}\,(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3},\,U_{1}U_{1}^{*}\rangle}_{=\,0}+\underbrace{\langle(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3},\,W_{f}W_{f}^{*}\rangle}_{\geq\,0},\qquad W_{f}:={(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}}^{\dagger}(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{2}^{*}U_{1}+U_{2}.

(21)

Writing
X(t_{k}^{-})=V_{k}V_{k}^{*}
with
V_{k}=[V_{k,1}^{*}\;V_{k,2}^{*}]^{*}

and applying Lemma
2.5
to
\mathcal{J}

at each
t_{k}
:

\langle\mathcal{D}_{Z}(k,P(t_{k}^{+}),P(t_{k}^{-})),\,X(t_{k}^{-})\rangle=\underbrace{\langle\mathcal{D}_{Z}\,\widetilde{/}\,(\mathcal{D}_{Z})_{3},\,V_{k,1}V_{k,1}^{*}\rangle}_{=\,0}+\underbrace{\langle(\mathcal{D}_{Z})_{3},\,W_{k}W_{k}^{*}\rangle}_{\geq\,0},

(22)

Therefore
I_{f}\geq 0
and
I_{j}\geq 0
, so

J_{T}(X,X_{1}^{0})\geq\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle\quad\text{for every admissible }X.

(23)

Attainment (flow).
Fix
K(t)\in\mathcal{K}_{c}(t,P(t))
. The general-element formula
gives
K={(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}}^{\dagger}(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{2}^{*}+F(I-(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}{(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}}^{\dagger})
, so

K-{(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}}^{\dagger}(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{2}^{*}=F(I-(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}{(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}}^{\dagger})
,
whose columns lie in
\ker((\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3})
.
Setting
U_{2}^{\star}=-KU_{1}^{\star}
:

W_{f}^{\star}={(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}}^{\dagger}(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{2}^{*}U_{1}^{\star}+U_{2}^{\star}=({(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}}^{\dagger}(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{2}^{*}-K)U_{1}^{\star}=-F(I-(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}{(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}}^{\dagger})U_{1}^{\star}.

Every column of
(I-(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}{(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}}^{\dagger})\allowbreak U_{1}^{\star}

lies in
\ker((\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3})
, so
(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}W_{f}^{\star}=0
,
giving
\langle(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3},\,W_{f}^{\star}W_{f}^{\star*}\rangle=\mathrm{trace}((\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}W_{f}^{\star}W_{f}^{\star*})=0
.
Combined with
\mathcal{C}_{Z}\,\widetilde{/}\,\allowbreak(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}=0
,
both terms in (
21
) vanish along
X^{\star}
:

\langle\mathcal{C}_{Z}(t,P(t)),\,X^{\star}(t)\rangle=0\quad\text{for all }t.

(24)

Attainment (jump).
The identical argument with
K_{k}\in\mathcal{K}_{d}
and

V_{k,2}^{\star}=-K_{k}V_{k,1}^{\star}
gives
W_{k}^{\star}\in\ker((\mathcal{D}_{Z})_{3})
, hence

\langle\mathcal{D}_{Z}(k,P(t_{k}^{+}),P(t_{k}^{-})),\,X^{\star}(t_{k}^{-})\rangle=0\quad\text{for every }k.

(25)

Substituting (
24
)–(
25
)
into (
20
):
J_{T}(X^{\star},X_{1}^{0})=\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
.
Combined with (
23
):

J_{T}^{\star}(X_{1}^{0})=\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
, attained at
X^{\star}
.

Positivity of
P
.

J_{T}^{\star}(X_{1}^{0})=\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle\geq 0
for all
X_{1}^{0}\succeq 0

(since
Z\succeq 0
,
Z_{k}\succeq 0
, and
X\succeq 0
).
Proposition
2.2
(
b
) gives
P(t_{0})\succeq 0
,
and since
t_{0}\in[t_{0},t_{0}+T]
is arbitrary,
P(t)\succeq 0
.

Uniqueness of
P
.

Suppose
\tilde{P}
is another piecewise-
C^{1}
solution of the
I-GRE with
\tilde{P}(t_{0}+T)=Z_{T}
. By the argument above,

J_{T}^{\star}(X_{1}^{0})=\langle\tilde{P}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
for all
X_{1}^{0}\succeq 0
.
Since also
J_{T}^{\star}(X_{1}^{0})=\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
:

\langle P(t_{0})-\tilde{P}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle=0
for all
X_{1}^{0}\succeq 0
.
Taking
X_{1}^{0}=vv^{*}
for every
v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
gives

(P(t_{0})-\tilde{P}(t_{0}))v=0
, hence
P(t_{0})=\tilde{P}(t_{0})
.
Since
t_{0}\in[t_{0},t_{0}+T]
is arbitrary,
P\equiv\tilde{P}
.
∎

3.1.4
Necessary condition

Conversely, whenever the optimization problem is well-posed
(the infimum is finite for every
X_{1}^{0}\succeq 0
and is attained),
the value function is necessarily linear in
X_{1}^{0}
and the
corresponding dual variable must satisfy the I-GRE.
Theorem
3.6
establishes this necessity via a
dynamic programming argument: the positivity constraint on

\mathcal{C}_{Z}
comes from the value-function inequality on flow
intervals, and the Riccati equation
\mathcal{C}_{Z}\,\widetilde{/}\,(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}=0
comes from the vanishing of the key identity
along optimal trajectories.

Theorem 3.6

(Necessity of the I-GRE)
.

Let
T>0
. Suppose (
9
) is well-posed and an
optimal trajectory exists for every
X_{1}^{0}\succeq 0
. Then:

(a)

The value function is linear: there exists a unique
piecewise-
C^{1}

P
with
P(t_{0}+T)=Z_{T}
such that

J_{T}^{\star}(X_{1}^{0})=\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
for all

X_{1}^{0}\succeq 0
.

(b)

P
satisfies the flow LMI:

\mathcal{C}_{Z}(t,P(t))\succeq 0
for a.e.
t
.

(c)

P
satisfies the jump LMI:

\mathcal{D}_{Z}(k,P(t_{k}^{+}),P(t_{k}^{-}))\succeq 0
for each
k
.

(d)

P
satisfies the full I-GRE, including both compatibility
conditions (
16b
) and (
17b
).

(e)

P(t)\succeq 0
for all
t
.

Proof.

Part (
a
): Linearity.
By well-posedness, for every
X_{1}^{0}\succeq 0
the optimum is attained;
coercivity (H2) makes the cost strictly convex in the trajectory, so
the optimal trajectory
X^{\star}(\cdot;X_{1}^{0})
is
unique
.

Positive homogeneity.

For
\lambda\geq 0
, linearity of the dynamics in
X
implies that

\lambda X^{\star}(\cdot;X_{1}^{0})
is admissible for
\lambda X_{1}^{0}
, with
cost
\lambda J_{T}^{*}(X_{1}^{0})
. By uniqueness it is optimal, so

J_{T}^{*}(\lambda X_{1}^{0})=\lambda J_{T}^{*}(X_{1}^{0})
.

Additivity.

For
X_{1}^{0},\tilde{X}_{1}^{0}\succeq 0
, the sum

X^{\star}(\cdot;X_{1}^{0})+X^{\star}(\cdot;\tilde{X}_{1}^{0})
is admissible for

X_{1}^{0}+\tilde{X}_{1}^{0}
(linearity of the dynamics), with cost

J_{T}^{*}(X_{1}^{0})+J_{T}^{*}(\tilde{X}_{1}^{0})
; hence

J_{T}^{*}(X_{1}^{0}+\tilde{X}_{1}^{0})\leq J_{T}^{*}(X_{1}^{0})+J_{T}^{*}(\tilde{X}_{1}^{0}).

Conversely, by dynamic programming
[
7
,
8
]

the optimum is attained by a
state-independent
linear feedback on
the free blocks (the optimal gain
K(t)
depends on the value function,
not on the initial condition). The induced optimal closed-loop is thus a
linear, cone-preserving flow
x\mapsto X^{\star}(\cdot;x)
, so

X^{\star}(\cdot;X_{1}^{0}+\tilde{X}_{1}^{0})=X^{\star}(\cdot;X_{1}^{0})+X^{\star}(\cdot;\tilde{X}_{1}^{0})
is the sum of two

admissible
(
\mathbb{H}^{n+m}_{\succeq 0}
-valued) optimal trajectories. Therefore

J_{T}^{*}(X_{1}^{0}+\tilde{X}_{1}^{0})=J_{T}^{*}(X_{1}^{0})+J_{T}^{*}(\tilde{X}_{1}^{0}),

which gives the reverse inequality (and in fact equality directly).

Polarisation.

J_{T}^{*}
is therefore linear on
\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
.
Extending by polarisation
Q=Q_{+}-Q_{-}
for
Q\in\mathbb{H}^{n}
yields a linear
functional on
\mathbb{H}^{n}

[
10
]
.
The Frobenius–Riesz representation
[
23
]
gives unique
P(t_{0})\in\mathbb{H}^{n}
with

J_{T}^{*}(X_{1}^{0})=\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
.
Applying dynamic programming
[
7
,
8
]
at every initial time
t
defines

P(t)
; piecewise-
C^{1}
regularity follows from continuity of the
optimal cost on each flow interval, with possible
discontinuities at jump times. The terminal condition

P(t_{0}+T)=Z_{T}
holds by evaluating at
t_{0}=t_{0}+T
.

Part (
b
): Flow LMI.

Fix a flow instant
t
in the interior of some interval
(t_{k-1},t_{k})
.
For any
[v_{1}^{*}\;v_{2}^{*}]^{*}\in\mathbb{C}^{n+m}
, consider a
constant perturbation
X_{2}\equiv v_{2}v_{1}^{*}
,

X_{3}\equiv v_{2}v_{2}^{*}
on
[t,t+h]\subset(t_{k-1},t_{k})

and continue optimally on
[t+h,t_{0}+T]
. The dynamic programming
inequality

\langle P(t),\,EX(t)E^{*}\rangle\leq\int_{t}^{t+h}\langle Z(t),\,X(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\langle P(t+h),\,EX(t+h)E^{*}\rangle
,
after subtracting, dividing by
h>0
, and letting
h\to 0
(using
the definition of
\mathcal{C}_{Z}(t,P(t))
), gives

0\leq\begin{bmatrix}v_{1}\\
v_{2}\end{bmatrix}^{*}\mathcal{C}_{Z}(t,P(t))\begin{bmatrix}v_{1}\\
v_{2}\end{bmatrix}.

Since
v_{1}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
and
v_{2}\in\mathbb{C}^{m}
are arbitrary,

\mathcal{C}_{Z}(t,P(t))\succeq 0
.

Part (
c
): Jump LMI.

At
t_{k}
, the dynamic programming inequality reads

\langle P(t_{k}^{-}),\,EX(t_{k}^{-})E^{*}\rangle\leq\langle Z_{k},\,X(t_{k}^{-})\rangle+\langle P(t_{k}^{+}),\,EX(t_{k}^{+})E^{*}\rangle.

Using (
5b
) and the adjoint relation:

0\leq\langle Z_{k}+\mathcal{J}^{*}(k,P(t_{k}^{+}))-E^{*}P(t_{k}^{-})E,\,X(t_{k}^{-})\rangle=\langle\mathcal{D}_{Z}(k,P(t_{k}^{+}),P(t_{k}^{-})),\,X(t_{k}^{-})\rangle.

Testing with
X(t_{k}^{-})=[v_{1}^{*}\;v_{2}^{*}]^{*}[v_{1}^{*}\;v_{2}^{*}]
for arbitrary
v_{1}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
,
v_{2}\in\mathbb{C}^{m}
gives

\mathcal{D}_{Z}(k,P(t_{k}^{+}),P(t_{k}^{-}))\succeq 0
.

Part (
d
): I-GRE conditions.

Let
X^{\star}
be an optimal trajectory.
From (
13
):

0=J_{T}(X^{\star},X_{1}^{0})-J_{T}^{\star}(X_{1}^{0})=\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\langle\mathcal{C}_{Z},\,X^{\star}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\sum_{k}\langle\mathcal{D}_{Z}(k,P(t_{k}^{+}),P(t_{k}^{-})),\,X^{\star}(t_{k}^{-})\rangle.

(26)

Since the integrand (Part (
b
)) and each summand
(Part (
c
)) are non-negative, each must vanish
separately:

\langle\mathcal{C}_{Z}(t,P(t)),\,X^{\star}(t)\rangle=0,\ t\geq 0\ \text{a.e.},\qquad\langle\mathcal{D}_{Z}(k,P(t_{k}^{+}),P(t_{k}^{-})),\,X^{\star}(t_{k}^{-})\rangle=0,\ k\geq 1.

(27)

We prove now the Flow Riccati equation and compatibility (
16a
),
(
16b
). Since
\mathcal{C}_{Z}(t,P(t))\succeq 0
, Lemma
2.4

gives compatibility (
3
).
Lemma
2.5
with (
27
) gives

0=\langle\mathcal{C}_{Z}\,\widetilde{/}\,(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3},\,U_{1}^{\star}U_{1}^{\star*}\rangle+\langle(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3},\,W_{f}^{\star}W_{f}^{\star*}\rangle,

with both terms non-negative. Hence each is zero.
Since the optimal trajectory
X_{1}^{\star}=EX^{\star}E^{*}
can reach
any
Q\in\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
by varying
X_{1}^{0}
,

\langle\mathcal{C}_{Z}\,\widetilde{/}\,(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3},\,Q\rangle=0
for all

Q\succeq 0
gives
\mathcal{C}_{Z}\,\widetilde{/}\,\allowbreak(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}=0

by Proposition
2.2
(
b
),
i.e., (
16a
) holds.
From
\langle(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3},\,W_{f}^{\star}W_{f}^{\star*}\rangle=0
and

(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}\succeq 0
:
(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}W_{f}^{\star}=0
.
Expanding
W_{f}^{\star}={(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}}^{\dagger}(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{2}^{*}U_{1}^{\star}+U_{2}^{\star}
(with
U_{2}^{\star}=-K^{\star}U_{1}^{\star}
) and
using
(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}K^{\star*}=-(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{2}^{*}
:

0=(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}\bigl({(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}}^{\dagger}(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{2}^{*}-K^{\star*}\bigr)U_{1}^{\star}=(I-(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}{(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}}^{\dagger})(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{2}^{*}U_{1}^{\star}.

Because
U_{1}^{\star}
spans
\mathbb{R}^{n}
over all initial conditions,

(I-(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}{(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}}^{\dagger})(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{2}^{*}=0
,
which is (
16b
).

Let us consider now the Jump Riccati equation and compatibility (
17a
),
(
17b
). The identical argument applied to
\mathcal{D}_{Z}(k,P(t_{k}^{+}),P(t_{k}^{-}))
and

V_{k,1}^{\star}:=EU^{\star}(t_{k}^{-})
, where
X^{\star}(t_{k}^{-})=U^{\star}(t_{k}^{-})U^{\star}(t_{k}^{-})^{*}
,
gives

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\langle\mathcal{D}_{Z}\,\widetilde{/}\,(\mathcal{D}_{Z})_{3},\,V_{k,1}^{\star}V_{k,1}^{\star*}\rangle+\langle(\mathcal{D}_{Z})_{3},\,W_{k}^{\star}W_{k}^{\star*}\rangle,

\displaystyle(\mathcal{D}_{Z})_{3}W_{k}^{\star}

\displaystyle=0,\quad(I-(\mathcal{D}_{Z})_{3}{(\mathcal{D}_{Z})_{3}}^{\dagger})(\mathcal{D}_{Z})_{2}^{*}=0,

which are (
17a
) and (
17b
) respectively.

Part (
e
): Positivity.

Same argument as in Theorem
3.5
.
∎

3.1.5
Equivalence and dual LMI

Combining Theorems
3.5
and
3.6

gives the equivalence Corollary
3.7
: the
existence of a positive solution to the I-GRE is both necessary
and sufficient for well-posedness.
Theorem
3.8
then provides an alternative
semidefinite program characterization of the optimal cost,
whose constraint
\mathcal{C}_{Z}\succeq 0
and
\mathcal{D}_{Z}\succeq 0

is
linear
in
P
(an LMI).
This is useful for numerical computation: rather than solving the
nonlinear I-GRE directly, one can maximize
\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle

over
P\succeq 0
subject to LMI constraints.

Corollary 3.7

(Equivalence)
.

Problem (
9
) is well-posed with an optimal
trajectory for every
X_{1}^{0}\succeq 0
if and only if the I-GRE
has a piecewise-
C^{1}
solution
P\succeq 0
with
P(t_{0}+T)=Z_{T}
.
The solution is unique and
J_{T}^{\star}(X_{1}^{0})=\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
.

Proof.

Sufficiency is Theorem
3.5
; necessity is
Theorem
3.6
.
∎

Theorem 3.8

(Dual LMI: finite-horizon)
.

Suppose the I-GRE has a solution
P^{*}
. Then

\displaystyle J_{T}^{\star}(X_{1}^{0})=\max_{P\text{ pw-}C^{1}}

\displaystyle\;\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle

s.t.

\displaystyle P\succeq 0,\;\mathcal{C}_{Z}(t,P(t))\succeq 0,\;\mathcal{D}_{Z}(k,P(t_{k}^{+}),P(t_{k}^{-}))\succeq 0,\;P(t_{0}+T)\preceq Z_{T},

(28)

and the maximum is attained at
P^{*}
.

Proof.

Step 1: Every feasible
P
is a lower bound.

Let
P
be feasible and
X
any admissible trajectory.
From the key identity (
12
) (before using

P(T)=Z_{T}
):

\displaystyle J_{T}(X,X_{1}^{0})

\displaystyle=\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle+\underbrace{\langle Z_{T}-P(t_{0}+T),\,EX(T)E^{*}\rangle}_{\geq\,0}

\displaystyle\quad+\underbrace{\int\langle\mathcal{C}_{Z}(t,P(t)),\,X(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t}_{\geq\,0}+\underbrace{\textstyle\sum_{k}\langle\mathcal{D}_{Z}(k,P(t_{k}^{+}),P(t_{k}^{-})),\,X(t_{k}^{-})\rangle}_{\geq\,0}

\displaystyle\geq\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle.

The first non-negative term uses
P(T)\preceq Z_{T}
and

EX(T)E^{*}\succeq 0
; the remaining two use the LMI constraints.
Taking the infimum:
J_{T}^{\star}(X_{1}^{0})\geq\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
.

Step 2:
P^{*}
is feasible and attains the bound.

The I-GRE and Lemma
2.4
give

\mathcal{C}_{Z}(t,P^{*})\succeq 0
and

\mathcal{J}\succeq 0
;
P^{*}(T)=Z_{T}
;
P^{*}\succeq 0
.
So
P^{*}
is feasible. By Theorem
3.5
,

\langle P^{*}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle=J_{T}^{\star}(X_{1}^{0})
.
Hence
\max_{P\text{ feas.}}\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle\geq\langle P^{*}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle=J_{T}^{\star}(X_{1}^{0})
.
Combined with Step 1: equality holds and
P^{*}
attains it.
∎

Corollary 3.9

(Continuous-time: finite horizon)
.

Set
N_{T}=0
. The jump conditions are vacuous, the I-GRE reduces
to the
CT-GRE

\mathcal{C}_{Z}(t,P(t))\succeq 0,\ \mathcal{C}_{Z}\,\widetilde{/}\ (\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}=0,\ P(T)=Z_{T},

(29)

and the impulsive key identity reduces to the continuous-time key identity

J_{T}(X,X_{1}^{0})=\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle+\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\langle\mathcal{C}_{Z}(t,P(t)),\,X(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t.

(30)

Theorems
3.5
,
3.6
,
Corollary
3.7
, and
Theorem
3.8
all hold with the dual LMI

J_{T}^{\star}(X_{1}^{0})=\max_{P\succeq 0}\;\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\mathcal{C}_{Z}(t,P(t))\succeq 0,\;P(T)\preceq Z_{T},

(31)

recovering
[
52
,
50
]
.

Corollary 3.10

(Discrete-time: finite horizon)
.

Apply Definitions
2.8
and set
Z=0
on flow,

t_{k}=k_{0}+k
,
Z_{k}=Z(k)
.
With
P
constant on each interval
(k,k+1)
the flow conditions
are trivially satisfied, and the I-GRE reduces to the

DT-GRE
at each step, with jump operator

\mathcal{D}_{Z}(k,P(k+1),P(k)):=\mathcal{J}^{*}(k,P(k+1))-E^{*}P(k)E+Z(k).

(DT-GRE)

The key identity reduces to the discrete-time key identity

J_{N}(X,X_{1}^{0})=\langle P(k_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle+\textstyle\sum_{k=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+N-1}\langle\mathcal{D}_{Z}(k,P(k+1),P(k)),\,X(k)\rangle,

and the dual LMI is

J_{N}^{*}(X_{1}^{0})=\max_{P\succeq 0}\;\langle P(k_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\mathcal{D}_{Z}(k,P(k+1),P(k))\succeq 0,\;P(k_{0}+N)\preceq Z_{N},

(32)

recovering
[
50
]
.

3.2
Infinite-horizon

We now study the optimal control problem on the infinite horizon

[t_{0},\infty)
. The setup mirrors Section
3.1
but with
two important differences. First, there is no terminal cost
Z_{T}
:
the horizon extends to infinity so no terminal penalty is imposed.
Second, the I-GRE no longer has a terminal condition
P(T)=Z_{T}
;
instead, the dual variable
P_{\infty}
is characterized as the
bounded monotone limit of the finite-horizon solutions
P_{T}
as

T\to\infty
(Theorem
3.12
).

The infinite-horizon problem is well-posed, meaning the optimal
cost is finite and uniquely represented as
\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
, under the uniform stabilizability, coercivity, and boundedness
assumptions (H1)–(H3) stated below. Unlike most treatments of
infinite-horizon problems for impulsive systems
[
46
,
27
,
1
]
, no time-invariance of
\mathcal{F}
,

\mathcal{J}
, or
Z
is assumed: the LTV setting requires the
monotone-limit argument below in place of the algebraic Riccati
equation. The theorem also provides a dual characterization: the
infinite-horizon optimal cost equals the supremum of
\langle P,\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle

over all bounded positive semidefinite
P
satisfying the LMI
constraints, paralleling Theorem
3.8
at the infinite
horizon.

3.2.1
Setting, cost function, and assumptions (infinite horizon)

The infinite-horizon cost removes the terminal penalty
\langle Z_{T},\,EX(T)E^{*}\rangle

from the finite-horizon cost (
8
), since no fixed terminal
time is imposed. The resulting integral over
[t_{0},\infty)
is

J_{\infty}(X,X_{1}^{0}):=\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}\langle Z(t),\,X(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\langle Z_{k},\,X(t_{k}^{-})\rangle,

(33)

with problem

\min_{X(\cdot)\in\mathbb{H}^{n+m}_{\succeq 0}}J_{\infty}(X,X_{1}^{0})\ \textnormal{subject to }\eqref{eq:sys:IMP}\textnormal{ and }EX(t_{0})E^{*}=X_{1}^{0},

(34)

and optimal cost

J_{\infty}^{\star}(X_{1}^{0}):=\inf_{X(\cdot)\in\mathbb{H}^{n+m}_{\succeq 0}}J_{\infty}(X,X_{1}^{0}).

As in Section
3.1
, the decision variable is the full
trajectory
X(\cdot)
with
X_{1}
constrained and
(X_{2},X_{3})
free.

Well-posedness of the infinite-horizon problem requires three
conditions on the system and cost operators.

(H1) Uniform stabilizability.

There exists an admissible trajectory
X_{s}
with free variables

X_{2}^{s}=K_{s}X_{1}^{s}
,
X_{3}^{s}=K_{s}X_{1}^{s}K_{s}^{*}
, constants

\beta\geq 1
,
\alpha>0
,
\rho\in(0,1)
,
C_{s}>0
, such that

\|EX_{s}(t)E^{*}\|_{*}\leq\beta e^{-\alpha(t-s)}\rho^{\kappa(t,s)}\|EX_{s}(s)E^{*}\|_{*}

for all
t\geq s\geq t_{0}
and

J_{\infty}(X_{s},X_{1}^{0})\leq C_{s}\|X_{1}^{0}\|_{*}
, where
\kappa(t,s)
denotes the number of jumps in the interval
(s,t)
.

(H2) Uniform coercivity.

EZ(t)E^{*}\succeq\delta I
uniformly for some
\delta>0
.

(H3) Uniform boundedness.

Z
,
Z_{k}
,
\mathcal{F}(t,\cdot)
,
\mathcal{J}(k,\cdot)
are
uniformly bounded;
(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}(t,P(t))\succeq 0
and

(\mathcal{D}_{Z})_{3}(k,P(t_{k}^{+}),P(t_{k}^{-}))\succeq 0
for all
P\succeq 0
;

{(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}}^{\dagger}
and
{(\mathcal{D}_{Z})_{3}}^{\dagger}
are
uniformly bounded on bounded sets of
P
.

The key differences from the finite-horizon case
(Section
3.1
) are as follows.
In the finite-horizon case, the I-GRE is a terminal-value ODE
starting from
P(t_{0}+T)=Z_{T}
and solved backward to
t_{0}
.
In the infinite-horizon case, there is no terminal condition:
instead, the dual variable
P_{\infty}
is characterized as the
bounded monotone limit of the finite-horizon solutions
P_{T}

as
T\to\infty
. The infinite-horizon I-GRE is the equation
satisfied by this limit, which in the LTI case reduces to the
classical algebraic Riccati equation. Under (H2),

P_{\infty}\succeq\alpha_{1}I\succ 0
(strict positivity).

3.2.2
Sufficient condition (infinite horizon)

The following theorem shows that any bounded positive solution of the
infinite-horizon I-GRE is the unique such solution and directly
provides the optimal cost and the optimal policy. The proof mirrors
Theorem
3.5
but with the finite-horizon terminal
correction replaced by a Gronwall argument that forces

\langle P_{\infty}(T),\,EX^{\star}(T)E^{*}\rangle\to 0
.

Theorem 3.11

(Sufficient condition, infinite-horizon)
.

Suppose there exists a bounded piecewise-
C^{1}
function

P_{\infty}:[t_{0},\infty)\to\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
satisfying the
infinite-horizon I-GRE
:
(
16
) for almost all
t\geq t_{0}
and (
17
) at all jump times.

Then
:

(a)

J_{\infty}^{\star}(X_{1}^{0})=\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
, attained at
the trajectory with
K_{\infty}(t)\in\mathcal{K}_{c}(t,P_{\infty}(t))

on flow and
K_{k}\in\mathcal{K}_{d}(k,P_{\infty}(t_{k}^{+}),P_{\infty}(t_{k}^{-}))

at each jump.

(b)

P_{\infty}
is the unique bounded solution of the
infinite-horizon I-GRE.

Proof.

Lower bound.

For any
X
with
J_{\infty}(X,X_{1}^{0})<\infty
, apply the key
identity (
13
) on
[t_{0},t_{0}+T]
with
P_{\infty}

(note that
\mathcal{C}_{Z}(t,P_{\infty})\succeq 0
and

\mathcal{D}_{Z}\succeq 0
by the infinite-horizon I-GRE):

\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\langle Z(t),\,X(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\sum_{k:\,t_{k}\leq T}\langle Z_{k},\,X(t_{k}^{-})\rangle\geq\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle-\langle P_{\infty}(T),\,EX(T)E^{*}\rangle.

Under (H2), finite cost forces
\|EX(t)E^{*}\|_{*}\to 0
; since

P_{\infty}\preceq\alpha_{2}I
,

\langle P_{\infty}(T),\,EX(T)E^{*}\rangle\leq\alpha_{2}\|EX(T)E^{*}\|_{*}\to 0
.
Taking
T\to\infty
:
J_{\infty}(X,X_{1}^{0})\geq\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
.

Attainment.

For
K_{\infty}\in\mathcal{K}_{c}(t,P_{\infty})
and

K_{k}\in\mathcal{K}_{d}(k,P_{\infty}(t_{k}^{+}),P_{\infty}(t_{k}^{-}))
,
(
24
)–(
25
) hold along

X^{\star}
. The key identity on
[t_{0},t_{0}+T]
gives

\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\langle Z(t),\,X^{\star}(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\sum_{k:\,t_{k}\leq T}\langle Z_{k},\,X^{\star}(t_{k}^{-})\rangle=\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle-\langle P_{\infty}(T),\,EX^{\star}(T)E^{*}\rangle.

(35)

Differentiating
\langle P_{\infty}(t),\,EX^{\star}(t)E^{*}\rangle
along the optimal
flow gives
\tfrac{d}{dt}\langle P_{\infty}(t),\,EX^{\star}(t)E^{*}\rangle=-\langle Z(t),\,X^{\star}(t)\rangle
over each
(t_{k},t_{k+1})
.
Since
\langle Z(t),\,X^{\star}(t)\rangle\geq\delta\|EX^{\star}(t)E^{*}\|_{*}\geq\tfrac{\delta}{\alpha_{2}}\langle P_{\infty}(t),\,EX^{\star}(t)E^{*}\rangle

(using (H2), that is
Z(t)\succeq\delta E^{*}E
, and
P_{\infty}(t)\preceq\alpha_{2}I
), Gronwall gives

\langle P_{\infty}(t),\,EX^{\star}(t)E^{*}\rangle\leq e^{-\delta(t-t_{k})/\alpha_{2}}\langle P_{\infty}(t_{k}^{+}),\,EX^{\star}(t_{k}^{+})E^{*}\rangle
.

At jumps we have
\langle P_{\infty}(t_{k}^{+}),\,EX^{\star}(t_{k}^{+})E^{*}\rangle-\langle P_{\infty}(t_{k}^{-}),\,EX^{\star}(t_{k}^{-})E^{*}\rangle=-\langle Z_{k},\,X^{\star}(t_{k}^{-})\rangle
, and using a similar argument as before, we get
\langle P_{\infty}(t_{k}^{+}),\,EX^{\star}(t_{k}^{+})E^{*}\rangle\leq\rho\langle P_{\infty}(t_{k}^{-}),\,EX^{\star}(t_{k}^{-})E^{*}\rangle
where
\rho:=1-\delta/\alpha_{2}<1
. Combining those expression yields

\langle P_{\infty}(T),\,EX^{\star}(T)E^{*}\rangle\leq e^{-\delta(T-t_{k})/\alpha_{2}}\rho^{\kappa(T,t_{0})}\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle\to 0\ \textnormal{as }T\to\infty

Taking
T\to\infty
in (
35
) yields
J_{\infty}(X^{\star},X_{1}^{0})=\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle=J_{\infty}^{\star}(X_{1}^{0})
.

Uniqueness.

If
\tilde{P}
is another bounded solution, the lower-bound and
attainment arguments give
J_{\infty}^{\star}(X_{1}^{0})=\langle\tilde{P}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
.
Since also
J_{\infty}^{\star}=\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
, taking

X_{1}^{0}=vv^{*}
for all
v
gives
P_{\infty}\equiv\tilde{P}
.
∎

3.2.3
Necessary condition and existence (infinite horizon)

Under assumptions (H1)–(H3), the existence of a bounded solution
to the infinite-horizon I-GRE is guaranteed by a monotone convergence
argument: the finite-horizon solutions
P_{T}
form a non-decreasing
bounded sequence and their pointwise limit satisfies the I-GRE.
This mirrors the role of Theorem
3.6
at the
infinite-horizon level.

Theorem 3.12

(Necessary condition and existence, infinite-horizon)
.

Under
(H1)–(H3)
, there exist
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}>0
and
a unique bounded piecewise-
C^{1}
solution

P_{\infty}:[t_{0},\infty)\to\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
,

\alpha_{1}I\preceq P_{\infty}\preceq\alpha_{2}I
, of the
infinite-horizon I-GRE. It satisfies

P_{\infty}=\lim_{T\to\infty}P_{T}
(monotone:
P_{T^{\prime}}\succeq P_{T}

for
T^{\prime}>T
), where
P_{T}
solves the finite-horizon I-GRE with

P_{T}(t_{0}+T)=0
, and the infinite-horizon optimal cost is

J_{\infty}^{\star}(X_{1}^{0})=\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
.

Proof.

Monotone limit.

Corollary
3.7
gives a unique
P_{T}\in\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
for
each
T>0
.

Monotonicity.

For
T^{\prime}>T
, restricting any
T^{\prime}
-admissible
X
to
[t_{0},t_{0}+T]

gives a
T
-admissible trajectory with no larger cost (since the
additional terms on
(T,T^{\prime}]
are non-negative). Hence

J_{T}^{\star}(X_{1}^{0})\leq J_{T^{\prime}}^{*}(X_{1}^{0})
and
P_{T}(t)\preceq P_{T^{\prime}}(t)
.

Bounds.

Lower:
P_{T}\succeq 0
since
Z,Z_{k}\succeq 0
.
Upper: (H1) gives
P_{T}(t_{0})\preceq C_{s}I=:\alpha_{2}I
uniformly.
Strict lower: by (H3) the generator
\mathcal{F}
and the optimal
gain
K(t)
are uniformly bounded, so along any optimal closed-loop
trajectory
\tfrac{d}{dt}\mathrm{trace}(X_{1}(t))=\mathrm{trace}(\mathcal{F}(t,X^{\star}(t)))\geq-c\,\mathrm{trace}(X_{1}(t))
for some
c>0
, whence
\mathrm{trace}(X_{1}(t))\geq e^{-c(t-t_{0})}\mathrm{trace}(X_{1}^{0})
(a Gronwall lower bound). By (H2),

Q(t)=EZ(t)E^{*}\succeq\delta I
, so for any fixed
h>0
with
t_{0}+h\leq t_{0}+T
,

\langle P_{T}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle=J_{T}^{\star}(X_{1}^{0})\geq\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+h}\!\!\langle Q(t),\,X_{1}(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t\geq\delta\!\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+h}\!\!\mathrm{trace}(X_{1}(t))\,\mathrm{d}t\geq\delta\,\tfrac{1-e^{-ch}}{c}\,\mathrm{trace}(X_{1}^{0}).

Taking
X_{1}^{0}=vv^{*}
and
\alpha_{1}:=\delta(1-e^{-ch})/c>0
gives

P_{T}(t_{0})\succeq\alpha_{1}I
for all
T\geq h
; the same argument from each

t
gives
P_{T}(t)\succeq\alpha_{1}I
.

Convergence.

\{P_{T}(t)\}
is bounded and non-decreasing; its pointwise limit

P_{\infty}
exists. Equicontinuity
2
2

2

A family of functions is

equicontinuous
when a single modulus of continuity serves all of
its members simultaneously; here it follows from a uniform Lipschitz
bound. On each flow interval the I-GRE flow
conditions (
16
) determine
E^{*}\dot{P}_{T}(t)E
as a
fixed continuous function of
P_{T}(t)
and the flow data; by the uniform
boundedness (H3) of the data and of the pseudoinverses on bounded sets,
together with the uniform bound
0\preceq P_{T}\preceq\alpha_{2}I
, there is
a constant
L
(independent of
T
) such that
\|\dot{P}_{T}(t)\|\leq L
for
all
T
and almost every
t
. Hence every
P_{T}
is
L
-Lipschitz, so the
family
\{P_{T}\}
is equicontinuous.
from the I-GRE and (H3), plus
Arzelà-Ascoli, give uniform convergence on compact subintervals.
Passing
T\to\infty
in the integrated I-GRE shows
P_{\infty}

satisfies the infinite-horizon I-GRE.

Optimality.

P_{\infty}
is bounded and satisfies the infinite-horizon I-GRE, so
Theorem
3.11
gives

J_{\infty}^{\star}(X_{1}^{0})=\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
.
∎

3.2.4
Equivalence and dual LMI (infinite horizon)

Theorems
3.11
and
3.12
together
give the complete characterization. The dual LMI extends the
finite-horizon version (Theorem
3.8
) by replacing
the terminal constraint
P(T)\preceq Z_{T}
with a boundedness condition.

Corollary 3.13

(Equivalence, infinite-horizon)
.

The infinite-horizon problem is well-posed with

J_{\infty}^{\star}(X_{1}^{0})=\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
for all

X_{1}^{0}\succeq 0
if and only if the infinite-horizon I-GRE has a
bounded piecewise-
C^{1}
solution
P_{\infty}\succeq 0
.
The solution is unique.

Proof.

Necessity: Theorem
3.12
.
Sufficiency: Theorem
3.11
.
Uniqueness: Theorem
3.11
(
b
).
∎

Theorem 3.14

(Dual LMI, infinite-horizon)
.

Under
(H1)–(H3)
,

J_{\infty}^{\star}(X_{1}^{0})=\sup_{P}\;\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\mathcal{C}_{Z}(t,P(t))\succeq 0,\;\mathcal{D}_{Z}(k,P(t_{k}^{+}),P(t_{k}^{-}))\succeq 0,\;P\text{ bounded};

(36)

the supremum is attained at
P_{\infty}
.

Proof.

For any bounded feasible
P
, the key identity on
[t_{0},t_{0}+T]

and the LMI constraints give

J_{T}(X,X_{1}^{0})\geq\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle-\langle P(T),\,EX(T)E^{*}\rangle
.
As
P
is bounded and
\|EX(T)E^{*}\|_{*}\to 0
(from (H2)),
taking
T\to\infty
gives
J_{\infty}^{\star}\geq\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
.
Since
P_{\infty}
is feasible and
J_{\infty}^{\star}=\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
,
the supremum equals
J_{\infty}^{\star}
and is attained at
P_{\infty}
.
∎

3.2.5
Dwell-time and causal conditions

Both the finite-horizon I-GRE (Section
3.1
) and the
infinite-horizon I-GRE (Section
3.2
) are solved

backward
in time: the dual variable
P
is computed from a
terminal condition and propagated toward the initial time. Evaluating
the optimal gain
K(t)\in\mathcal{K}_{c}(t,P(t))
at any given time
t

therefore requires knowing
P(t)
, which in turn depends on the next
jump time
t_{k+1}
through the backward GRE’s terminal condition at

t_{k+1}^{-}
. Since
t_{k+1}
is not known in advance in most
applications, these optimal policies are non-causal and cannot be
implemented online.

This section derives
causal
policies by restricting to
specific dwell-time scenarios. Section
3.2.5

treats periodic impulses (fixed period
T_{p}
): the periodic I-GRE
(Theorem
3.17
) gives a causal policy that is also
necessary and sufficient. Theorem
3.19
treats a
minimum dwell-time constraint
\Delta_{k}\geq T_{\min}
: the
Geromel–Colaneri stability trick (Theorem
3.19
) reduces
the infinite-horizon verification to an LMI on the bounded interval

[0,T_{\min}]
plus a single frozen condition at
T_{\min}
.
Section
3.2.5
treats a range dwell-time

T_{\min}\leq\Delta_{k}\leq T_{\max}
: Theorem
3.22
gives a
policy verifiable on
[0,T_{\max}]
without the stability condition.
In all three cases, the key tool is the
forward Riccati ODE

(equation (
39
)), which propagates
P
forward
in the timer
\tau=t-t_{k}
from the post-jump value.

Non-causality of the I-GRE and the timer variable

The I-GRE is a terminal-value problem:
P
is computed backward from

P(t_{0}+T)=Z_{T}
toward the initial time
t_{0}
.
On the inter-jump interval
(t_{k},t_{k+1})
, the flow GRE requires
knowing the value
P(t_{k+1}^{-})
, which depends on the jump
condition at
t_{k+1}
. Since the next jump time
t_{k+1}
is not
known before it occurs, the optimal policy is
non-causal
.

Timer variable.

Introduce the
timer

\tau(t):=t-t_{k},\quad t\in[t_{k},t_{k+1}),

(37)

satisfying
\dot{\tau}=1
and resetting to
0
after each jump.
A policy depending only on
X_{1}
and
\tau
is
causal
.
Set
\tilde{P}(\tau):=P(t_{k}+\tau)
for
\tau\in[0,\Delta_{k})
,

\Delta_{k}:=t_{k+1}-t_{k}
. The I-GRE flow condition
in terms of
\tau
reads (with a slight abuse of notation)

\mathcal{C}_{Z}\!\left(\tau,\tilde{P}(\tau)\right)\succeq 0,\qquad\mathcal{C}_{Z}\!\left(\tau,\tilde{P}\right)\,\widetilde{/}\,\left(\mathcal{C}_{Z}\right)_{3}\!\left(\tau,\tilde{P}\right)=0,

(38)

where
\mathcal{C}_{Z}
uses
t=t_{k}+\tau
.

Forward-in-time reformulation.

The Schur complement condition in (
38
) expresses

\frac{d\tilde{P}}{d\tau}
as a closed-form Riccati-type ODE:
with
(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}(\tau,\tilde{P})\succ 0
(regular case), the
GRE equality
\mathcal{C}_{Z}\,\widetilde{/}\,\allowbreak(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}=0

gives

E^{*}\tfrac{d\tilde{P}}{d\tau}E=-\mathcal{F}^{*}(\tau,\tilde{P})-Z(\tau)+(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{2}(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}^{-1}(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{2}^{*}.

(39)

This is a
forward
ODE: given the post-jump value

\tilde{P}(0^{+})
set by the jump GRE, equation (
39
)
propagates
\tilde{P}(\tau)
forward in
\tau
without requiring
knowledge of the future dwell-time
\Delta_{k}
. Along its solution,

\mathcal{C}_{Z}=0
by construction, so the GRE condition (
38
)
is automatically satisfied and the policy

K(\tau)\in\mathcal{K}_{c}(\tau,\tilde{P}(\tau))
is optimal within
each flow interval.

The remaining difficulty is the
initial condition

\tilde{P}(0^{+})
, which comes from the jump GRE:

\mathcal{D}_{Z}(k,\tilde{P}(0^{+}),\tilde{P}(\Delta_{k}^{-}))\succeq 0

and its Schur complement equals zero. This condition links

\tilde{P}(0^{+})
to
\tilde{P}(\Delta_{k}^{-})
, the value at the

next
jump, which is again unknown. The three special cases
below show how to obtain fully causal policies under specific
dwell-time constraints.

Periodic impulses

Assume
\Delta_{k}\equiv T_{p}
(constant period), that
\mathcal{F}(\cdot,X)

and
Z(\cdot)
are
T_{p}
-periodic functions of
t
, and that

\mathcal{J}(k,\cdot)
and
Z_{k}
are independent of
k
, i.e.,
\mathcal{J}(k,\cdot)=\mathcal{J}(\cdot)
,
Z
,
Z_{k}\equiv Z_{0}
(equivalently,

T_{p}
-periodic in the sense that the same jump operator and jump cost
apply at every
t_{k}
).
Under these assumptions, the operators

\mathcal{D}_{Z}(P^{+},P^{-}):=\mathcal{J}^{*}(P^{+})-E^{*}P^{-}E+Z_{0},\qquad\mathcal{K}_{d}(P^{+},P^{-}):=\{K\mid(\mathcal{D}_{Z})_{3}K^{*}+(\mathcal{D}_{Z})_{2}^{*}=0\}

are also independent of
k
, and we drop the
k
argument throughout
this section.

The I-GRE on each period
[t_{k},t_{k+1}]
takes the same form, and we
seek a
T_{p}
-periodic solution
P_{\infty}
of the

periodic I-GRE
: a piecewise-
C^{1}

P_{\infty}:[0,T_{p}]\to\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}

satisfying

\displaystyle\textit{Flow on }(0,T_{p}):\quad\mathcal{C}_{Z}\!\left(\tau,P_{\infty}(\tau)\right)\succeq 0,\quad\mathcal{C}_{Z}\,\widetilde{/}\,(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}=0,

(40a)

\displaystyle\textit{Jump at }\tau=0:\quad\mathcal{D}_{Z}\!\left(P_{\infty}(0^{+}),P_{\infty}(T_{p}^{-})\right)\succeq 0,\quad\mathcal{D}_{Z}\,\widetilde{/}\,(\mathcal{D}_{Z})_{3}=0,

(40b)

\displaystyle\textit{Periodicity:}\quad P_{\infty}(0^{+})\text{~determined by the jump GRE from }P_{\infty}(T_{p}^{-}).

(40c)

The system (
40
) is equivalent to the fixed-point
equation
\Phi(P_{\infty}(T_{p}^{-}))=P_{\infty}(T_{p}^{-})
, where the

one-period map

\Phi:\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}\to\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
is constructed as
follows: given any
Q\in\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
, define
\Phi(Q)
by

(i)

applying the jump GRE (
40b
) with
pre-jump value
Q
to obtain
\tilde{P}(0^{+})\in\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
;

(ii)

integrating the forward Riccati ODE (
39
)
on
[0,T_{p}]
with initial condition
\tilde{P}(0^{+})
, and
setting
\Phi(Q):=\tilde{P}(T_{p}^{-})
.

Lemma 3.15

(Monotone convergence of the multi-period I-GRE)
.

Under the conditions of Theorem
3.17
,
let
P_{n}
denote the I-GRE solution over exactly
n
periods
with zero terminal cost
P_{n}(t_{0}+nT_{p})=0
. Define the

one-period map

\Phi:\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}\to\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0},\qquad\Phi(Q)\;:=\;\tilde{P}(T_{p}^{-})

(41)

where
\tilde{P}(0^{+})
is obtained from
Q
via the jump
GRE (
40b
) and
\tilde{P}(T_{p}^{-})
is
the endpoint of the forward Riccati ODE (
39
)
integrated on
[0,T_{p}]
from
\tilde{P}(0^{+})
.
Then:

(a)

The iterates
\Phi^{n}(0):=P_{n}(0^{-})
form a non-decreasing
sequence:
\Phi^{n+1}(0)\succeq\Phi^{n}(0)
for all
n\geq 1
.

(b)

The iterates are bounded:
\Phi^{n}(0)\preceq\alpha_{2}I

for all
n
, with
\alpha_{2}=C_{s}
from
(H1)
.

(c)

The limit
Q_{\infty}:=\lim_{n\to\infty}\Phi^{n}(0)
exists,
satisfies
\alpha_{1}I\preceq Q_{\infty}\preceq\alpha_{2}I
,
and is the unique fixed point of
\Phi
in
[\alpha_{1}I,\alpha_{2}I]
.

Proof.

(a) Monotonicity.

For any
(n+1)T_{p}
-horizon admissible trajectory
X
, its restriction
to the first
nT_{p}
is a valid
nT_{p}
-horizon trajectory (the remaining

T_{p}
contributes non-negative cost since
Z,Z_{k}\succeq 0
). Hence

J_{nT_{p}}^{*}(X_{1}^{0})\leq J_{(n+1)T_{p}}^{*}(X_{1}^{0})
, giving

\langle P_{n}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle\leq\langle P_{n+1}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
for all

X_{1}^{0}\succeq 0
, i.e.,
P_{n}(t_{0})\preceq P_{n+1}(t_{0})
.
Under periodic data, this translates to

\Phi^{n}(0)=P_{n}(0^{-})\preceq P_{n+1}(0^{-})=\Phi^{n+1}(0)
.

(b) Upper bound.

By (H1),
J_{nT_{p}}^{*}(X_{1}^{0})\leq C_{s}\|X_{1}^{0}\|_{*}
for all
n
, so

\langle P_{n}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle\leq C_{s}\|X_{1}^{0}\|_{*}
. Taking
X_{1}^{0}=vv^{*}

for all
v
gives
P_{n}(t_{0})\preceq C_{s}I=:\alpha_{2}I
.

(c) Convergence and uniqueness.

Parts (
a
)–(
b
) give a non-decreasing
sequence
\Phi^{n}(0)
bounded in
[\alpha_{1}I,\alpha_{2}I]

(strict positivity
P_{n}\succeq\alpha_{1}I
follows from (H2) as in
Theorem
3.12
). By standard monotone convergence for
symmetric matrices,
Q_{\infty}:=\lim_{n}\Phi^{n}(0)
exists.

Fixed point:

\Phi
is continuous on
[\alpha_{1}I,\alpha_{2}I]

by the Lipschitz bound on the forward Riccati ODE and the pseudoinverse
continuity in (H3). Hence

\Phi(Q_{\infty})=\Phi\!\left(\lim_{n}\Phi^{n}(0)\right)=\lim_{n}\Phi^{n+1}(0)=Q_{\infty}
.

Uniqueness:
Suppose
\hat{Q}_{\infty}
is another fixed point, i.e.,
another
T_{p}
-periodic solution of (
40
).
Both
Q_{\infty}
and
\hat{Q}_{\infty}
represent infinite-horizon costs:

\langle Q_{\infty},\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle=J_{\infty}^{\star}(X_{1}^{0})=\langle\hat{Q}_{\infty},\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle

for all
X_{1}^{0}\succeq 0
(by Theorem
3.12
, which identifies
the unique infinite-horizon optimal cost regardless of the periodicity
structure). Taking
X_{1}^{0}=vv^{*}
gives
Q_{\infty}=\hat{Q}_{\infty}
.
∎

Remark 3.16
.

Lemma
3.15
establishes existence and uniqueness
via monotone iteration, which does not require the one-period map

\Phi
to be a contraction in operator norm on a single step.
In fact,
\Phi
is always a contraction for sufficiently large

n_{0}
: the iterates
\Phi^{n_{0}}
are a contraction on
[\alpha_{1}I,\alpha_{2}I]
with constant
L<1
(the Lipschitz bound is

L=O(\rho^{2n_{0}}e^{-2\alpha n_{0}T_{p}})
, accumulating one jump
factor
\rho
and one flow factor
e^{-\alpha T_{p}}
per period over the

n_{0}
periods, from the exponential stability rate
in (H1)), but the single-step map need not satisfy
L<1
.
See
[
11
]
for the classical periodic Riccati theory
and
[
16
]
for the stability counterpart.

Theorem 3.17

(Causal conditions: periodic impulses)
.

Assume
\Delta_{k}\equiv T_{p}
, that
\mathcal{F}(\cdot,X)
and

Z(\cdot)
are
T_{p}
-periodic, and that
\mathcal{J}(k,\cdot)
and

Z_{k}
are independent of
k
. Under
(H1)–(H3)
:

(a)

(Existence and uniqueness.)

There exists a unique
T_{p}
-periodic piecewise-
C^{1}
solution

P_{\infty}(\tau)
of the periodic I-GRE (
40
).

(b)

(Sufficiency.)

The policy
K(\tau)\in\mathcal{K}_{c}(\tau,P_{\infty}(\tau))
on
flow and
K_{k}\in\mathcal{K}_{d}(P_{\infty}(0^{+}),P_{\infty}(T_{p}^{-}))

at each jump is causal and achieves

J_{\infty}^{*}(X_{1}^{0})=\langle P_{\infty}(0),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
.

(c)

(Necessity.)

Any causal policy that achieves the infinite-horizon optimal
cost
J_{\infty}^{*}
must use gains from the periodic I-GRE, so
the conditions are also necessary.

Proof.

Existence and uniqueness (a).

Lemma
3.15
(
c
) gives the unique
fixed point
Q_{\infty}
of
\Phi
, so the pre-jump value

P_{\infty}(T_{p}^{-}):=Q_{\infty}
is uniquely determined. The post-jump
value
P_{\infty}(0^{+})
follows from the jump
GRE (
40b
) with terminal value
Q_{\infty}

(the attaining gain is unique by Proposition
3.1
).
The flow profile
\tau\mapsto P_{\infty}(\tau)
on
(0,T_{p})
is then
the unique solution of the forward Riccati
ODE (
39
) started at
P_{\infty}(0^{+})
. By
construction,
P_{\infty}
is
T_{p}
-periodic and
piecewise-
C^{1}
, and satisfies
(
40a
)–(
40c
).

Sufficiency (b).

The
T_{p}
-periodic extension of
P_{\infty}
to all of
[t_{0},\infty)

satisfies the flow GRE with equality
\mathcal{C}_{Z}\widetilde{/}(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}=0
and the
jump GRE with equality
\mathcal{D}_{Z}\,\widetilde{/}\,(\mathcal{D}_{Z})_{3}=0
at every jump. Apply the key identity
(Proposition
3.1
) on
[t_{0},t_{0}+T]
with this
P_{\infty}

and no terminal cost (
Z_{T}=0
):

\displaystyle J_{T}(X,X_{1}^{0})

\displaystyle=\langle P_{\infty}(0^{+}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle-\langle P_{\infty}(T^{-}),\,EX(T)E^{*}\rangle

\displaystyle\quad+\underbrace{\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\langle\mathcal{C}_{Z}(\tau,P_{\infty}),\,X\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t}_{\geq 0}+\underbrace{\textstyle\sum_{k}\langle\mathcal{D}_{Z}(P_{\infty}(0^{+}),P_{\infty}(T_{p}^{-})),\,X(t_{k}^{-})\rangle}_{\geq 0}.

(42)

Since
\mathcal{C}_{Z},\mathcal{D}_{Z}\succeq 0
on the periodic solution,

J_{T}(X,X_{1}^{0})\geq\langle P_{\infty}(0^{+}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle-\langle P_{\infty}(T^{-}),\,EX(T)E^{*}\rangle
for all admissible
X
.
Under (H1),
\|EX(T)E^{*}\|_{*}\to 0
for any admissible trajectory;
since
P_{\infty}
is bounded (Lemma
3.15
),

\langle P_{\infty}(T^{-}),\,EX(T)E^{*}\rangle\to 0
as
T\to\infty
, giving

J_{\infty}(X,X_{1}^{0})\geq\langle P_{\infty}(0^{+}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
.

For the policy
K(\tau)\in\mathcal{K}_{c}(\tau,P_{\infty}(\tau))
(flow)
and
K_{k}\in\mathcal{K}_{d}(P_{\infty}(0^{+}),P_{\infty}(T_{p}^{-}))
(jump),
both GRE Schur equalities are achieved, so

\langle\mathcal{C}_{Z},\,X^{\star}\rangle=0
and
\langle\mathcal{D}_{Z},\,X^{\star}\rangle=0

everywhere. The key identity gives

J_{T}(X^{\star},X_{1}^{0})=\langle P_{\infty}(0^{+}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle-\langle P_{\infty}(T^{-}),\,EX^{\star}(T)E^{*}\rangle
,
and taking
T\to\infty
with
\langle P_{\infty}(T^{-}),\,EX^{\star}(T)E^{*}\rangle\to 0

yields
J_{\infty}(X^{\star},X_{1}^{0})=\langle P_{\infty}(0^{+}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
.
The policy is causal since
P_{\infty}(\tau)
depends only on the timer.

Necessity (c).

By Theorem
3.12
, under (H1)–(H3) there is a

unique
bounded piecewise-
C^{1}
solution
P_{\infty}^{ih}
of the
infinite-horizon I-GRE satisfying

J_{\infty}^{\star}(X_{1}^{0})=\langle P_{\infty}^{ih}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
.
We show
P_{\infty}^{ih}
is
T_{p}
-periodic, hence equal to
P_{\infty}
.

Define
\hat{P}(\tau):=P_{\infty}^{ih}(t_{0}+T_{p}+\tau)
for
\tau\geq 0
.
Since
\mathcal{F}
and
Z
are
T_{p}
-periodic and
\mathcal{J}
and
Z_{k}
are time-independent,

\hat{P}
satisfies the same infinite-horizon I-GRE as
P_{\infty}^{ih}
.
By uniqueness (Theorem
3.12
),

\hat{P}\equiv P_{\infty}^{ih}
, i.e.,

P_{\infty}^{ih}(t_{0}+T_{p}+\tau)=P_{\infty}^{ih}(t_{0}+\tau)
for all

\tau\geq 0
: the infinite-horizon dual is
T_{p}
-periodic.

Being a
T_{p}
-periodic solution of the I-GRE,
P_{\infty}^{ih}
is a
fixed point of
\Phi
. By uniqueness of the fixed point
(Lemma
3.15
(
c
)),

P_{\infty}^{ih}=P_{\infty}
. Any optimal dual variable must equal

P_{\infty}^{ih}=P_{\infty}
, so the optimal policy must arise from the
periodic I-GRE.
∎

Minimum dwell-time

For a minimum dwell-time (MDT) constraint
\Delta_{k}\geq T_{\min}>0
,
the dwell-time
\Delta_{k}
is variable and unknown in advance.
We assume throughout this subsection that the flow and jump data

depend only on the timer

\tau:=t-t_{k}
(not on absolute time
or on the jump index), and
the flow data are time-invariant beyond

T_{\min}
and that
the jump data are
k
-independent
:

\mathcal{F}(\tau,\cdot)\equiv\mathcal{F}_{\infty}(\cdot),\quad Z(\tau)\equiv Z(T_{\min})\;\;\text{for }\tau\geq T_{\min},\qquad\mathcal{J}(k,\cdot)\equiv\mathcal{J}(\cdot),\quad Z_{k}\equiv Z_{\mathrm{jp}}\;\;\text{for all }k.

(43)

Under (
43
), the operators

\mathcal{D}_{Z}(P^{+},P^{-})
and
\mathcal{K}_{d}(P^{+},P^{-})
are also

k
-independent, and we drop the
k
argument throughout this
subsection. Under (
43
), the Geromel–Colaneri
technique
[
32
]
shows that the GRE condition for all

\tau\geq T_{\min}
reduces to a single LMI at
\tau=T_{\min}

combined with a condition on the bounded interval
[0,T_{\min}]
.

Definition 3.18

(Frozen composite)
.

Under (
43
), for any
Q\in\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
, define
the
frozen composite
:

\mathcal{C}_{Z}^{0}(Q):=\mathcal{F}_{\infty}^{*}(Q)+Z(T_{\min})\in\mathbb{H}^{n+m}.

(44)

The frozen composite is linear in
Q
(hence an LMI) and represents
the composite
\mathcal{C}_{Z}
evaluated with no time derivative
of
Q
and on the time-invariant data beyond
T_{\min}
.

Theorem 3.19

(Sufficient causal condition: MDT)
.

Let
T_{\min}>0
and assume the flow data satisfy the
time-invariance condition (
43
).
Suppose there exists a piecewise-
C^{1}
function

P_{s}:[0,T_{\min}]\to\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
satisfying:

(i)

Flow GRE on
[0,T_{\min}]
:

P_{s}
solves the forward Riccati ODE (
39
)
on
(0,T_{\min})
, i.e.,

\mathcal{C}_{Z}(\tau,P_{s}(\tau))\succeq 0
with

\mathcal{C}_{Z}\,\widetilde{/}\,(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}=0
for all

\tau\in(0,T_{\min})
.

(ii)

Jump GRE at
\tau=T_{\min}
:

\mathcal{D}_{Z}(P_{s}(0^{+}),P_{s}(T_{\min}))\succeq 0

and
\mathcal{D}_{Z}\,\widetilde{/}\,(\mathcal{D}_{Z})_{3}=0
.

(iii)

Geromel–Colaneri stability condition:

The frozen composite satisfies the single LMI

\mathcal{C}_{Z}^{0}(P_{s}(T_{\min})):=\mathcal{F}_{\infty}^{*}(P_{s}(T_{\min}))+Z(T_{\min})\preceq 0.

(45)

Then for any dwell-time sequence with
\Delta_{k}\geq T_{\min}
, the
policy

K(\tau)\in\mathcal{K}_{c}(\tau,P_{s}(\tau))
for
\tau\in[0,T_{\min}]
,

K(\tau)=K(T_{\min})
for
\tau>T_{\min}
on flow,
and
K_{k}\in\mathcal{K}_{d}(P_{s}(0^{+}),P_{s}(T_{\min}))
at each jump,
is causal and achieves
J_{T}(X^{\star},X_{1}^{0})\leq\langle P_{s}(0),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle

for all
T>0
. In particular,

J_{\infty}^{*}(X_{1}^{0})\leq\langle P_{s}(0),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
. The
conditions
(i)–(iii)
are verifiable on

[0,T_{\min}]
alone, without knowledge of future dwell-times.

Proof.

Extension.

Define
\bar{P}:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
by

\bar{P}(\tau):=P_{s}(\tau)
for
\tau\in[0,T_{\min}]
and

\bar{P}(\tau):=P_{s}(T_{\min})
for
\tau>T_{\min}
.

Verification of GRE conditions on
\bar{P}
.

Flow,
\tau\in(0,T_{\min})
:

Condition (i) gives
\mathcal{C}_{Z}(\tau,\bar{P}(\tau))=0
.

Flow,
\tau>T_{\min}
:

\bar{P}
is constant and the data are time-invariant by
(
43
), so
\dot{\bar{P}}=0
and

\mathcal{C}_{Z}(\tau,\bar{P}(\tau))=\mathcal{F}_{\infty}^{*}(P_{s}(T_{\min}))+ZT_{\min}=\mathcal{C}_{Z}^{0}(P_{s}(T_{\min}))\preceq 0

by condition (iii).

Jump at every
t_{k}
with
\Delta_{k}\geq T_{\min}
:

\bar{P}(\Delta_{k}^{-})=P_{s}(T_{\min})
, so

\mathcal{D}_{Z}(\bar{P}(0^{+}),\bar{P}(\Delta_{k}^{-}))=\mathcal{D}_{Z}(P_{s}(0^{+}),P_{s}(T_{\min}))\succeq 0

and the Schur complement equals zero (condition (ii)).
With
K_{k}\in\mathcal{K}_{d}(P_{s}(0^{+}),P_{s}(T_{\min}))
, the corresponding
inner product
\langle\mathcal{D}_{Z}(P_{s}(0^{+}),P_{s}(T_{\min})),\,X^{\star}(t_{k}^{-})\rangle=0

at every jump.

Cost bound.

Let
\{t_{k}\}_{k=0}^{N_{T}}
denote the jump times in
[t_{0},t_{0}+T]
and
define the absolute-time dual variable

P(t):=\bar{P}(t-t_{k})
for
t\in(t_{k},t_{k+1}]
.
Applying the key identity (Proposition
3.1
) on

[t_{0},t_{0}+T]
with
P
and
Z_{T}=0
gives

\displaystyle J_{T}(X^{\star},X_{1}^{0})

\displaystyle=\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle-\underbrace{\langle P(t_{0}+T),\,EX^{\star}(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\rangle}_{\geq\,0}

\displaystyle\quad+\underbrace{\sum_{k=0}^{N_{T}}\int_{0}^{\Delta_{k}}\langle\mathcal{C}_{Z}(\tau,\bar{P}(\tau)),\,X^{\star}(t_{k}+\tau)\rangle\,\,\mathrm{d}\tau}_{=:\,I_{f}}

\displaystyle\quad+\underbrace{\sum_{k=1}^{N_{T}}\langle\mathcal{D}_{Z}(\bar{P}(0^{+}),\bar{P}(\Delta_{k}^{-})),\,X^{\star}(t_{k}^{-})\rangle}_{=\,0},

where on each inter-jump interval
(t_{k},t_{k+1})
the change of
variables
\tau=t-t_{k}
has been applied to the flow integral.
