The jump sum equals zero because the gain

K_{k}\in\mathcal{K}_{d}(P_{s}(0^{+}),P_{s}(T_{\min}))
saturates the
Schur equality (condition (ii)).

The flow integral on each interval splits at
\tau=T_{\min}
:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\Delta_{k}}\langle\mathcal{C}_{Z}(\tau,\bar{P}(\tau)),\,X^{\star}(t_{k}+\tau)\rangle\,\,\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\int_{0}^{T_{\min}}\langle\mathcal{C}_{Z}(\tau,P_{s}(\tau)),\,X^{\star}(t_{k}+\tau)\rangle\,\,\mathrm{d}\tau}_{=\,0\text{(Schur decomp., (i))}}

\displaystyle\quad+\underbrace{\int_{T_{\min}}^{\Delta_{k}}\langle\mathcal{C}_{Z}^{0}(P_{s}(T_{\min})),\,X^{\star}(t_{k}+\tau)\rangle\,\,\mathrm{d}\tau}_{\leq\,0\text{ by~(iii)}},

since
\langle\mathcal{C}_{Z}(\tau,P_{s}(\tau)),\,X^{\star}(t_{k}+\tau)\rangle=0
on

(0,T_{\min})
along the optimal trajectory (the Schur decomposition of
Lemma
2.5
with the gain in
\mathcal{K}_{c}
, as in
Theorem
3.5
), by (i), and
for
\tau>T_{\min}
we have
\bar{P}(\tau)=P_{s}(T_{\min})
constant
and the data time-invariant, so

\mathcal{C}_{Z}(\tau,\bar{P}(\tau))=\mathcal{C}_{Z}^{0}(P_{s}(T_{\min}))\preceq 0

by (iii) and
X^{\star}\succeq 0
.
Summing over
k
,
I_{f}\leq 0
.
The terminal correction
-\langle P(t_{0}+T),\,EX^{\star}(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\rangle\leq 0

since
P(t_{0}+T)\succeq 0
and
X^{\star}(t_{0}+T)\succeq 0
.
Combining:
J_{T}(X^{\star},X_{1}^{0})\leq\langle P_{s}(0),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
for all
T>0
,
and letting
T\to\infty
gives
J_{\infty}^{*}(X_{1}^{0})\leq\langle P_{s}(0),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
.
The policy is causal since conditions (i)–(iii) depend only on

\tau\leq T_{\min}
.
∎

Remark 3.20
.

Condition (iii) is an LMI in
P_{s}(T_{\min})
and is the
matrix-valued analogue of the
Geromel–Colaneri stability
condition

[
16
]
: the frozen flow dynamics (with no
Riccati correction) must dissipate at least as much as the cost
accumulates at
\tau=T_{\min}
. In the stability limit

Z=Z_{k}=0
, condition (iii) reduces to

\mathcal{F}^{*}(P_{s}(T_{\min}))\preceq 0
, which is the standard
Lyapunov condition for asymptotic stability of the
flow
[
16
]
.

Remark 3.21
.

The dual variable
\bar{P}
constructed in the proof of
Theorem
3.19
is in general not
C^{1}
at the breakpoints

\tau=T_{\min}
: just before,
\dot{\bar{P}}(T_{\min}^{-})=\dot{P}_{s}(T_{\min}^{-})

is determined by the forward Riccati ODE and is typically nonzero,
whereas just after,
\dot{\bar{P}}(T_{\min}^{+})=0
since
\bar{P}
is
constant on
(T_{\min},\Delta_{k}]
. This is not a difficulty, for two
reasons. First, the I-GRE only requires
P
to be piecewise-
C^{1}
,
which permits a finite set of isolated discontinuities of

\dot{P}
; the key identity (Proposition
3.1
) and the
verification arguments in the proof use
\dot{P}
only inside
integrals and are unaffected by a measure-zero set of discontinuities,
so the piecewise-
C^{1}
condition can in fact be relaxed to absolute
continuity of
P
throughout. Second, when a
C^{1}
solution is
desired, the construction of
[
44
,
43
]
can be
adapted to produce a smoothed
\tilde{P}
satisfying the same
GRE conditions, by interpolating in a small neighbourhood of
each breakpoint without affecting the cost bound.

Range dwell-time

For a range dwell-time (RDT) constraint

T_{\min}\leq\Delta_{k}\leq T_{\max}
, the policy must be valid for
any dwell-time in this interval.

Theorem 3.22

(Sufficient causal condition: RDT)
.

Let
0<T_{\min}\leq T_{\max}
. Suppose there exists

P_{s}:[0,T_{\max}]\to\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
satisfying:

(i)

Flow GRE on
[0,T_{\max}]
:

\mathcal{C}_{Z}(\tau,P_{s}(\tau))\succeq 0
and

\mathcal{C}_{Z}\,\widetilde{/}\,\allowbreak(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}=0

for
\tau\in(0,T_{\max})
.

(ii)

Jump GRE for all
\tau\in[T_{\min},T_{\max}]
:

\mathcal{D}_{Z}(k,P_{s}(0^{+}),P_{s}(\tau^{-}))\succeq 0
and

\mathcal{D}_{Z}\,\widetilde{/}\,\allowbreak(\mathcal{D}_{Z})_{3}=0
.

Then for any dwell-time sequence with
\Delta_{k}\in[T_{\min},T_{\max}]
,
the policy

K(\tau)\in\mathcal{K}_{c}(\tau,P_{s}(\tau))
on flow and

K_{k}\in\mathcal{K}_{d}(k,P_{s}(0^{+}),P_{s}(\Delta_{k-1}^{-}))
at each jump

t_{k}
is causal and achieves
J_{T}(X^{\star},X_{1}^{0})\leq\langle P_{s}(0),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle

for all
T>0
. In particular,

J_{\infty}^{*}(X_{1}^{0})\leq\langle P_{s}(0),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
. The gain
K_{k}
depends
only on the just-observed dwell-time
\Delta_{k-1}
, which is
available at
t_{k}
, so the policy is fully causal.

Proof.

Let
\{t_{k}\}_{k=0}^{N_{T}}
denote the jump times in
[t_{0},t_{0}+T]
,
with
t_{0}
the initial time and
\Delta_{k}:=t_{k+1}-t_{k}\in[T_{\min},T_{\max}]
.
Define the absolute-time dual variable

P(t):=P_{s}(t-t_{k})\quad\text{for }t\in(t_{k},t_{k+1}],\;k=0,\ldots,N_{T},

so that on each inter-jump interval the dual is the timer-domain
function
P_{s}
evaluated at the timer
\tau=t-t_{k}\in(0,\Delta_{k}]
.
By construction
P(t_{k}^{+})=P_{s}(0^{+})
and
P(t_{k}^{-})=P_{s}(\Delta_{k-1}^{-})

for
k\geq 1
.

Verification of GRE conditions on
P
.

Flow,
t\in(t_{k},t_{k+1})
:

Setting
\tau=t-t_{k}\in(0,\Delta_{k})\subset(0,T_{\max})
,
condition (i) gives
\mathcal{C}_{Z}(\tau,P_{s}(\tau))=0
, hence

\mathcal{C}_{Z}(t,P(t))=0
on the entire flow interval.

Jump at
t_{k}
(
k\geq 1
):

The pre-jump dwell-time is
\Delta_{k-1}\in[T_{\min},T_{\max}]
, so

\tau:=\Delta_{k-1}\in[T_{\min},T_{\max}]
satisfies the range in
condition (ii). Thus

\mathcal{D}_{Z}(k,P(t_{k}^{+}),P(t_{k}^{-}))=\mathcal{D}_{Z}(k,P_{s}(0^{+}),P_{s}(\Delta_{k-1}^{-}))\succeq 0

with Schur complement equal to zero.

Cost bound.

Apply the key identity (Proposition
3.1
) on

[t_{0},t_{0}+T]
with
P
and
Z_{T}=0
:

\displaystyle J_{T}(X^{\star},X_{1}^{0})

\displaystyle=\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle-\underbrace{\langle P(t_{0}+T),\,EX^{\star}(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\rangle}_{\geq\,0}

\displaystyle\quad+\underbrace{\sum_{k=0}^{N_{T}}\int_{0}^{\Delta_{k}}\langle\mathcal{C}_{Z}(\tau,P_{s}(\tau)),\,X^{\star}(t_{k}+\tau)\rangle\,\,\mathrm{d}t}_{=:\,I_{f}}

\displaystyle\quad+\underbrace{\sum_{k=1}^{N_{T}}\langle\mathcal{D}_{Z}(k,P_{s}(0^{+}),P_{s}(\Delta_{k-1}^{-})),\,X^{\star}(t_{k}^{-})\rangle}_{=:\,I_{j}},

where on each inter-jump interval
(t_{k},t_{k+1})
the change of
variables
\tau=t-t_{k}
has been applied to the flow integral, and
the last interval is truncated at
t_{0}+T
if it ends mid-flow.

Flow integral.

By condition (i),
\mathcal{C}_{Z}(\tau,P_{s}(\tau))=0
pointwise on

(0,T_{\max})
, so each integrand vanishes and
I_{f}=0
.

Jump sum.

With the gain
K_{k}\in\mathcal{K}_{d}(k,P_{s}(0^{+}),P_{s}(\Delta_{k-1}^{-}))

applied at
t_{k}
, the Schur equality of condition (ii) gives

\langle\mathcal{D}_{Z}(k,P_{s}(0^{+}),P_{s}(\Delta_{k-1}^{-})),\,X^{\star}(t_{k}^{-})\rangle=0

at every jump, so
I_{j}=0
.

Terminal correction.

Since
P(t_{0}+T)=P_{s}(t_{0}+T-t_{N_{T}})\succeq 0
and
X^{\star}(t_{0}+T)\succeq 0
,
the terminal term
-\langle P(t_{0}+T),\,EX^{\star}(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\rangle\leq 0
.

Combining,
J_{T}(X^{\star},X_{1}^{0})\leq\langle P_{s}(0),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
for all
T>0
.
Under (H1),
\|EX^{\star}(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\|_{*}\to 0
as
T\to\infty
;
combined with
P(t_{0}+T)
bounded (
P_{s}
is continuous on the compact
set
[0,T_{\max}]
), the terminal correction vanishes in the limit
and
J_{\infty}^{*}(X_{1}^{0})\leq\langle P_{s}(0),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
.

Causality.

The flow gain
K(\tau)
depends on the current timer
\tau\in[0,\Delta_{k}]
,
known continuously during the inter-jump flow.
The jump gain
K_{k}
at
t_{k}
depends on
\Delta_{k-1}
, which is

t_{k}-t_{k-1}
, available at
t_{k}
. Thus the entire policy is causal.

No Geromel–Colaneri extension is needed: the flow GRE is satisfied
with equality everywhere on
[0,T_{\max}]
, and the jump GRE is
satisfied for every admissible
\Delta_{k-1}\in[T_{\min},T_{\max}]
.
∎

3.2.6
Continuous- and discrete-time corollaries (infinite horizon)

Corollary 3.23

(Continuous-time: infinite horizon)
.

Set
N_{T}=0
. Theorem
3.12
gives the unique
bounded
C^{1}
solution
P_{\infty}
of the continuous-time infinite-horizon GRE
(
\mathcal{C}_{Z}(t,P(t))\succeq 0
,

\mathcal{C}_{Z}\,\widetilde{/}\,\allowbreak(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}=0
, for all
t\geq t_{0}
),
with
J_{\infty}^{\star}(X_{1}^{0})=\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
,
the monotone limit
P_{\infty}=\lim_{T}P_{T}
, and dual

J_{\infty}^{\star}=\sup_{P}\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
s.t.
\mathcal{C}_{Z}(t,P(t))\succeq 0
,
P
bounded
[
52
]
.

Corollary 3.24

(Discrete-time: infinite horizon)
.

Apply Theorem
3.12
with
\mathcal{F}=0
,
Z=0
,

t_{k}=k+k_{0}
. The infinite-horizon DT
optimal cost is
J_{\infty}^{N*}(X_{1}^{0})=\langle P_{\infty}(k_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
,
where
P_{\infty}
satisfies the algebraic DT-GRE and equals the
non-decreasing limit of the
N
-step solutions.

3.3
Long-term average cost

The finite- and infinite-horizon problems of
Sections
3.1
–
3.2
are well-posed only when the
total accumulated cost on
[t_{0},t_{0}+T]
remains bounded as
T\to\infty
.
This rules out a class of problems in which the cost grows

linearly
in
T
, with a non-zero rate, due to a persistent
exogenous input acting on the state block. In the
stochastic interpretation
X_{1}=\mathbb{E}[xx^{*}]
, this corresponds to
a continuous-time process noise covariance and a jump-noise
covariance that keep injecting energy into the second-moment, so that

X_{1}(t)
cannot decay to zero and the integrated cost diverges; in a
deterministic reading, the same effect is produced by a fixed
Hermitian disturbance acting on the state block. In both cases, the
quantity of interest is the
long-term average cost rate

\rho^{\star}=\lim_{T\to\infty}J_{T}^{\star}/T
.

This subsection develops the corresponding theory. The dynamics
are augmented by a Hermitian forcing on the state block
(Section
3.3.1
), the key identity acquires a
linear-in-
P
forcing term
(Section
3.3.2
), and the average cost is
characterised by the same I-GRE as in Section
3.2
with

\rho^{\star}
given by a linear pairing of the I-GRE solution

P_{\infty}
with the forcing data. Finite-horizon and
infinite-horizon results parallel
Sections
3.1
–
3.2
, with sufficient
(Theorem
3.29
), necessary and existence
(Theorem
3.31
), equivalence
(Corollary
3.33
), and dual-LMI
(Theorem
3.34
) statements, plus CT and DT corollaries
(Section
3.3.7
). A central feature is that the

Riccati equation itself is unchanged
: only the post-processing
of
P_{\infty}
with the forcing data changes.

3.3.1
Forced dynamics, cost, and assumptions

We extend Definition
2.6
by allowing an additive
Hermitian forcing on the state block.

Definition 3.25

(Forced impulsive system)
.

The
forced
impulsive matrix-valued system is

\displaystyle E\dot{X}(t)E^{*}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}(t,X(t))+\mathcal{W}(t),\quad t\neq t_{k},\quad t\geq t_{0},

(46a)

\displaystyle EX(t_{k}^{+})E^{*}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{J}(k,X(t_{k}^{-}))+\mathcal{W}_{k},\quad k\geq 1,

(46b)

with
EX(t_{0})E^{*}=X_{1}^{0}\in\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
, where
\mathcal{F}
and

\mathcal{J}
are as in Definition
2.6
, and the

flow forcing

\mathcal{W}:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
is piecewise
continuous and uniformly bounded, and the
jump forcing

\mathcal{W}_{k}\in\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
is a bounded sequence.

The forcing is taken positive semidefinite to preserve the cone

\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
for
X_{1}
, and to match the stochastic interpretation where

\mathcal{W}=BB^{*}
is a process-noise covariance and
\mathcal{W}_{k}

is the covariance of the jump noise.

The cost is the integrated linear cost (
33
) without
terminal penalty:

J_{T}(X,X_{1}^{0}):=\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\langle Z(t),\,X(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\sum_{k:\,t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T}\langle Z_{k},\,X(t_{k}^{-})\rangle,

(47)

with
Z(t)\succeq 0
,
Z_{k}\succeq 0
. The associated optimal
finite-horizon cost is

J_{T}^{\star}(X_{1}^{0}):=\inf_{X(\cdot)}J_{T}(X,X_{1}^{0})

subject
to (
46
) and
EX(t_{0})E^{*}=X_{1}^{0}
. The decision variable is the
full trajectory
X(\cdot)\in\mathbb{H}^{n+m}_{\succeq 0}
satisfying (
46
)
with
EX(t_{0})E^{*}=X_{1}^{0}
, and the free blocks
X_{2},X_{3}
are chosen
exactly as in Section
3.1
.

Definition 3.26

(Long-term average cost)
.

The
long-term average cost
(or
average-cost rate
)
[
8
,
9
]
is

\rho^{\star}(X_{1}^{0}):=\limsup_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}\,J_{T}^{\star}(X_{1}^{0}).

(48)

In contrast to the infinite-horizon problem of
Section
3.2
, the persistent forcing

\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W}_{k}
generally makes the integrated cost

J_{\infty}^{\star}=\lim_{T\to\infty}J_{T}^{\star}
infinite, so (
33
)
is no longer well-posed and the rate (
48
) is the
appropriate object of study.

Assumptions.

The well-posedness of the average-cost problem requires three
assumptions on the homogeneous dynamics and the forcing data.

(A1) Uniform stabilizability of the homogeneous
dynamics.

There exist a feedback
K_{s}\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times n}
, constants

\beta\geq 1
,
\rho\in(0,1)
and
\alpha>0
, such that the homogeneous closed-loop
trajectory
X_{s}^{\mathrm{h}}
of (
5
) (i.e.,

\mathcal{W}=0
,
\mathcal{W}_{k}=0
) with feedback

X_{2}^{s}=K_{s}X_{1}^{s}
,
X_{3}^{s}=K_{s}X_{1}^{s}K_{s}^{*}
satisfies

\|EX_{s}^{\mathrm{h}}(t)E^{*}\|_{*}\leq\beta e^{-\alpha(t-s)}\rho^{\kappa(t,s)}\|EX_{s}^{\mathrm{h}}(s)E^{*}\|_{*}
for all
t\geq s\geq t_{0}
.

(A2) Uniform coercivity.

EZ(t)E^{*}\succeq\delta I
uniformly for some
\delta>0
.

(A3) Uniform boundedness of the operators
and supply rates.

Z,Z_{k},\mathcal{F}(t,\cdot),\mathcal{J}(k,\cdot)
are uniformly bounded;

(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}(t,P(t))\succeq 0
and
(\mathcal{D}_{Z})_{3}\succeq 0

for all
P\succeq 0
;
{(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}}^{\dagger}
and

{(\mathcal{D}_{Z})_{3}}^{\dagger}
are uniformly bounded on bounded sets of

P
; and
\mathcal{W}(t)
,
\mathcal{W}_{k}
are uniformly bounded.

(A4) Jump-rate regularity.

The inter-jump times
\Delta_{k}=t_{k+1}-t_{k}
are bounded both above
and below by positive constants: there exist
0<\Delta_{\min}\leq\Delta_{\max}<\infty
such that
\Delta_{k}\in[\Delta_{\min},\Delta_{\max}]
for all
k\geq 0
. In particular, the jump rate

\rho_{\mathrm{rate}}:=\lim_{T\to\infty}T^{-1}\#\{k:t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T\}

exists and is finite, with
\Delta_{\max}^{-1}\leq\rho_{\mathrm{rate}}\leq\Delta_{\min}^{-1}
.

Assumption (A1) is the same uniform stabilizability condition as in
(H1) except that the boundedness
J_{\infty}(X_{s},X_{1}^{0})\leq C_{s}\|X_{1}^{0}\|_{*}

of the cumulative cost is dropped, since this can no longer hold under
persistent forcing; only the exponential bound on

\|EX_{s}^{\mathrm{h}}E^{*}\|_{*}
for the homogeneous system is retained.
Assumptions (A2), (A3) coincide with (H2), (H3) except for the
additional uniform boundedness of the forcing data. Together,
(A1)–(A3) imply that the infinite-horizon I-GRE of
Section
3.2
, which only sees the homogeneous part of the
dynamics, has a unique bounded solution

P_{\infty}:[t_{0},\infty)\to\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
by
Theorem
3.12
.

Assumption (A4) plays a different role from (A1)–(A3).
Assumption (A1), through the dual decay rate

\beta e^{-\alpha(t-s)}\rho^{\kappa(t,s)}
, already ensures that the
homogeneous closed-loop trajectory decays robustly under both small
and large inter-jump times: small
\Delta_{k}
are absorbed by the
geometric factor
\rho^{\kappa(t,s)}
(each jump contributing decay),
while large
\Delta_{k}
are absorbed by the continuous factor

e^{-\alpha(t-s)}
(the flow being independently stable).
Consequently, state stability and the summability of the jump-cost
contribution
\sum_{k}\|Z_{k}\|\,\|X(t_{k}^{-})\|
along stabilising
trajectories follow from (A1) alone, without any direct constraint
on the
\Delta_{k}
. The role of (A4) is purely to make the

time-averaged
long-term cost well-defined as a genuine limit
rather than a
\limsup
: the existence of the jump rate

\rho_{\mathrm{rate}}
ensures that the discrete jump contribution
admits a long-run average, and bounded
\Delta_{k}
ensures that the
supply rates
\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W}_{k}
are properly time-averaged.
Theorems giving only an upper bound on the long-term cost invoke
only (A1)–(A3); theorems giving exact long-term cost equalities
additionally require (A4). The condition can be weakened to an
average dwell-time bound at the cost of replacing the limit by a

\limsup
.

3.3.2
Forced key identity

Compared with Proposition
3.1
, the differentiation
of
\langle P(t),\,EX(t)E^{*}\rangle
along the forced
flow (
46a
) produces an additional term

\langle P(t),\,\mathcal{W}(t)\rangle
, and the jump
relation (
46b
) contributes an additional
term
\langle P(t_{k}^{+}),\,\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle
at each
t_{k}
. Both terms are

linear in
P
and
decoupled from the trajectory
X
:
they appear as additive corrections that do not interact with the

\mathcal{C}_{Z},\mathcal{D}_{Z}
blocks.

Proposition 3.27

(Forced impulsive key identity)
.

For any piecewise-
C^{1}

P:[t_{0},t_{0}+T]\to\mathbb{H}^{n}
and any trajectory

X(\cdot)\in\mathbb{H}^{n+m}_{\succeq 0}
satisfying (
46
) with

EX(t_{0})E^{*}=X_{1}^{0}
,

\displaystyle J_{T}(X,X_{1}^{0})

\displaystyle=\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle-\langle P(t_{0}+T),\,EX(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\rangle

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\langle\mathcal{C}_{Z}(t,P(t)),\,X(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\sum_{k=1}^{N_{T}}\langle\mathcal{D}_{Z}(k,P(t_{k}^{+}),P(t_{k}^{-})),\,X(t_{k}^{-})\rangle

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\langle P(t),\,\mathcal{W}(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\sum_{k=1}^{N_{T}}\langle P(t_{k}^{+}),\,\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle.

(49)

Proof.

On the flow interval
(t_{k-1},t_{k})
, differentiating

\langle P(t),\,EX(t)E^{*}\rangle
along (
46a
):

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\langle P(t),\,EX(t)E^{*}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\dot{P}(t),\,EX(t)E^{*}\rangle+\langle P(t),\,E\dot{X}(t)E^{*}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle E^{*}\dot{P}(t)E,\,X(t)\rangle+\langle P(t),\,\mathcal{F}(t,X(t))+\mathcal{W}(t)\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle E^{*}\dot{P}(t)E+\mathcal{F}^{*}(t,P(t)),\,X(t)\rangle+\langle P(t),\,\mathcal{W}(t)\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\mathcal{C}_{Z}(t,P(t))-Z(t),\,X(t)\rangle+\langle P(t),\,\mathcal{W}(t)\rangle,

using the adjoint identity

\langle P,\,\mathcal{F}(t,X)\rangle=\langle\mathcal{F}^{*}(t,P),\,X\rangle
and the
definition (
10
) of
\mathcal{C}_{Z}
.
Integrating on
(t_{k-1},t_{k})
and rearranging:

\displaystyle\langle P(t_{k}^{-}),\,EX(t_{k}^{-})E^{*}\rangle-\langle P(t_{k-1}^{+}),\,EX(t_{k-1}^{+})E^{*}\rangle

\displaystyle\quad=-\int_{t_{k-1}}^{t_{k}}\langle Z(t),\,X(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{t_{k-1}}^{t_{k}}\langle\mathcal{C}_{Z}(t,P(t)),\,X(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{t_{k-1}}^{t_{k}}\langle P(t),\,\mathcal{W}(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t.

(50)

At each jump
t_{k}
, using (
46b
) and the
adjoint relation,

\displaystyle\langle P(t_{k}^{+}),\,EX(t_{k}^{+})E^{*}\rangle-\langle P(t_{k}^{-}),\,EX(t_{k}^{-})E^{*}\rangle

\displaystyle\quad=\langle P(t_{k}^{+}),\,\mathcal{J}(k,X(t_{k}^{-}))+\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle-\langle P(t_{k}^{-}),\,EX(t_{k}^{-})E^{*}\rangle

\displaystyle\quad=\langle\mathcal{J}^{*}(k,P(t_{k}^{+}))-E^{*}P(t_{k}^{-})E,\,X(t_{k}^{-})\rangle+\langle P(t_{k}^{+}),\,\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle

\displaystyle\quad=\langle\mathcal{D}_{Z}(k,P(t_{k}^{+}),P(t_{k}^{-})),\,X(t_{k}^{-})\rangle-\langle Z_{k},\,X(t_{k}^{-})\rangle+\langle P(t_{k}^{+}),\,\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle,

using (
11
). Hence

\displaystyle\langle P(t_{k}^{+}),\,EX(t_{k}^{+})E^{*}\rangle-\langle P(t_{k}^{-}),\,EX(t_{k}^{-})E^{*}\rangle=\langle\mathcal{D}_{Z},\,X(t_{k}^{-})\rangle-\langle Z_{k},\,X(t_{k}^{-})\rangle+\langle P(t_{k}^{+}),\,\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle.

(51)

Summing (
3.3.2
) over all
N_{T}+1
flow intervals
and (
51
) over all
N_{T}
jumps, the

\langle P,\,EXE^{*}\rangle
terms telescope to

\langle P(t_{0}+T),\,EX(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\rangle-\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
on the left.
Substituting and rearranging gives (
49
).
∎

The forced key identity (
49
) is the source of
every average-cost result below. The first four terms are exactly
those of (
12
) with
Z_{T}=0
, and the last two are
the linear forcing pairings that produce
\rho^{\star}
.

3.3.3
Finite-horizon average cost

The finite-horizon
time-averaged cost
is

\bar{J}_{T}(X,X_{1}^{0}):=J_{T}(X,X_{1}^{0})/T
, with optimum

\bar{J}_{T}^{\star}(X_{1}^{0}):=J_{T}^{\star}(X_{1}^{0})/T
. By a routine
adaptation of Theorem
3.5
to the forced
dynamics,
J_{T}^{\star}(X_{1}^{0})
is attained by the same I-GRE feedback

K(t)\in\mathcal{K}_{c}(t,P_{T}(t))
and
K_{k}\in\mathcal{K}_{d}
as in the
homogeneous case, where
P_{T}
is the unique piecewise-
C^{1}
solution
of the finite-horizon I-GRE (Definition
3.2
) with
terminal condition
P_{T}(t_{0}+T)=0
. This is because the forcing

\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W}_{k}
enters the forced key
identity (
49
) only through the last two terms,
which are independent of the free blocks
(X_{2},X_{3})
; the
minimization over
(X_{2},X_{3})
is therefore identical to that of
Section
3.1
and is achieved by the homogeneous I-GRE
feedback. The value of the optimum and its time-averaged version are
given by the following theorem.

Theorem 3.28

(Finite-horizon average cost decomposition)
.

Let
T>0
and suppose the finite-horizon I-GRE
(Definition
3.2
) has a solution
P_{T}\succeq 0
with

P_{T}(t_{0}+T)=0
. Then the optimal cost

J_{T}^{\star}(X_{1}^{0})
of (
47
) under (
46
)
decomposes as

J_{T}^{\star}(X_{1}^{0})=\langle P_{T}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle+\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\langle P_{T}(t),\,\mathcal{W}(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\sum_{k:\,t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T}\langle P_{T}(t_{k}^{+}),\,\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle,

(52)

and the finite-horizon time-averaged optimum is

\bar{J}_{T}^{\star}(X_{1}^{0})=\frac{1}{T}\langle P_{T}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle+\frac{1}{T}\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\langle P_{T}(t),\,\mathcal{W}(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{1}{T}\!\!\sum_{k:\,t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T}\!\!\langle P_{T}(t_{k}^{+}),\,\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle.

(53)

The optimum is attained at
X^{\star}
with

K(t)\in\mathcal{K}_{c}(t,P_{T}(t))
on flow and

K_{k}\in\mathcal{K}_{d}(k,P_{T}(t_{k}^{+}),P_{T}(t_{k}^{-}))
at each jump.

Proof.

Apply the forced key identity (
49
) with

P=P_{T}
and
P_{T}(t_{0}+T)=0
:

\displaystyle J_{T}(X,X_{1}^{0})

\displaystyle=\langle P_{T}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle-\underbrace{\langle P_{T}(t_{0}+T),\,EX(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\rangle}_{=\,0}

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\langle\mathcal{C}_{Z}(t,P_{T}(t)),\,X(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\sum_{k=1}^{N_{T}}\langle\mathcal{D}_{Z}(k,P_{T}(t_{k}^{+}),P_{T}(t_{k}^{-})),\,X(t_{k}^{-})\rangle

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\langle P_{T}(t),\,\mathcal{W}(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\sum_{k=1}^{N_{T}}\langle P_{T}(t_{k}^{+}),\,\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle.

Since
P_{T}
solves the I-GRE,
\mathcal{C}_{Z}(t,P_{T}(t))\succeq 0
with

\mathcal{C}_{Z}\,\widetilde{/}\,(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}=0
, and the analogous
condition for
\mathcal{D}_{Z}
at each jump (Definition
3.2
).
The Schur inner-product decomposition (Lemma
2.5
)
applied as in the proof of Theorem
3.5
gives

\langle\mathcal{C}_{Z}(t,P_{T}(t)),\,X(t)\rangle\geq 0
and

\langle\mathcal{D}_{Z},\,X(t_{k}^{-})\rangle\geq 0
for every admissible
X
, with
equality whenever
K(t)\in\mathcal{K}_{c}(t,P_{T}(t))
on flow and

K_{k}\in\mathcal{K}_{d}
at each jump. Since the last two terms in the
forced key identity are independent of the free blocks

(X_{2},X_{3})
, the infimum over admissible
X
is attained at any such
choice and equals (
52
).
Dividing by
T
gives (
53
).
∎

The decomposition (
53
) separates

\bar{J}_{T}^{\star}
into a
transient term

\langle P_{T}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle/T
that depends on the initial condition and
vanishes as
T\to\infty
, and
forcing terms
whose

T\to\infty
limit gives the long-term average cost rate; this is the
content of the next subsection.

3.3.4
Infinite-horizon average cost: sufficient condition

The following theorem is the central sufficient condition of this
subsection. It says that any bounded solution of the

homogeneous
infinite-horizon I-GRE
(Section
3.2
) determines the long-term average cost rate
through a linear pairing with the forcing data. No new
Riccati-type equation is required.

Theorem 3.29

(Sufficient condition, infinite-horizon average cost)
.

Suppose there exists a bounded piecewise-
C^{1}
function

P_{\infty}:[t_{0},\infty)\to\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
satisfying the infinite-horizon
I-GRE:
\mathcal{C}_{Z}(t,P_{\infty})\succeq 0
,

\mathcal{C}_{Z}\,\widetilde{/}\,(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}=0
for a.e.
t
, and the
corresponding jump conditions at every
t_{k}
. Assume further that
the limit

\rho^{\star}:=\lim_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}\!\left[\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\langle P_{\infty}(t),\,\mathcal{W}(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\!\!\sum_{k:\,t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T}\!\!\langle P_{\infty}(t_{k}^{+}),\,\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle\right]

(54)

exists. Then
:

(a)

The long-term average cost satisfies

\rho^{\star}(X_{1}^{0})=\rho^{\star}
for every
X_{1}^{0}\in\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
,
i.e.,
\rho^{\star}
is independent of the initial condition.

(b)

The optimum is attained at any trajectory
X^{\star}
with

K_{\infty}(t)\in\mathcal{K}_{c}(t,P_{\infty}(t))
on flow and

K_{k}\in\mathcal{K}_{d}(k,P_{\infty}(t_{k}^{+}),P_{\infty}(t_{k}^{-}))
at each
jump.

(c)

P_{\infty}
is the unique bounded solution of the infinite-horizon
I-GRE.

Proof.

Lower bound.

Let
X
be any admissible trajectory of (
46
).
Apply the forced key identity (
49
) on

[t_{0},t_{0}+T]
with
P=P_{\infty}
. Since
P_{\infty}
solves the
infinite-horizon I-GRE,

\mathcal{C}_{Z}(t,P_{\infty}(t))\succeq 0
and

\mathcal{D}_{Z}(k,P_{\infty}(t_{k}^{+}),P_{\infty}(t_{k}^{-}))\succeq 0
.
The Schur inner-product decomposition gives

\langle\mathcal{C}_{Z}(t,P_{\infty}(t)),\,X(t)\rangle\geq 0
a.e. and

\langle\mathcal{D}_{Z},\,X(t_{k}^{-})\rangle\geq 0
for every
k
, so

J_{T}(X,X_{1}^{0})\geq\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle-\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}+T),\,EX(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\rangle+R_{T}(P_{\infty}),

(55)

where

R_{T}(P_{\infty}):=\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\langle P_{\infty}(t),\,\mathcal{W}(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\sum_{k:\,t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T}\langle P_{\infty}(t_{k}^{+}),\,\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle.

The boundary term
\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}+T),\,EX(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\rangle\geq 0
since

P_{\infty}\succeq 0
and
EX(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\succeq 0
; dropping it can only
decrease the right side, so (
55
) also holds with
that term omitted. Dividing (
55
) by
T
, and
noting that
\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle/T\to 0
as
T\to\infty
(both
factors bounded), and that

\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}+T),\,EX(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\rangle/T\to 0
provided

\|EX(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\|_{*}
is bounded (which holds along any admissible
trajectory under (A1)–(A3) by Lemma
3.30
below):

\limsup_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}J_{T}(X,X_{1}^{0})\geq\lim_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}R_{T}(P_{\infty})=\rho^{\star}.

Taking the infimum over admissible
X
yields

\rho^{\star}(X_{1}^{0})\geq\rho^{\star}
.

Attainment.

Let
X^{\star}
be the trajectory with gains

K_{\infty}(t)\in\mathcal{K}_{c}(t,P_{\infty}(t))
on flow and

K_{k}\in\mathcal{K}_{d}(k,P_{\infty}(t_{k}^{+}),P_{\infty}(t_{k}^{-}))
at each jump.
By the attainment argument of Theorem
3.5
(which
relies only on the I-GRE and not on the homogeneous form of the
dynamics),

\langle\mathcal{C}_{Z}(t,P_{\infty}(t)),\,X^{\star}(t)\rangle=0
for a.e.
t
and

\langle\mathcal{D}_{Z}(k,P_{\infty}(t_{k}^{+}),P_{\infty}(t_{k}^{-})),\,X^{\star}(t_{k}^{-})\rangle=0

for every
k
. Substituting into (
49
) gives

J_{T}(X^{\star},X_{1}^{0})=\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle-\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}+T),\,EX^{\star}(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\rangle+R_{T}(P_{\infty}).

(56)

Dividing by
T
and letting
T\to\infty
, the first and second terms
vanish (boundedness and
1/T
), and
R_{T}(P_{\infty})/T\to\rho^{\star}

by hypothesis. Hence

\limsup_{T\to\infty}J_{T}(X^{\star},X_{1}^{0})/T=\rho^{\star}
, so the
infimum is achieved and

\rho^{\star}(X_{1}^{0})=\rho^{\star}
, proving (
a
)
and (
b
).

Uniqueness of
P_{\infty}
.

If
\tilde{P}
is another bounded solution of the infinite-horizon
I-GRE, the lower-bound and attainment arguments above give

\rho^{\star}=\lim_{T\to\infty}R_{T}(\tilde{P})/T
as well. But

\tilde{P}\equiv P_{\infty}
already by Theorem
3.11

(the homogeneous I-GRE has a unique bounded solution). This
proves (
c
).
∎

The bounded-trajectory claim used in the lower bound is recorded as
the following lemma, whose proof is essentially the standard
input-to-state argument for linear systems and is included for
completeness.

Lemma 3.30

(Bounded second moment under forcing)
.

Under (A1) and (A3), for any admissible trajectory
X(\cdot)

of (
46
) with stabilizing feedback
K_{s}
from
(A1), there exists a constant
C>0
(depending only on
K_{s}
,

\beta,\alpha,\rho
from (A1), and the bounds in (A3), and

\|X_{1}^{0}\|_{*}
) such that
\|EX(t)E^{*}\|_{*}\leq C
for all
t\geq t_{0}
.

Proof.

Let
X^{\mathrm{h}}
be the homogeneous trajectory with the same
feedback and initial condition. By the variation-of-constants
formula for flow-jump linear evolutions on
\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
, the forced trajectory satisfies

\displaystyle\|EX(t)E^{*}\|_{*}

\displaystyle\leq\beta e^{-\alpha(t-t_{0})}\rho^{\kappa(t,t_{0})}\|X_{1}^{0}\|_{*}

\displaystyle\quad+\beta\!\int_{t_{0}}^{t}e^{-\alpha(t-s)}\rho^{\kappa(t,s)}\|\mathcal{W}(s)\|_{*}\,ds+\beta\!\!\sum_{t_{k}\in[t_{0},t]}\!\!e^{-\alpha(t-t_{k})}\rho^{\kappa(t,t_{k})}\|\mathcal{W}_{k}\|_{*},

where the
\rho^{\kappa}
factors come from the dual decay in (A1).
The first term is bounded by
\beta\|X_{1}^{0}\|_{*}
.
The second term is bounded by

\beta(\sup_{s}\|\mathcal{W}(s)\|_{*})\int_{t_{0}}^{t}e^{-\alpha(t-s)}\,ds\leq\beta\alpha^{-1}\sup_{s}\|\mathcal{W}(s)\|_{*}
.
For the third term, enumerate the jumps in
[t_{0},t]
in reverse
chronological order so that
\kappa(t,t_{k})=N_{t}-k
where
N_{t}
is the
total number of jumps in
[t_{0},t]
; using
e^{-\alpha(t-t_{k})}\leq 1
,

\beta\!\!\sum_{t_{k}\in[t_{0},t]}\!\!e^{-\alpha(t-t_{k})}\rho^{\kappa(t,t_{k})}\|\mathcal{W}_{k}\|_{*}\leq\beta(\sup_{k}\|\mathcal{W}_{k}\|_{*})\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\rho^{j}=\beta(1-\rho)^{-1}\sup_{k}\|\mathcal{W}_{k}\|_{*}.

Combining:

\|EX(t)E^{*}\|_{*}\leq\underbrace{\beta\|X_{1}^{0}\|_{*}+\beta\alpha^{-1}\sup_{s}\|\mathcal{W}(s)\|_{*}+\beta(1-\rho)^{-1}\sup_{k}\|\mathcal{W}_{k}\|_{*}}_{=:\,C},

which is uniform in
t
and independent of the inter-jump
times.
∎

Theorem 3.31

(Necessary condition and existence,
infinite-horizon average cost)
.

Under (A1)–(A3), there exists a unique bounded piecewise-
C^{1}

solution
P_{\infty}:[t_{0},\infty)\to\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
,

\alpha_{1}I\preceq P_{\infty}\preceq\alpha_{2}I
, of the
infinite-horizon I-GRE, and

\rho^{\star}(X_{1}^{0})\;=\;\limsup_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}\!\left[\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\!\langle P_{\infty}(t),\,\mathcal{W}(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\!\!\sum_{k:\,t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T}\!\!\langle P_{\infty}(t_{k}^{+}),\,\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle\right]

(57)

independently of
X_{1}^{0}
. The right-hand side is finite whenever the
jump count grows at most linearly, i.e.,

\limsup_{T\to\infty}T^{-1}\#\{k:t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T\}<\infty
.
The optimum is attained at the closed-loop trajectory
X^{\star}
with
gains
K_{\infty}(t)\in\mathcal{K}_{c}(t,P_{\infty}(t))
on flow and

K_{k}\in\mathcal{K}_{d}(k,P_{\infty}(t_{k}^{+}),P_{\infty}(t_{k}^{-}))
at each jump.

Proof.

Existence and bounds on
P_{\infty}
.

By Theorem
3.12
applied to the homogeneous system,
(A1)–(A3) provide a unique bounded piecewise-
C^{1}
solution
P_{\infty}

with
\alpha_{1}I\preceq P_{\infty}\preceq\alpha_{2}I
.

Key identity with
P_{\infty}
.

For any admissible trajectory
X
of (
46
), the
forced key identity (
49
) with
P=P_{\infty}
on

[t_{0},t_{0}+T]
reads

J_{T}(X,X_{1}^{0})=\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle-\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}+T),\,EX(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\rangle+\!\!\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\!\!\langle\mathcal{C}_{Z}(t,P_{\infty}),\,X\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t

+\sum_{k:\,t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T}\langle\mathcal{D}_{Z},\,X(t_{k}^{-})\rangle+R_{T}(P_{\infty}),

with

R_{T}(P_{\infty}):=\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\!\langle P_{\infty}(t),\,\mathcal{W}(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\sum_{k:\,t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T}\!\langle P_{\infty}(t_{k}^{+}),\,\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle
. Since

P_{\infty}
solves the infinite-horizon I-GRE,

\mathcal{C}_{Z}(t,P_{\infty})\succeq 0
and
\mathcal{D}_{Z}\succeq 0
, so
Lemma
2.5
gives
\langle\mathcal{C}_{Z},\,X(t)\rangle\geq 0
a.e. and
\langle\mathcal{D}_{Z},\,X(t_{k}^{-})\rangle\geq 0
, with equality along the
closed-loop trajectory
X^{\star}
. Hence

J_{T}(X,X_{1}^{0})\;\geq\;\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle-\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}+T),\,EX(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\rangle+R_{T}(P_{\infty}),

(58)

with equality at
X^{\star}
.

Boundary term is
O(1)
in
T
.

Lemma
3.30
bounds

\|EX(t)E^{*}\|\leq C_{X}<\infty
uniformly in
t
along any admissible
trajectory with the stabilising feedback of (A1); its proof uses the
geometric factor
\rho^{j}
from the dual decay in (A1) and never
invokes a bound on
\Delta_{k}
. Consequently

\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}+T),\,EX(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\rangle\leq\alpha_{2}C_{X}
uniformly in
T
.

Conclusion.

Dividing (
58
) by
T
and taking

\limsup_{T\to\infty}
, both

T^{-1}\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
and

T^{-1}\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}+T),\,EX(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\rangle
vanish, leaving

\limsup_{T}T^{-1}J_{T}(X,X_{1}^{0})\geq\limsup_{T}T^{-1}R_{T}(P_{\infty})
. Taking
the infimum over admissible
X
on the left gives

\rho^{\star}(X_{1}^{0})\geq\limsup_{T}T^{-1}R_{T}(P_{\infty})
. The closed-loop
trajectory
X^{\star}
saturates (
58
), so the
reverse inequality holds and

\rho^{\star}(X_{1}^{0})=\limsup_{T}T^{-1}R_{T}(P_{\infty})
, which
is (
57
). The right-hand side does not depend on

X_{1}^{0}
.

Finiteness.

|R_{T}(P_{\infty})|\leq\alpha_{2}\bigl(T\sup_{t}\|\mathcal{W}(t)\|+\#\{k:t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T\}\cdot\sup_{k}\|\mathcal{W}_{k}\|\bigr)
, so

T^{-1}|R_{T}(P_{\infty})|
is bounded above whenever

\limsup_{T}T^{-1}\#\{k:t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T\}<\infty
.
∎

Remark 3.32
.

Formula (
57
) characterises
\rho^{\star}
as a

\limsup
regardless of any inter-jump regularity. A genuine
\lim
is
recovered under additional structural assumptions on

(P_{\infty},\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W}_{k})
, the cleanest case being

T_{p}
-periodicity of the data (Theorem
3.35
)
where both contributions of
R_{T}(P_{\infty})/T
converge by genuine
periodic averaging. In the LTI specialisation
(Corollary
3.40
),
P_{\infty}
is constant and the formula
collapses to
\rho^{\star}=\mathrm{trace}(P_{\infty}\mathcal{W})
, again a true limit.
This is the matrix-valued analogue of the classical ergodic property
of LQG control with stable closed-loop dynamics: the long-run cost
rate depends only on the closed-loop steady state, not on the initial
condition.

3.3.5
Equivalence and dual LMI (infinite horizon)

The equivalence statement and dual LMI for the average-cost
problem are direct consequences of
Theorems
3.29
and
3.31
.

Corollary 3.33

(Equivalence, infinite-horizon average cost)
.

The infinite-horizon average-cost
problem (
48
) under (
46
) is
well-posed with
\rho^{\star}(X_{1}^{0})=\rho^{\star}
given
by (
54
) if and only if the homogeneous
infinite-horizon I-GRE has a bounded piecewise-
C^{1}
solution

P_{\infty}\succeq 0
. The solution is unique.

Proof.

Sufficiency: Theorem
3.29
.
Necessity: Theorem
3.31
.
Uniqueness: Theorem
3.29
(
c
).
∎

The LMI characterisation expresses
\rho^{\star}
as the supremum
of a linear-in-
P
functional over the LMI feasibility set of
Theorem
3.14
. Define the

average-forcing pairing

\Phi(P):=\limsup_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}\!\left[\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\langle P(t),\,\mathcal{W}(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\!\!\sum_{k:\,t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T}\!\!\langle P(t_{k}^{+}),\,\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle\right],

(59)

which is well-defined and finite for any bounded
P
under (A3).
The functional
\Phi:\mathbb{H}^{n}\text{-valued bounded functions}\to\mathbb{R}
is
linear and order-preserving: if
P\preceq P^{\prime}
pointwise then

\Phi(P)\leq\Phi(P^{\prime})
(since
\mathcal{W}(t)\succeq 0
and

\mathcal{W}_{k}\succeq 0
).

Theorem 3.34

(Dual LMI, infinite-horizon average cost)
.

Under (A1)–(A4),

\rho^{\star}=\sup_{P}\;\Phi(P)\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\mathcal{C}_{Z}(t,P(t))\succeq 0,\;\mathcal{D}_{Z}(k,P(t_{k}^{+}),P(t_{k}^{-}))\succeq 0,\;P\text{ bounded};

(60)

the supremum is attained at
P_{\infty}
.

Proof.

Step 1: every feasible
P
is a lower bound for

\rho^{\star}
.

Let
P
be any feasible function for (
60
). By the
proof of Theorem
3.14
,

P(t)\preceq P_{\infty}(t)
for all
t
. Hence

\Phi(P)\leq\Phi(P_{\infty})
by the order-preserving property, so

\sup_{P}\Phi(P)\leq\Phi(P_{\infty})
.

A direct argument that does not invoke the comparison

P\preceq P_{\infty}
proceeds as follows. For any admissible
trajectory
X
of (
46
) and any feasible
P
,
the forced key identity (
49
) combined with
the LMI constraints

\mathcal{C}_{Z}(t,P(t))\succeq 0
and
\mathcal{D}_{Z}\succeq 0
gives

J_{T}(X,X_{1}^{0})\geq\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle-\langle P(t_{0}+T),\,EX(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\rangle+R_{T}(P),

where
R_{T}(P):=\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\langle P(t),\,\mathcal{W}(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\sum_{k:\,t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T}\langle P(t_{k}^{+}),\,\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle
.
Dividing by
T
, the boundary terms vanish (boundedness of
P
and
of
EXE^{*}
by Lemma
3.30
, divided by
T
), so

\liminf_{T\to\infty}J_{T}(X,X_{1}^{0})/T\geq\Phi(P)
.
Taking the infimum over
X
on the left, then

\rho^{\star}\geq\Phi(P)
. Since this holds for every feasible
P
,

\rho^{\star}\geq\sup_{P}\Phi(P)
.

Step 2:
P_{\infty}
is feasible and attains the
bound.

P_{\infty}
satisfies the LMI constraints by the I-GRE
(Lemma
3.4
) and is bounded. By
Theorem
3.31
,
\Phi(P_{\infty})=\rho^{\star}
, so

\sup_{P}\Phi(P)\geq\Phi(P_{\infty})=\rho^{\star}
.

Combining Steps 1 and 2:
\rho^{\star}=\sup_{P}\Phi(P)
, attained at

P_{\infty}
.
∎

3.3.6
Dwell-time conditions

The infinite-horizon I-GRE solution
P_{\infty}
used in
Sections
3.3.4
–
3.3.5
is non-causal
in the sense of Section
3.2.5
: its value at time
t

depends on the future jump times. We now combine the average-cost
formula (
54
) with the causal dwell-time policies
of Section
3.2.5
(periodic, MDT, RDT) to obtain causal
characterisations and bounds for
\rho^{\star}
. In all three cases,
the timer-dependent storage function
P_{s}(\tau)
used in
Section
3.2.5
provides the average forcing pairing
through the same construction as in
Section
3.3.4
, with the difference that
P_{s}(\tau)

is now defined on a bounded timer interval and the time-average of
the forcing data must be re-expressed in timer-dependent form. The
structural assumptions on the system operators

\mathcal{F},\mathcal{J},Z,Z_{k}
in the three scenarios are inherited
verbatim from Section
3.2.5
; the only additions are the
corresponding assumptions on the forcing data

\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W}_{k}
.

Periodic impulses.

Adopt the periodicity hypothesis of Theorem
3.17
:

\Delta_{k}\equiv T_{p}
,
\mathcal{F}(\cdot,X)
and
Z(\cdot)
are

T_{p}
-periodic in
t
, and
\mathcal{J}(k,\cdot)
,
Z_{k}
are
independent of
k
. For the forcing data, assume in addition that

\mathcal{W}(\cdot)
is
T_{p}
-periodic in
t
and that

\mathcal{W}_{k}
is independent of
k
, i.e.,

\mathcal{W}_{k}\equiv\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{jp}}
. Under these
hypotheses we regard
\mathcal{W}
as a function of the timer

\tau\in[0,T_{p})
.

Theorem 3.35

(Causal LTAC under periodic impulses)
.

Under
(A1)–(A3)
and the periodicity assumption above

(
note that
(A4)
is automatic from

\Delta_{k}\equiv T_{p}
)
, the long-term average
cost (
48
) is given by the closed-form expression

\rho^{\star}=\frac{1}{T_{p}}\!\left[\int_{0}^{T_{p}}\langle P_{\infty}(\tau),\,\mathcal{W}(\tau)\rangle\,d\tau+\langle P_{\infty}(0^{+}),\,\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{jp}}\rangle\right],

(61)

where
P_{\infty}:[0,T_{p}]\to\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
is the unique
T_{p}
-periodic
piecewise-
C^{1}
solution of the periodic I-GRE (
40
),
attained by the causal policy

K(\tau)\in\mathcal{K}_{c}(\tau,P_{\infty}(\tau))
on flow and

K_{k}\in\mathcal{K}_{d}(P_{\infty}(0^{+}),P_{\infty}(T_{p}^{-}))
at each jump.

Proof.

By Theorem
3.17
applied to the homogeneous dynamics,
the periodic I-GRE has a unique
T_{p}
-periodic solution
P_{\infty}
.
Its
T_{p}
-periodic extension to
[t_{0},\infty)
coincides with the
infinite-horizon dual variable of Theorem
3.31
, so
formula (
57
) applies and the
\limsup
becomes a
true
\lim
by genuine periodic averaging:
the flow integral over
[t_{0},t_{0}+T]
contains
\lfloor T/T_{p}\rfloor

complete periods plus a boundary remainder, so its time-average
converges to

T_{p}^{-1}\int_{0}^{T_{p}}\langle P_{\infty}(\tau),\,\mathcal{W}(\tau)\rangle\,d\tau
;
the jump sum contains
\lfloor T/T_{p}\rfloor
jumps each contributing

\langle P_{\infty}(0^{+}),\,\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{jp}}\rangle
, with time-average

T_{p}^{-1}\langle P_{\infty}(0^{+}),\,\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{jp}}\rangle
. Combining
yields (
61
). The causal policy is
the same as in Theorem
3.17
and is the optimal LTAC
policy by Theorem
3.29
(
b
).
∎

Minimum dwell-time.

Adopt the timer-dependence hypothesis of Theorem
3.19
:

\Delta_{k}\geq T_{\min}>0
, the flow data depend only on the timer

\tau=t-t_{k}
, and the time-invariance condition
(
43
) holds for
\mathcal{F},Z,\mathcal{J},Z_{k}
.
For the forcing data, assume in addition that

\mathcal{W}
depends only on the timer, with

\mathcal{W}(\tau)\equiv\mathcal{W}_{\infty}\;\;\text{for }\tau\geq T_{\min},\qquad\mathcal{W}_{k}\equiv\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{jp}}\;\;\text{for all }k.

(62)

Theorem 3.36

(Causal LTAC upper bound under MDT)
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
3.19

and (
62
), suppose moreover that the
jump rate
\bar{\nu}:=\limsup_{T\to\infty}T^{-1}\#\{k:t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T\}

is finite
(
so
\bar{\nu}\leq 1/T_{\min}
)
and is in
fact a
\lim
. Then the causal policy of Theorem
3.19

achieves a long-term average cost rate bounded above by

\rho^{\star}\leq\bar{\nu}\!\int_{0}^{T_{\min}}\!\langle P_{s}(\tau),\,\mathcal{W}(\tau)\rangle\,d\tau+(1-\bar{\nu}T_{\min})\langle P_{s}(T_{\min}),\,\mathcal{W}_{\infty}\rangle+\bar{\nu}\langle P_{s}(0^{+}),\,\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{jp}}\rangle,

(63)

where
P_{s}:[0,T_{\min}]\to\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
is as in Theorem
3.19
.
The condition is verifiable on
[0,T_{\min}]
alone.

Proof.

Extend
P_{s}
to
\bar{P}:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
by

\bar{P}(\tau)=P_{s}(\tau)
for
\tau\leq T_{\min}
and

\bar{P}(\tau)=P_{s}(T_{\min})
for
\tau>T_{\min}
as in the proof of
Theorem
3.19
. Apply the forced key
identity (
49
) on
[t_{0},t_{0}+T]
with
P=\bar{P}

and
Z_{T}=0
, denoting by
\tau(t)
the timer value at time
t
:

\displaystyle J_{T}(X^{\star},X_{1}^{0})

\displaystyle=\langle\bar{P}(0^{+}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle-\langle\bar{P}(\tau(t_{0}+T)),\,EX^{\star}(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\rangle

\displaystyle\quad+\underbrace{\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\!\langle\mathcal{C}_{Z}(\bar{P}),\,X^{\star}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t}_{\leq\,0\text{ by Thm~\ref{th:OC:MDT}~(iii)}}+\underbrace{\sum_{k:\,t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T}\!\!\langle\mathcal{D}_{Z},\,X^{\star}(t_{k}^{-})\rangle}_{=\,0\text{ at the optimal policy}}

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\!\langle\bar{P}(\tau(t)),\,\mathcal{W}(\tau(t))\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\!\!\sum_{k:\,t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T}\!\!\langle\bar{P}(0^{+}),\,\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{jp}}\rangle.

Dividing by
T
and taking
T\to\infty
, the boundary terms vanish.
The time-average of
\langle\bar{P}(\tau(t)),\,\mathcal{W}(\tau(t))\rangle

decomposes according to the timer distribution: under MDT, in each
inter-jump interval of length
\Delta_{k}\geq T_{\min}
the timer spends
time
T_{\min}
on
[0,T_{\min}]
(where
\bar{P}=P_{s}
and

\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{W}(\tau)
vary) and time
\Delta_{k}-T_{\min}
on
the frozen region (where
\bar{P}=P_{s}(T_{\min})
and

\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{W}_{\infty}
). With average jump rate
\bar{\nu}
,
the fraction of time spent on
[0,T_{\min}]
is
\bar{\nu}T_{\min}
and
on the frozen region is
1-\bar{\nu}T_{\min}
,
giving (
63
).
∎

Remark 3.37
.

For the constant dwell-time
\Delta_{k}\equiv T_{\min}
, the jump rate

\bar{\nu}=1/T_{\min}
and the frozen contribution

(1-\bar{\nu}T_{\min})\langle P_{s}(T_{\min}),\,\mathcal{W}_{\infty}\rangle
vanishes,
so (
63
) recovers the periodic
formula (
61
) (with constant data and

T_{p}=T_{\min}
). For larger jump rates (
\bar{\nu}<1/T_{\min}
), the
frozen contribution accounts for the time spent past the minimum
dwell-time.

Range dwell-time.

Adopt the timer-dependence hypothesis of Theorem
3.22
:

\Delta_{k}\in[T_{\min},T_{\max}]
and the flow and jump data depend
only on the timer
\tau
as in (
43
), now over
the extended interval
[0,T_{\max}]
(so that

\mathcal{F}(\tau,\cdot),Z(\tau)
are defined for

\tau\in[0,T_{\max}]
without imposing constancy beyond
T_{\min}
,
and
\mathcal{J},Z_{k}
are
k
-independent). For the forcing
data, assume similarly that
\mathcal{W}
depends only on the timer

\tau\in[0,T_{\max}]
and that
\mathcal{W}_{k}
is
k
-independent:

\mathcal{W}_{k}\equiv\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{jp}}
.

Theorem 3.38

(Causal LTAC under RDT)
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
3.22
and the
timer-dependence hypotheses above, suppose moreover that the
empirical occupation measure of the inter-jump sequence

\{\Delta_{k}\}
converges weakly to a probability measure
\mu
on

[T_{\min},T_{\max}]
as
T\to\infty
, and that the jump rate

\bar{\nu}=(\int_{[T_{\min},T_{\max}]}\Delta\,\mu(d\Delta))^{-1}

is finite. Then the causal policy of Theorem
3.22

achieves a long-term average cost rate bounded above by

\rho^{\star}\leq\bar{\nu}\!\int_{T_{\min}}^{T_{\max}}\!\!\biggl(\int_{0}^{\Delta}\!\langle P_{s}(\tau),\,\mathcal{W}(\tau)\rangle\,d\tau\biggr)\mu(d\Delta)+\bar{\nu}\langle P_{s}(0^{+}),\,\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{jp}}\rangle,

(64)

where
P_{s}:[0,T_{\max}]\to\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
is as in Theorem
3.22
.

Proof.

Apply the forced key identity with
P=P_{s}
over
[t_{0},t_{0}+T]
. The

\mathcal{C}_{Z}
and
\mathcal{D}_{Z}
contributions vanish identically
at the optimal policy by the conditions of Theorem
3.22
,
so

\displaystyle J_{T}(X^{\star},X_{1}^{0})

\displaystyle=\langle P_{s}(0^{+}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle-\langle P_{s}(\tau(t_{0}+T)),\,EX^{\star}(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\rangle

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\!\langle P_{s}(\tau(t)),\,\mathcal{W}(\tau(t))\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\!\!\sum_{k:\,t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T}\!\!\langle P_{s}(0^{+}),\,\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{jp}}\rangle.

Dividing by
T
and letting
T\to\infty
, the boundary terms vanish,
the jump-sum time-average is

\bar{\nu}\langle P_{s}(0^{+}),\,\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{jp}}\rangle
, and the flow
time-average is obtained from the timer occupation measure: in an
inter-jump interval of length
\Delta_{k}
, the timer traverses

[0,\Delta_{k}]
, contributing

\int_{0}^{\Delta_{k}}\langle P_{s}(\tau),\,\mathcal{W}(\tau)\rangle\,d\tau
to the
integral; dividing by
T
and using

\bar{\nu}=(\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\Delta])^{-1}
gives the
\mu
-integral
in (
64
).
∎

Remark 3.39
.

When
\mu
is concentrated at a single point
\Delta_{k}\equiv T_{p}

with
T_{\min}\leq T_{p}\leq T_{\max}
, the
bound (
64
) becomes equality and reduces
to (
61
). For unknown
\mu
, the
worst case over admissible distributions on
[T_{\min},T_{\max}]

gives a robust upper bound on
\rho^{\star}
.

3.3.7
Continuous- and discrete-time corollaries

The continuous-time and discrete-time specialisations follow by
setting
N_{T}=0
or
\mathcal{F}=0
as in
Sections
3.1
–
3.2
.

Corollary 3.40

(Continuous-time average cost)
.

Set
N_{T}=0
(no jumps), so
\mathcal{W}_{k}
is irrelevant. Under the
continuous-time forced
flow
E\dot{X}E^{*}=\mathcal{F}(t,X(t))+\mathcal{W}(t)
and (A1)–(A3),

\rho^{\star}=\lim_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\langle P_{\infty}(t),\,\mathcal{W}(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t,

(65)

where
P_{\infty}
is the unique bounded
C^{1}
solution of the
continuous-time infinite-horizon GRE (Corollary
3.23
).
The dual LMI is

\rho^{\star}=\sup_{P}\;\lim_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}\!\!\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\!\!\langle P(t),\,\mathcal{W}(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\mathcal{C}_{Z}(t,P(t))\succeq 0,\;P\text{ bounded}.

In the LTI case (
\mathcal{F},Z,\mathcal{W}
time-invariant),
P_{\infty}

is constant and (
65
) reduces to

\rho^{\star}=\langle P_{\infty},\,\mathcal{W}\rangle=\mathrm{trace}(P_{\infty}\mathcal{W}),

(66)

recovering the classical LQG steady-state cost formula
[
52
]

when
\mathcal{W}=BB^{*}
is a process-noise covariance.

Corollary 3.41

(Discrete-time average cost)
.

Apply with
\mathcal{F}=0
,
Z=0
on flow,
t_{k}=k_{0}+k
,

\mathcal{J}=\mathcal{D}
,
\mathcal{W}=0
on flow. Under the
discrete-time forced jump

EX(k+1)E^{*}=\mathcal{D}(k,X(k))+\mathcal{W}_{k}
and the
discrete-time analogues of (A1)–(A3),

\rho^{\star}=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+N-1}\langle P_{\infty}(k+1),\,\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle,

(67)

where
P_{\infty}
satisfies the algebraic DT-GRE
(Corollary
3.24
). In the LTI case
(
\mathcal{D},Z_{k},\mathcal{W}_{k}
constant),
P_{\infty}
is constant
and (
67
) reduces to

\rho^{\star}=\langle P_{\infty},\,\mathcal{W}\rangle=\mathrm{trace}(P_{\infty}\mathcal{W})
,
again recovering the classical steady-state cost
formula
[
52
]
.

4
Finite-Time Optimal Steering

The optimal control theory of Section
3
is unconstrained
at the terminal time: the state
EX(T)E^{*}
is shaped by a terminal
cost
\langle Z_{T},\,EX(T)E^{*}\rangle
but not fixed to any prescribed value.
This section addresses the complementary problem of enforcing a

hard equality constraint
on the terminal state. Given initial
and terminal targets
X_{1}^{0},X_{1}^{f}\in\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
, we seek the
minimum-cost admissible trajectory that steers the constrained block

EX(T)E^{*}
from
X_{1}^{0}
to
X_{1}^{f}
at a fixed final time
T
.

The framework is fully general:
X
is any Hermitian matrix-valued
state satisfying (
5
), and
X_{1}^{f}
need not arise
from any stochastic model. When
X
represents the second-order
moment of a linear stochastic system, the problem specialises to
the covariance steering setting of
[
20
,
21
,
38
,
3
]
, but the analysis here applies without
that interpretation, covering deterministic matrix-valued dynamics
and indefinite costs as in the rest of the paper.

The problem is solved via Lagrangian duality
(Section
4.2
): a multiplier
M\succeq 0
for the
terminal constraint converts it into an unconstrained optimal control problem
(Section
3.1
), with the optimal multiplier
M^{\star}

identified by the steering condition

EX^{\star}(T;M^{\star})E^{*}=X_{1}^{f}
(Theorem
4.3
).
Feasibility and the reachable set are characterized in
Section
4.3
.
The problem structure is made explicit via a two-point boundary
value problem (TPBVP) coupling the backward I-GRE with a forward
moment equation for
\Sigma(t):=EX^{\star}(t)E^{*}

(Section
4.4
), and a forward-backward algorithm
for computing
M^{\star}
is described in Section
4.5
.

4.1
Problem formulation

The linear-cost optimal control of Section
3
leaves the
terminal state
EX(T)E^{*}
free (beyond the terminal cost
Z_{T}
).
The present problem adds a
hard equality constraint
: given a
target
X_{1}^{f}\in\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
, find the minimum-cost trajectory that
steers the constrained block from
X_{1}^{0}
to
X_{1}^{f}
exactly.

Definition 4.1

(Finite-time optimal steering problem)
.

The
optimal steering problem
is

J^{\star}(X_{1}^{0},X_{1}^{f}):=\inf_{X(\cdot)\in\mathbb{H}^{n+m}_{\succeq 0}}\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\langle Z(t),\,X(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\sum_{k=1}^{N_{T}}\langle Z_{k},\,EX(t_{k}^{-})E^{*}\rangle

(68)

subject to (
5
),
EX(t_{0})E^{*}=X_{1}^{0}
, and the

terminal constraint

EX(t_{0}+T)E^{*}=X_{1}^{f}.

(69)

The problem is
feasible
if the infimum is finite.
The
reachable set
is

\mathcal{R}_{T}(X_{1}^{0}):=\{EX(T)E^{*}:\text{admissible }X,\;EX(t_{0})E^{*}=X_{1}^{0}\}
.

Remark 4.2
.

The running cost has no terminal term (
Z_{T}=0
) since the terminal
state is fixed by the constraint. The case
Z_{k}=0
corresponds to
the minimum-energy optimal steering studied (as a covariance problem)
in
[
38
,
3
]
; retaining general
Z,Z_{k}
gives
a
minimum linear-cost
extension natural to the
matrix-valued impulsive framework.

4.2
Lagrangian duality and the steering condition

The terminal constraint (
69
) is relaxed via a
multiplier
M\in\mathbb{H}^{n}
. Define the Lagrangian

L(X,M):=J_{T}(X,X_{1}^{0})+\langle M,\,EX(T)E^{*}-X_{1}^{f}\rangle.

(70)

Since
\langle M,\,EX(T)E^{*}\rangle
equals the terminal cost
term
\langle Z_{T},\,EX(T)E^{*}\rangle
with
Z_{T}=M
, the infimum of
L
over

X
for a given
M
is the unconstrained optimal cost
(Theorem
3.5
) with terminal weight
Z_{T}=M
, minus
the constant
\langle M,\,X_{1}^{f}\rangle
. The dual function is therefore

d(M):=\inf_{X}L(X,M)=\langle P(t_{0};M),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle-\langle M,\,X_{1}^{f}\rangle,

(71)

where
P(\cdot;M)
is the I-GRE solution with
P(T;M)=M
.
For
M\not\succeq 0
the I-GRE has no solution and
d(M)=-\infty
,
so the domain of
d
is
\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
. The dual problem is

\sup_{M\succeq 0}\,d(M)=\sup_{M\succeq 0}\bigl[\langle P(t_{0};M),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle-\langle M,\,X_{1}^{f}\rangle\bigr],

(72)

which is a concave maximization over the PSD cone:
d
is concave in

M
as the infimum of affine functions (for each fixed
X
,

L(X,M)
is affine in
M
).

Theorem 4.3

(Lagrangian duality for optimal steering)
.

Suppose the optimal steering problem (
68
) is
feasible. Then:

(a)

(Strong duality.)

J^{\star}(X_{1}^{0},X_{1}^{f})=\sup_{M\succeq 0}d(M)
;
there is no duality gap.

(b)

(Steering condition.)

The supremum is attained at
M^{\star}\succeq 0
, which satisfies

EX^{\star}(T;M^{\star})E^{*}=X_{1}^{f},

(73)

where
X^{\star}(\cdot;M)
denotes the optimal trajectory of the
unconstrained optimal control problem with terminal cost
Z_{T}=M
.

(c)

(Optimal cost.)

J^{\star}(X_{1}^{0},X_{1}^{f})=\langle P(t_{0};M^{\star}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle-\langle M^{\star},\,X_{1}^{f}\rangle
,
attained by the optimal control policy with gains

K(\cdot)\in\mathcal{K}_{c}(\cdot,P(\cdot;M^{\star}))
on flow and

K_{k}\in\mathcal{K}_{d}(k,\cdot)
at each jump.

Proof.

(a) Strong duality.

The primal (
68
) is a linear program over the self-dual
cone
\mathbb{H}^{n+m}_{\succeq 0}
: the objective is linear in
X
, the flow and jump
dynamics are affine, and the terminal constraint (
69
)
is affine. Under feasibility and the standard constraint qualification
for conic programs—namely the existence of a strictly feasible
(relative-interior) admissible trajectory for the conic constraint

X\succeq 0
, the affine equality constraints requiring no
qualification—strong duality holds and the dual optimum is
attained
[
13
]
.

(b) Steering condition.

By (a), the dual maximum is attained at some
M^{\star}
(since
d

is concave, upper semicontinuous on
\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
, and coercive under
the feasibility assumption).

To identify
M^{\star}
, compute
\nabla_{M}d(M)
. For a perturbation

\delta M
, the envelope theorem for parameterised minimization gives

\frac{d}{d\epsilon}d(M+\epsilon\,\delta M)\bigg|_{\epsilon=0}=\frac{\partial L}{\partial M}(X^{\star}(\cdot;M),M)\cdot\delta M=\langle\delta M,\,EX^{\star}(T;M)E^{*}-X_{1}^{f}\rangle,

(74)

where the
\partial L/\partial X
contribution vanishes by the
optimality of
X^{\star}(\cdot;M)
. Hence

\nabla_{M}d(M)=EX^{\star}(T;M)E^{*}-X_{1}^{f}
.
The stationarity condition
\nabla_{M}d(M^{\star})=0

gives (
73
).

(c) Optimal cost.

By the steering condition,
X^{\star}(\cdot;M^{\star})
satisfies the
terminal constraint
EX^{\star}(T;M^{\star})E^{*}=X_{1}^{f}
. Its running
cost (without terminal weight) is

J_{T}(X^{\star}(\cdot;M^{\star}))=\langle P(t_{0};M^{\star}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle-\langle M^{\star},\,EX^{\star}(T;M^{\star})E^{*}\rangle=\langle P(t_{0};M^{\star}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle-\langle M^{\star},\,X_{1}^{f}\rangle
,
where the first equality uses the key identity with
P(\cdot;M^{\star})

(the I-GRE gives zero integrand and zero jump summands along the
optimal trajectory, and
P(T;M^{\star})=M^{\star}
eliminates the
terminal correction), and the second uses (
73
).
By strong duality, this equals
J^{\star}
.
∎

4.3
Feasibility and the reachable set

The optimal steering problem is feasible iff
X_{1}^{f}
belongs to
the reachable set
\mathcal{R}_{T}(X_{1}^{0})
. The following gives a
computable characterization.

Proposition 4.4

(Reachable set)
.

Define the
free-evolution state

X_{1}^{\text{free}}(T):=EX^{\text{free}}(T)E^{*}
where

X^{\text{free}}
is the solution of (
5
)
with free variables
X_{2}=X_{3}=0
. Then:

(a)

\mathcal{R}_{T}(X_{1}^{0})
is a convex subset of
\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}

containing the free evolution
X_{1}^{\mathrm{free}}(T)
, and

X_{1}^{f}\in\mathcal{R}_{T}(X_{1}^{0})
if and only if the
terminal-constraint steering problem is feasible, i.e. there
exists an admissible
X\succeq 0
of (
5
) with

EX(t_{0})E^{*}=X_{1}^{0}
and
EX(T)E^{*}=X_{1}^{f}
.

(b)

Let
\mathcal{L}_{T}:(X_{2}(\cdot),X_{3}(\cdot))\mapsto EX(T)E^{*}-X_{1}^{\mathrm{free}}(T)
denote the (linear) input-to-terminal
map sending the admissible free-block trajectory to the induced
perturbation of the terminal state block, and define the

\mathbb{H}^{n}
-valued reachability Gram

\mathcal{G}_{T}:=\int_{t_{0}}^{T}\Phi_{T}(t)\,E_{\bot}^{*}E_{\bot}\,\Phi_{T}(t)^{*}\,\mathrm{d}t+\sum_{k:\,t_{k}\leq T}\Psi_{T}(t_{k})\,E_{\bot}^{*}E_{\bot}\,\Psi_{T}(t_{k})^{*}\;\in\mathbb{H}^{n},

(75)

where
\Phi_{T}(t),\Psi_{T}(t_{k}):\mathbb{C}^{n+m}\to\mathbb{C}^{n}
are the
flow/jump sensitivity operators of
EX(T)E^{*}
with respect to the
free blocks at
t
resp.
t_{k}
. If
\mathcal{G}_{T}\succ 0

(controllability of the terminal block from the free inputs), then

\mathcal{L}_{T}
is surjective onto
\mathbb{H}^{n}
, and every
X_{1}^{f}
in a
neighborhood of
X_{1}^{\mathrm{free}}(T)
within
\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
is
reachable.

Proof.

Part (a): convexity of
\mathcal{R}_{T}(X_{1}^{0})
follows from
linearity of the dynamics and convexity of the admissible set

\{X\succeq 0\}
(a convex combination of two admissible trajectories is
admissible and its terminal block is the corresponding convex
combination);
X_{1}^{\mathrm{free}}(T)
is reached by the admissible
choice
X_{2}=X_{3}=0
. The “if and only if” is the definition of
reachability as feasibility of the terminal constraint. We do

not
claim the order bound
X_{1}^{f}\succeq X_{1}^{\mathrm{free}}(T)
:
nonzero free blocks may either increase or decrease
EX(T)E^{*}
depending
on
\mathcal{F},\mathcal{J}
, so no monotone characterization holds in
general.
Part (b):
\mathcal{G}_{T}\succ 0
is exactly the condition that the linear
map
\mathcal{L}_{T}
has full range
\mathbb{H}^{n}
(its image is

\operatorname{Im}\mathcal{G}_{T}
); surjectivity and the inverse/implicit
function theorem then give local reachability around

X_{1}^{\mathrm{free}}(T)
. A complete global description of

\mathcal{R}_{T}(X_{1}^{0})
(e.g. as the convex set swept by the dual map

M\mapsto EX^{\star}(T;M)E^{*}
, with explicit sensitivity operators

\Phi_{T},\Psi_{T}
) is not pursued here: the steering results below do not
use this proposition, proceeding instead through the Lagrangian dual of
Theorem
4.3
, which characterises reachability of a given

X_{1}^{f}
by feasibility of the steering condition (
73
).
∎

4.4
Forward moment equation and two-point boundary value problem

Theorem
4.3
identifies the optimal terminal weight

M^{\star}
via the steering condition (
73
) but leaves
its computation implicit. We now make the structure explicit by deriving
the
forward moment equation
for the optimal state block

\Sigma(t):=EX^{\star}(t;M^{\star})E^{*}
and combining it with the backward
I-GRE into a
two-point boundary value problem
(TPBVP).

4.4.1
Closed-loop operators

With optimal gain

K(t;P):=-(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}(t,P(t))^{{\dagger}}\,(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{2}^{*}(t,P(t))

(the pseudo-inverse
{\dagger}
handling the singular case), the closed-loop
trajectory satisfies
X_{2}^{\star}=K\Sigma
and
X_{3}^{\star}=K\Sigma K^{*}
.
Define the
closed-loop flow operator
and

closed-loop jump operator

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{cl}}(t,P(t))[\Sigma]

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{F}\!\left(t,\begin{bmatrix}\Sigma&\Sigma K^{*}\\
K\Sigma&K\Sigma K^{*}\end{bmatrix}\right),

(76)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{cl},k}(P(t_{k}^{+}),P(t_{k}^{-}))[\Sigma]

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{J}\!\left(k,\begin{bmatrix}\Sigma&\Sigma K_{k}^{*}\\
K_{k}\Sigma&K_{k}\Sigma K_{k}^{*}\end{bmatrix}\right),

(77)

where
K_{k}:=-(\mathcal{D}_{Z})_{3}(k,P(t_{k}^{+}),P(t_{k}^{-}))^{{\dagger}}\,(\mathcal{D}_{Z})_{2}^{*}(k,P(t_{k}^{+}),P(t_{k}^{-}))
.
Both operators are
linear
in
\Sigma
(by linearity of

\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{J}
in
X
) and depend on
P
through the
optimal gain. The second-moment
\Sigma(t;M):=EX^{\star}(t;M)E^{*}

satisfies the
forward moment equation

\dot{\Sigma}=\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{cl}}(t,P(t;M))[\Sigma],\quad t\in(t_{k},t_{k+1}),

(78)

with
\Sigma(t_{0})=X_{1}^{0}
and closed-loop jump update

\Sigma(t_{k}^{+})=\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{cl},k}(P(t_{k}^{+};M),P(t_{k}^{-};M))[\Sigma(t_{k}^{-})].

(79)

4.4.2
The two-point boundary value problem

Definition 4.5

(Impulsive optimal steering TPBVP)
.

Find
(P,\Sigma,M^{\star})
satisfying:

\displaystyle\textit{Backward I-GRE on }(t_{k},t_{k+1})\textit{:}\quad\mathcal{C}_{Z}(t,P(t))\succeq 0,\quad\mathcal{C}_{Z}\,\widetilde{/}\,(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}=0,

(80a)

\displaystyle\textit{Backward jump at }t_{k}\textit{:}\quad\mathcal{D}_{Z}(k,P(t_{k}^{+}),P(t_{k}^{-}))\succeq 0,\quad\mathcal{D}_{Z}\,\widetilde{/}\,(\mathcal{D}_{Z})_{3}=0,

(80b)

\displaystyle\textit{Forward moment equation on }(t_{k},t_{k+1})\textit{:}\quad\dot{\Sigma}=\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{cl}}(t,P(t))[\Sigma],

(80c)

\displaystyle\textit{Forward jump at }t_{k}\textit{:}\quad\Sigma(t_{k}^{+})=\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{cl},k}(P(t_{k}^{+}),P(t_{k}^{-}))[\Sigma(t_{k}^{-})],

(80d)

\displaystyle\textit{Boundary conditions:}\quad\Sigma(t_{0})=X_{1}^{0},\quad P(T)=M^{\star},\quad\Sigma(T)=X_{1}^{f}.

(80e)

The TPBVP (
80
) is coupled in two ways, as noted
in
[
20
]
for the scalar stochastic case:

(i)

Boundary coupling
: the terminal weight
M^{\star}=P(T)

is unknown and is determined by the terminal constraint
\Sigma(T)=X_{1}^{f}
.

(ii)

Nonlinear dynamic coupling
: the gain
K(t;P)
in
(
76
)–(
77
) depends on
P(t)
,
so the forward and backward equations are coupled through their
dynamics, not only through boundary values.
This coupling vanishes when the controlled and noise channels
coincide (e.g.,
B=B_{1}
in the stochastic case): the forward
equation then reduces to a Lyapunov equation with
coefficient
A+BK(t)
depending only on the backward solution.

Theorem 4.6

(Equivalence)
.

Any solution
(P^{\star},\Sigma^{\star},M^{\star})
of (
80
)
satisfies the steering condition (
73
); conversely,
any
M^{\star}
satisfying (
73
) generates a solution
via
P^{\star}=P(\cdot;M^{\star})
and forward integration
of (
80c
)–(
80d
) from
\Sigma(t_{0})=X_{1}^{0}
.
The optimal cost is
J^{\star}=\langle P^{\star}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle-\langle M^{\star},\,X_{1}^{f}\rangle
.

Proof.

Direct substitution:
P^{\star}=P(\cdot;M^{\star})
solves
(
80a
)–(
80b
) by
Theorem
3.5
;
\Sigma^{\star}(T)=X_{1}^{f}

is (
73
); the cost is Theorem
4.3
(c).
∎

4.5
Existence, uniqueness, and the forward-backward algorithm

Theorem 4.7

(Existence and uniqueness)
.

Suppose
X_{1}^{f}\in\mathcal{R}_{T}(X_{1}^{0})
and that assumption
(H1)

holds, so the closed-loop generator is strictly stable at
M=0
.
Then the map
M\mapsto\Sigma(T;M)
is strictly monotone decreasing

(
M_{1}\succ M_{2}\Rightarrow\Sigma(T;M_{1})\prec\Sigma(T;M_{2})
)
,
the TPBVP (
80
) has a unique solution, and the
optimal policy is unique a.e.

Proof.

Strict monotonicity.

We establish the
operator-monotone
form of the terminal map

M\mapsto\Sigma(T;M)
, which is what the uniqueness argument uses. Since

d
is concave (an infimum of affine functions of
M
) with gradient

\nabla d(M)=\Sigma(T;M)-X_{1}^{f}
, monotonicity of the gradient of a
concave function gives, for all
M_{1}\neq M_{2}
,

\langle M_{1}-M_{2},\,\Sigma(T;M_{1})-\Sigma(T;M_{2})\rangle=\langle M_{1}-M_{2},\,\nabla d(M_{1})-\nabla d(M_{2})\rangle\leq 0,

with
strict
inequality under (H1) (which makes
d

strictly concave, since the reachability Gram of
Proposition
4.4
(b) is then positive definite). Thus

M\mapsto\Sigma(T;M)
is strictly monotone decreasing in the operator
sense. The stronger Löwner statement
\Sigma(T;M_{1})\prec\Sigma(T;M_{2})

for
M_{1}\succ M_{2}
additionally requires the Riccati comparison principle

P(t;M_{1})\succeq P(t;M_{2})

[
1
]
and a closed-loop
comparison; it is not needed in what follows.

Existence.

The dual function
d(M)=\langle P(t_{0};M),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle-\langle M,\,X_{1}^{f}\rangle
is
concave (an infimum of affine functions: by the envelope/sufficiency
identity,
\langle P(t_{0};M),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle=\min_{X}\bigl[J_{T}^{Z}(X)+\langle M,\,EX(T)E^{*}\rangle\bigr]

over admissible
X
with running cost
J_{T}^{Z}
, so

d(M)=\min_{X}\bigl[J_{T}^{Z}(X)+\langle M,\,EX(T)E^{*}-X_{1}^{f}\rangle\bigr]
).
Assume the Slater condition
X_{1}^{f}\in\operatorname{int}\mathcal{R}_{T}(X_{1}^{0})

(strict feasibility). Then for every direction
N\succeq 0
,
\|N\|=1
,
strict feasibility provides an admissible
\bar{X}
with

E\bar{X}(T)E^{*}=X_{1}^{f}-\varepsilon N\in\mathcal{R}_{T}(X_{1}^{0})
for some

\varepsilon>0
, so
d(tN)\leq J_{T}^{Z}(\bar{X})-t\varepsilon\to-\infty
as

t\to\infty
; hence
d
is coercive on
\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
. The supremum is
therefore attained at some

M^{\star}\succeq 0
, and the stationarity condition

\nabla_{M}d(M^{\star})=\Sigma(T;M^{\star})-X_{1}^{f}=0
yields existence.

Uniqueness.

Suppose
M_{1}^{\star}\neq M_{2}^{\star}
both satisfy (
73
),
so
\Sigma(T;M_{1}^{\star})=\Sigma(T;M_{2}^{\star})=X_{1}^{f}
. The strict
monotonicity just established holds in the operator sense:

\langle M_{1}-M_{2},\,\Sigma(T;M_{1})-\Sigma(T;M_{2})\rangle<0
for all
M_{1}\neq M_{2}

(equivalently,
d
is strictly concave). Applying this to

M_{1}^{\star},M_{2}^{\star}
gives

0=\langle M_{1}^{\star}-M_{2}^{\star},\,X_{1}^{f}-X_{1}^{f}\rangle<0
, a contradiction (this
avoids the invalid step of assuming
M_{1}^{\star},M_{2}^{\star}
are
Löwner-comparable). Hence

M^{\star}
is unique, and the optimal policy is unique a.e. by
Theorem
3.5
.
∎

Remark 4.8
.

Strict monotonicity holds whenever (H1) ensures the closed-loop
generator is strictly negative definite at
M=0
. For the scalar
stochastic case, Chen et al.
[
20
]
prove existence and
uniqueness via the Schrödinger factorization; the proof above
gives an alternative Riccati-based argument.

4.5.1
The forward-backward algorithm

Since
\nabla_{M}d(M)=\Sigma(T;M)-X_{1}^{f}
, maximizing
d
is equivalent
to finding the zero of
\Sigma(T;M)-X_{1}^{f}
. The

forward-backward
(FB) algorithm does this iteratively.

(FB0)

Initialize
: set
M^{(0)}=0
(or any
M^{(0)}\succeq 0
).

(FB1)

Backward pass
: integrate the I-GRE
backward from
P^{(k)}(T)=M^{(k)}
to obtain the gain

K^{(k)}(t)=K(t;P^{(k)}(t))
on
[t_{0},T]
.

(FB2)

Forward pass
: integrate
(
80c
)–(
80d
) forward from

\Sigma^{(k)}(t_{0})=X_{1}^{0}
using
K^{(k)}
to obtain

\Sigma^{(k)}(T)
.

(FB3)

Update
:

M^{(k+1)}=M^{(k)}+\alpha^{(k)}\bigl(\Sigma^{(k)}(T)-X_{1}^{f}\bigr),

(81)

where
\alpha^{(k)}>0
is a step size (a Newton step using the
Jacobian of
M\mapsto\Sigma(T;M)
gives quadratic convergence
near
M^{\star}

[
20
,
38
]
).

(FB4)

Terminate
when

\|\Sigma^{(k)}(T)-X_{1}^{f}\|<\varepsilon
; otherwise return to (FB1).

Step (
81
) is gradient ascent on
d
: if

\Sigma^{(k)}(T)\succ X_{1}^{f}
the terminal second-moment is too large,
so
M
is increased to penalize it more, driving
\Sigma(T)
toward

X_{1}^{f}
; and conversely. Convergence is guaranteed by the concavity
of
d
and the strict monotonicity of Theorem
4.7
.

Remark 4.9

(Connection to the literature)
.

The TPBVP (
80
) and the FB algorithm recover,
in the impulsive matrix-valued setting, the optimality conditions
of
[
20
]
(equations (39a)–(39c) therein) and the
computational scheme of
[
38
]
.
For
N_{T}=0
(no jumps) and

Z=\bigl[\begin{smallmatrix}0&0\\
0&R\end{smallmatrix}\bigr]
with
standard Itô dynamics, (
80a
)–(
80c
)
reduce exactly to the backward Riccati and forward Lyapunov equations
of
[
20
]
.
The impulsive framework adds jump
conditions (
80b
)–(
80d
),
extends the cost to general
Z,Z_{k}
, and requires no Gaussian or
stochastic structure.

5
Dissipativity

This section develops a dissipativity theory for impulsive matrix-valued
systems in the spirit of Willems
[
63
]
. The supply rate
is
linear
in the trajectory
X
, with no sign assumed on the
supply matrix
M(t)
: this encompasses passivity-type supply rates,

H_{\infty}
-type supply rates, and integral quadratic constraints as
special cases. The storage function is also linear in
EXE^{*}
, again
with no sign on its coefficient
P
.

The finite-horizon theory (Section
5.1
) introduces the
supply rate, the available storage, and the I-D-GRE, and establishes
necessary and sufficient conditions via Propositions
5.5
and Theorems in Sections
5.1.3
–
5.1.5
.
The infinite-horizon theory (Section
5.2
) shows that the
available storage is the pointwise minimum over all storage functions, and
that the sequence
(-P_{T})
converges monotonically under detectability
conditions, again without time-invariance. All results parallel those of
Section
3
with
Z\leftarrow M
and

\mathcal{C}_{Z}\leftarrow\mathcal{C}_{M}
,

\mathcal{D}_{Z}\leftarrow\mathcal{D}_{M}
.
Dwell-time conditions are in Section
5.2.5
.

5.1
Finite-horizon

The finite-horizon analysis begins with the definitions of supply rate
and storage function. The key point is that the supply and storage are
both
linear
in the trajectory, which allows the same Schur
complement and key-identity machinery as in Section
3
to
apply verbatim.

5.1.1
Supply rates, storage functions, and available storage (finite horizon)

Definition 5.1

(Linear supply rate)
.

A
linear supply rate
consists of a
flow supply

\sigma_{f}(t,X):=\langle M(t),\,X\rangle
with
M:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{H}^{n+m}

piecewise continuous and bounded (no sign assumed on
M
),

jump supplies

\sigma_{k}(X):=\langle M_{k},\,EXE^{*}\rangle
with

M_{k}\in\mathbb{H}^{n}
, and
terminal supply

\sigma_{T}(X):=\langle M_{T},\,EXE^{*}\rangle
with
M_{T}\in\mathbb{H}^{n}
.

Definition 5.2

(Linear storage function)
.

A
linear storage function
is

V(t,X):=\langle-P(t),\,EXE^{*}\rangle
for some piecewise-
C^{1}

P:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{H}^{n}
; no sign is placed on
P
.
The sign convention
-P
is natural since the available storage
equals
\langle-P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
when
P
solves the D-GRE.

Definition 5.3

(Dissipativity and available
storage)
.

The system (
5
) is
finite-horizon dissipative

over
[t_{0},t_{0}+T]
with respect to
(\sigma_{f},\sigma_{k},\sigma_{T})

if there exists
\alpha:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
continuous with

\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\sigma_{f}(t,X(t))\,\mathrm{d}t+\sum_{k}\sigma_{k}(X(t_{k}^{-}))+\sigma_{T}(X(t_{0}+T))\geq-\alpha(\|X_{1}^{0}\|_{*})

for all admissible
X
and all
X_{1}^{0}\succeq 0
.
The
finite-horizon available storage
is

\displaystyle V_{a}^{T}(t_{0},X_{1}^{0})

\displaystyle:=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}X(\cdot)\in\mathbb{H}^{n+m}_{\succeq 0},\;\text{s.t.~\eqref{eq:sys:IMP}}\\
EX(t_{0})E^{*}=X_{1}^{0}\end{subarray}}\Bigl\{-\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\langle M(t),\,X(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad-\sum_{k}\langle M_{k},\,EX(t_{k}^{-})E^{*}\rangle-\langle M_{T},\,EX(T)E^{*}\rangle\Bigr\}.

(82)

The
infinite-horizon available storage
is

V_{a}(t_{0},X_{1}^{0}):=\sup_{T\geq 0}V_{a}^{T}(t_{0},X_{1}^{0})
.
The
required supply
is

V_{r}(t_{0},X_{1}^{0}):=\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}t_{1}\leq t_{0},\;EX(t_{1})E^{*}=0\\
EX(t_{0})E^{*}=X_{1}^{0}\end{subarray}}\Bigl\{\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{0}}\langle M(t),\,X(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\sum_{k:\,t_{1}<t_{k}<t_{0}}\langle M_{k},\,EX(t_{k}^{-})E^{*}\rangle\Bigr\}.

(83)

Finite-horizon dissipativity holds iff
V_{a}^{T}<\infty
for all

X_{1}^{0}\succeq 0
.

Definition 5.4

(Strict dissipativity)
.

The system is
strictly dissipative
with respect to

(\sigma_{f},\sigma_{k},\sigma_{T})
if there exist
\varepsilon>0

and
\alpha(\cdot)
continuous such that

\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\sigma_{f}(t,X)\,\mathrm{d}t+\sum_{k}\sigma_{k}(X(t_{k}^{-}))+\sigma_{T}(X(T))\geq\varepsilon\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\|E_{\bot}XE_{\bot}^{*}\|_{*}\,\mathrm{d}t-\alpha(\|X_{1}^{0}\|_{*}).

Strict dissipativity can always be obtained from dissipativity
by replacing
M\leftarrow M+\varepsilon I
(perturbing the supply
rate), which tightens the I-D-GRE by adding
\varepsilon I
to

(\mathcal{C}_{M})_{3}
. Such a perturbation changes
V_{a}
continuously.

Proposition 5.5

(Storage function bounds,
[
63
]
)
.

Suppose the system (
5
) is dissipative with supply

(\sigma_{f},\sigma_{k},\sigma_{T})
, and let
V=-\langle P,\,EXE^{*}\rangle
be any
storage function satisfying
V(t,0)=0
. Then:

(a)

V_{a}(t,X_{1})\leq V(t,X_{1})\leq V_{r}(t,X_{1})

for all
t\geq t_{0}
and
X_{1}\in\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
.

(b)

The available storage
V_{a}
is itself a storage function

(
the smallest
)
: every storage function satisfies

V(t,X_{1})\geq V_{a}(t,X_{1})
.

(c)

The required supply
V_{r}
is itself a storage function

(
the largest
)
: every storage function satisfies

V(t,X_{1})\leq V_{r}(t,X_{1})
.

(d)

In the linear storage function parametrization,

V_{a}=\langle-P_{a},\,\cdot\rangle
and
V_{r}=\langle-P_{r},\,\cdot\rangle
with

P_{a}\succeq P\succeq P_{r}
for any storage function matrix
P
,
i.e.,
-P_{a}\preceq-P\preceq-P_{r}
.

(e)

The system is dissipative if and only if
V_{r}<\infty

(
or equivalently
V_{a}<\infty
)
.

Proof.

(
a
).

Upper bound

V\leq V_{r}
: For any past trajectory steering
the state from
X_{1}(t_{1})=0
to
X_{1}(t_{0})=X_{1}^{0}
, integrating
the dissipation inequality
\dot{V}\leq\sigma_{f}
on flow and

\Delta V\leq\sigma_{k}
at jumps gives
V(t_{0},X_{1}^{0})-V(t_{1},0)\leq\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{0}}\sigma_{f}\,\mathrm{d}t+\sum_{k:\,t_{1}<t_{k}<t_{0}}\sigma_{k}
. Since
V(t_{1},0)=0
, taking
the infimum over all such past trajectories:

V(t_{0},X_{1}^{0})\leq V_{r}(t_{0},X_{1}^{0})
.

Lower bound

V\geq V_{a}
: For any future trajectory from

X_{1}(t_{0})=X_{1}^{0}
, the dissipation inequality integrated
forward gives

-\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\sigma_{f}\,\mathrm{d}t-\sum_{k}\sigma_{k}-\sigma_{T}\leq V(t_{0},X_{1}^{0})-\bigl[V(t_{0}+T,X(t_{0}+T))+\sigma_{T}\bigr].

In the indefinite-supply setting storage functions are
sign-indefinite (no sign is placed on
P
), so the classical step
“
V(t_{0}+T,\cdot)\geq 0
” is
not
available. The lower bound

-\int\sigma_{f}-\sum_{k}\sigma_{k}-\sigma_{T}\leq V(t_{0},X_{1}^{0})
instead holds
under the terminal-compatibility condition

V(t_{0}+T,X(t_{0}+T))+\sigma_{T}\geq 0
, i.e. the terminal boundary condition
of the finite-horizon D-GRE relating
P(t_{0}+T)
to
M_{T}

(cf. Theorem
5.11
); this is the precise hypothesis
that must replace nonnegativity of
V
. Taking the supremum:
V_{a}^{T}\leq V(t_{0},X_{1}^{0})
, hence
V_{a}\leq V
.

(
b
)–(
c
) follow from (
a
)
and the fact that
V_{a}
,
V_{r}
are attained by the D-GRE
solutions (Theorems
5.8

and
5.15
).

(
d
): In our linear parametrization,

V\geq V_{a}
reads
\langle-P,\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle\geq\langle-P_{a},\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
for all

X_{1}^{0}\succeq 0
, hence
-P\succeq-P_{a}
, i.e.,
P\preceq P_{a}
.
Similarly
V\leq V_{r}
gives
P\succeq P_{r}
.

(
e
): Dissipative
\Rightarrow

V_{a}<\infty

(Def.
5.3
). Conversely,
V_{r}<\infty
implies any
trajectory can be steered from
0
at finite supply cost, so

V_{r}
itself is a storage function (by (
c
)).
∎

5.1.2
Dissipativity GRE and operators (finite horizon)

Replacing
Z
and
Z_{k}
by
M
and
M_{k}
in (
10
)
and (
11
) gives the
dissipation operators

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{M}(t,P(t)):=E^{*}\dot{P}(t)E+\mathcal{F}^{*}(t,P(t))+M(t),

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{M}\bigl(k,P(t_{k}^{+}),P(t_{k}^{-})\bigr):=\mathcal{J}^{*}\!\bigl(k,P(t_{k}^{+})\bigr)-E^{*}P(t_{k}^{-})E+M_{k}.

(84)

The dissipation inequality
\tfrac{d}{dt}V\leq\sigma_{f}
on flow is
equivalent to
\mathcal{C}_{M}(t,P(t))\succeq 0
; the jump dissipation
inequality
V(t_{k}^{+})-V(t_{k}^{-})\leq\sigma_{k}
is equivalent to

\mathcal{D}_{M}(k,P(t_{k}^{+}),P(t_{k}^{-}))\succeq 0
.

Definition 5.6

(Impulsive D-GRE)
.

The
impulsive dissipativity GRE
(I-D-GRE) is the I-GRE
(Definition
3.2
) with
\mathcal{C}_{M}
and

\mathcal{D}_{M}
in place of
\mathcal{C}_{Z}
and

\mathcal{J}
, terminal condition
P(t_{0}+T)=-M_{T}
,
and no sign constraint on
P
.
The optimal sets are

\mathcal{K}_{c}^{M}(t,P(t)):=\{K:(\mathcal{C}_{M})_{3}K^{*}+(\mathcal{C}_{M})_{2}^{*}=0\}
and

\mathcal{K}_{d}^{M}:=\{K:(\mathcal{D}_{M})_{3}K^{*}+(\mathcal{D}_{M})_{2}^{*}=0\}
.

Remark 5.7
.

The I-D-GRE is formally identical to the I-GRE with
Z\leftarrow M

and
Z_{k}\leftarrow M_{k}
. Because
M
is indefinite,

(\mathcal{C}_{M})_{3}
need not be positive semidefinite for arbitrary

P
; condition (
16c
) (with
M
) is therefore a
genuine restriction on the solution.

The key identity for the dissipativity problem is obtained by
replacing
Z\leftarrow M
,
Z_{k}\leftarrow M_{k}
,
Z_{T}\leftarrow M_{T}

in (
13
): for
P(T)=-M_{T}
,

\displaystyle-\int\langle M(t),\,X(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t-\sum_{k}\langle M_{k},\,EX(t_{k}^{-})E^{*}\rangle-\langle M_{T},\,EX(T)E^{*}\rangle

\displaystyle\quad=\langle-P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle-\int\langle\mathcal{C}_{M}(t,P(t)),\,X(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t-\sum_{k}\langle\mathcal{D}_{M},\,X(t_{k}^{-})\rangle.

(85)

5.1.3
Sufficient condition (finite horizon)

Theorem
5.8
shows that any solution
P

of the I-D-GRE provides a valid storage function:

V(t,X)=-\langle P(t),\,EXE^{*}\rangle
satisfies the dissipation inequality
for all admissible trajectories. The proof is identical in
structure to Theorem
3.5
, with

\mathcal{C}_{M}
in place of
\mathcal{C}_{Z}
.

Theorem 5.8

(Sufficiency, impulsive dissipativity)
.

Let
T>0
. Suppose the I-D-GRE has a piecewise-
C^{1}
solution

P
with
P(t_{0}+T)=-M_{T}
. Then:

(a)

V_{a}^{T}(t_{0},X_{1}^{0})=\langle-P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
, attained at

X^{\star}
with
K\in\mathcal{K}_{c}^{M}
on flow and

K_{k}\in\mathcal{K}_{d}^{M}
at each jump.

(b)

P
is the unique piecewise-
C^{1}
solution with this
property.

(c)

V(t,X)=\langle-P(t),\,EXE^{*}\rangle
satisfies the dissipation
inequality on flow and at each jump.

Proof.

Part (a): Value.

The I-D-GRE gives
\mathcal{C}_{M}\succeq 0
and

\mathcal{D}_{M}\succeq 0
, so both the integral and the
sum in (
5.1.2
) are
\geq 0
(apply
Lemma
2.5
as in the proof of
Theorem
3.5
).
Hence the left-hand side of (
5.1.2
) is

\leq\langle-P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
for every admissible
X
, giving

V_{a}^{T}\leq\langle-P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
.

For
K\in\mathcal{K}_{c}^{M}
and
K_{k}\in\mathcal{K}_{d}^{M}
,
the attainment argument of Theorem
3.5

(with
\mathcal{C}_{M}
,
\mathcal{D}_{M}
) gives

\langle\mathcal{C}_{M}(t,P(t)),\,X^{\star}(t)\rangle=0
on flow and

\langle\mathcal{D}_{M},\,X^{\star}(t_{k}^{-})\rangle=0
at each jump.
Identity (
5.1.2
) then gives equality:

V_{a}^{T}=\langle-P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
.

Part (b): Uniqueness.

If
\tilde{P}
is another piecewise-
C^{1}
solution with

\tilde{P}(T)=-M_{T}
, then

\langle-\tilde{P}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle=V_{a}^{T}=\langle-P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
for all

X_{1}^{0}\succeq 0
. Taking
X_{1}^{0}=vv^{*}
gives

P(t_{0})=\tilde{P}(t_{0})
for all
t_{0}
, hence
P\equiv\tilde{P}
.

Part (c): Storage function.

On any flow subinterval
[s,t]
without jumps, the key identity
with sub-horizon
[s,t]
gives

\displaystyle V(t,X(t))-V(s,X(s))

\displaystyle=\int_{s}^{t}\langle M(\tau),\,X(\tau)\rangle\mathrm{d}\tau-\int_{s}^{t}\langle\mathcal{C}_{M}(\tau,P(t)),\,X(\tau)\rangle\mathrm{d}\tau\leq\int_{s}^{t}\sigma_{f}(\tau,X(\tau))\mathrm{d}\tau,

using
\mathcal{C}_{M}\succeq 0
and
X\succeq 0
. At jump
t_{k}
:

\displaystyle V(t_{k}^{+},X(t_{k}^{+}))-V(t_{k}^{-},X(t_{k}^{-}))

\displaystyle=\langle-P(t_{k}^{+}),\,\mathcal{J}(k,X(t_{k}^{-}))\rangle+\langle P,\,EX(t_{k}^{-})E^{*}\rangle

\displaystyle=-\langle\mathcal{D}_{M}-M_{k},\,EX(t_{k}^{-})E^{*}\rangle
