\displaystyle\leq\langle M_{k},\,EX(t_{k}^{-})E^{*}\rangle=\sigma_{k}(X(t_{k}^{-})),

using
\mathcal{D}_{M}\succeq 0
(so the

\mathcal{D}_{M}
term contributes non-negatively to
the right side).
∎

5.1.4
Necessary condition (finite horizon)

Conversely, if a linear storage function exists, then its coefficient
must satisfy the I-D-GRE and the available storage is the unique
smallest storage function.

Theorem 5.9

(Necessity, impulsive dissipativity)
.

Suppose the system is finite-horizon dissipative. Then the unique
piecewise-
C^{1}

P
with
V_{a}^{T}=\langle-P(t_{0}),\,\cdot\rangle
satisfies
the full I-D-GRE.

Proof.

Linearity of
V_{a}^{T}(t_{0},X_{1}^{0})
in
X_{1}^{0}
and the Riesz
representation give piecewise-
C^{1}

P
with
P(T)=-M_{T}
, by the
same positive-homogeneity and super-additivity argument as
Theorem
3.6
(
a
).

Flow DLMI.

Fix a flow instant
t\in(t_{k-1},t_{k})
. For any

[v_{1}^{*}\;v_{2}^{*}]^{*}\in\mathbb{C}^{n+m}
, consider the constant
perturbation
X_{1}\equiv v_{1}v_{1}^{*}
,
X_{2}\equiv v_{2}v_{1}^{*}
,

X_{3}\equiv v_{2}v_{2}^{*}
on
[t,t+h]
and continue optimally.
The dynamic programming inequality for the supremum reads

\langle-P(t),\,EX(t)E^{*}\rangle\leq-\int_{t}^{t+h}\langle M(t),\,X(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\langle-P(t+h),\,EX(t+h)E^{*}\rangle.

Dividing by
h>0
, letting
h\to 0
, and substituting

\dot{V}=\langle M(t),\,X(t)\rangle-\langle\mathcal{C}_{M}(t,P(t)),\,X(t)\rangle
:

0\leq[v_{1}^{*}\;v_{2}^{*}]\mathcal{C}_{M}(t,P(t))[v_{1}^{*}\;v_{2}^{*}]^{*},

so
\mathcal{C}_{M}(t,P(t))\succeq 0
.

Jump DLI.

At
t_{k}
, the dynamic programming inequality for the supremum gives

\langle-P,\,EX(t_{k}^{-})E^{*}\rangle\leq-\langle M_{k},\,EX(t_{k}^{-})E^{*}\rangle+\langle-P(t_{k}^{+}),\,\mathcal{J}(k,X(t_{k}^{-}))\rangle,

which rearranges to

\langle\mathcal{D}_{M}(k,P(t_{k}^{+}),P(t_{k}^{-})),\,X(t_{k}^{-})\rangle\geq 0

for all
X(t_{k}^{-})\succeq 0
, giving

\mathcal{D}_{M}\succeq 0
.

I-D-GRE conditions.

On the optimal trajectory,

\int\langle\mathcal{C}_{M},\,X^{\star}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\sum_{k}\langle\mathcal{D}_{M},\,X^{\star}(t_{k}^{-})\rangle=0

(from (
5.1.2
) at equality), with both terms

\geq 0
. The decomposition argument of
Theorem
3.6
(
d
) (with
\mathcal{C}_{M}

and
\mathcal{D}_{M}
) then gives the flow Riccati
equation (
16a
), flow compatibility
(
16b
), jump Riccati (
17a
),
and jump compatibility (
17b
).
∎

5.1.5
Equivalence and dual DLMI (finite horizon)

As in the optimal control case, necessity and sufficiency combine into an equivalence
and a dual DLMI. The dual DLMI for dissipativity seeks the

minimum
storage: the constraint
\mathcal{C}_{M}\succeq 0
,

\mathcal{D}_{M}\succeq 0
is again linear in
P
, but now we
minimize
\langle-P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
(the available storage) rather
than maximizing it.

Corollary 5.10

(Equivalence)
.

The system is finite-horizon dissipative with supply

(\sigma_{f},\sigma_{k},\sigma_{T})
if and only if the I-D-GRE has a
piecewise-
C^{1}
solution
P
with
P(T)=-M_{T}
.

Theorem 5.11

(Dual DLMI, impulsive dissipativity)
.

If the I-D-GRE has a solution
P^{*}
, then

V_{a}^{T}(t_{0},X_{1}^{0})=\inf_{P\text{ pw-}C^{1}}\;\langle-P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\begin{gathered}\mathcal{C}_{M}(t,P(t))\succeq 0,\quad\mathcal{D}_{M}(k,P(t_{k}^{+}),P(t_{k}^{-}))\succeq 0,\\
P(T)\succeq-M_{T}\end{gathered}

(86)

attained at
P^{*}
. Moreover
V_{a}^{T}=\langle-P^{*},\,\cdot\rangle
is the

smallest
storage function:

-P^{*}(t)\preceq-P(t)
(equivalently
P^{*}(t)\succeq P(t)
)
for every other valid storage function
-\langle P,\,EXE^{*}\rangle
.

Proof.

Step 1: Every feasible
P
is an upper bound.

For feasible
P
and admissible
X
, the key
identity (
12
) with
Z\leftarrow M
,

Z_{k}\leftarrow M_{k}
(not yet using
P(T)=-M_{T}
) gives

\displaystyle-\int\langle M(t),\,X(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t-\textstyle\sum_{k}\langle M_{k},\,EX(t_{k}^{-})E^{*}\rangle-\langle M_{T},\,EX(T)E^{*}\rangle

\displaystyle\quad=\langle-P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle+\underbrace{\langle-M_{T}-P(T),\,EX(T)E^{*}\rangle}_{\leq\,0}

\displaystyle\quad\quad-\underbrace{\int\langle\mathcal{C}_{M}(t,P(t)),\,X(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t}_{\geq\,0}-\underbrace{\textstyle\sum_{k}\langle\mathcal{D}_{M},\,X(t_{k}^{-})\rangle}_{\geq\,0}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\langle-P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle.

Taking the supremum over
X
:
V_{a}^{T}\leq\langle-P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
for
every feasible
P
, hence

V_{a}^{T}\leq\inf_{P\,\mathrm{feas.}}\langle-P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
.

Step 2:
P^{*}
is feasible and attains the infimum.

P^{*}
satisfies both DLMI constraints (from the I-D-GRE and
Lemma
2.4
) and
P^{*}(T)=-M_{T}
(so the terminal
constraint holds with equality). By Theorem
5.8
,

\langle-P^{*}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle=V_{a}^{T}
.
Hence
\inf\langle-P,\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle\leq\langle-P^{*},\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle=V_{a}^{T}
.
Combining with Step 1: equality, attained at
P^{*}
.

Sandwich.

For any storage function
V=-\langle P,\,EXE^{*}\rangle
, the dissipation
inequality gives
\mathcal{C}_{M}(t,P(t))\succeq 0
, so
P
is feasible.
Step 1 applied to this
P
gives
V_{a}^{T}\leq\langle-P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle=V
,
i.e.,
\langle P-P^{*},\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle=\langle-P^{*},\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle-\langle-P,\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle=V_{a}^{T}-V\leq 0
for all
X_{1}^{0}\succeq 0
. Taking

X_{1}^{0}=vv^{*}
gives
P^{*}(t)\succeq P(t)
,
i.e.,
-P^{*}(t)\preceq-P(t)
: the available storage is the smallest
storage function, consistent with
Proposition
5.5
(
b
).
∎

5.1.6
Continuous- and Discrete-Time Corollaries

Corollary 5.12

(Continuous-time dissipativity: finite horizon)
.

Set
N_{T}=0
. The I-D-GRE reduces to the continuous-time D-GRE:

\mathcal{C}_{M}(t,P(t))\succeq 0
,

\mathcal{C}_{M}\,\widetilde{/}\,\allowbreak(\mathcal{C}_{M})_{3}=0
,

P(T)=-M_{T}
.
The dual DLMI is

V_{a}^{T}(t_{0},X_{1}^{0})=\inf_{P}\;\langle-P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\mathcal{C}_{M}(t,P(t))\succeq 0,P(T)\succeq-M_{T},

(87)

recovering the results of
[
52
,
16
]
.

Corollary 5.13

(Discrete-time dissipativity: finite horizon)
.

Apply Corollary
5.12
with
\mathcal{F}=0
,

M=0
on flow,
t_{k}=k
,
\mathcal{J}=\mathcal{D}
,
M_{k}=M(k)
.
The I-D-GRE reduces to the discrete-time D-GRE at each step:

\mathcal{D}_{M}(k,P^{+},P(t))\succeq 0
,

\mathcal{D}_{M}\,\widetilde{/}\,\allowbreak(\mathcal{D}_{M})_{3}=0
,
where
\mathcal{D}_{M}(k,P^{+},P(t)):=\mathcal{D}^{*}(k,P^{+})-E^{*}PE+M(k)
.
The dual DLMI is

V_{a,N}(k_{0},X_{1}^{0})=\inf_{P}\;\langle-P(k_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\mathcal{D}_{M}(k,P^{+},P(t))\succeq 0,P(k_{0}+N)\succeq-M_{N}.

(88)

5.2
Infinite-horizon

The infinite-horizon available storage
V_{a}(t_{0},X_{1}^{0})=\sup_{T\geq 0}V_{a}^{T}(t_{0},X_{1}^{0})
is the tightest lower bound on the
energy that the system can extract from any initial state.
This subsection mirrors the structure of Section
5.1

for the infinite horizon: we first give a sufficient condition
(Theorem
5.14
), then an existence result under
detectability assumptions (Theorem
5.15
), and finally
the equivalence and dual DLMI (Theorem
5.17
).

5.2.1
Setting and assumptions (infinite horizon)

Standing assumptions for the infinite-horizon dissipativity problem:

(D1) Controllability.

V_{r}(t_{0},X_{1}^{0})<\infty
for all
X_{1}^{0}\succeq 0
.

(D2) Uniform boundedness.

M
,
M_{k}
,
\mathcal{F}
,
\mathcal{J}
are uniformly bounded;

{(\mathcal{C}_{M})_{3}}^{\dagger}
and
{(\mathcal{D}_{M})_{3}}^{\dagger}
are
uniformly bounded on compact sets of
P
.

(D3) Supply lower bound.

There exists
\delta>0
with

\langle M(t),\,X\rangle\geq-\delta\|EXE^{*}\|_{*}
for all
X\succeq 0
, uniformly in
t
.

(D4) Detectability.

Along any admissible trajectory with bounded supply extraction,

\|EX(t)E^{*}\|_{*}
is bounded and

T^{-1}\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}+T),\,EX(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\rangle\to 0
as
T\to\infty
. This is
the dissipativity analogue of the stabilizability/detectability
hypothesis (H1) of Section
3.2
.

Observe that (D4) is
not
implied by
the supply lower bound (D3), and is needed to make the infinite-horizon
boundary term asymptotically negligible.

5.2.2
Sufficient condition (infinite horizon)

Any bounded solution of the infinite-horizon I-D-GRE is a valid
storage function achieving the available storage.

Theorem 5.14

(Sufficient condition for infinite-horizon dissipativity)
.

Assume
(D1)–(D3)
. Suppose there exists a bounded
piecewise-
C^{1}
solution
P_{\infty}
of the infinite-horizon I-D-GRE

(Definition
5.6
)
. Then:

(a)

The system is dissipative on the infinite horizon:

V_{a}(t_{0},X_{1}^{0})<\infty
on
\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
, and

V_{a}(t_{0},X_{1}^{0})=\langle-P_{\infty}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
, attained by the
trajectory with gains from
\mathcal{K}_{c}^{M}
and
\mathcal{K}_{d}^{M}
.

(b)

V(t,X)=\langle-P_{\infty}(t),\,EXE^{*}\rangle
satisfies the dissipation
inequalities on flow and at each jump.

(c)

P_{\infty}
is the largest solution: for any other bounded
storage matrix
P
,
P_{\infty}(t)\succeq P(t)
uniformly in
t
,
equivalently
-P_{\infty}(t)\preceq-P(t)
.

(d)

P_{\infty}
is the unique bounded solution of the
infinite-horizon I-D-GRE.

Proof.

Value and dissipativity (
a
).

For any admissible
X
with
EX(t_{0})E^{*}=X_{1}^{0}
, the key
identity (
5.1.2
) applied with
P_{\infty}
on

[t_{0},t_{0}+T]
, using
\mathcal{C}_{M}\succeq 0
and

\mathcal{D}_{M}\succeq 0
, gives

-\!\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\!\langle M(t),\,X(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t-\!\sum_{k:\,t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T}\!\langle M_{k},\,EX(t_{k}^{-})E^{*}\rangle\leq\langle-P_{\infty}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle+\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}+T),\,EX(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\rangle.

Under the detectability assumption (D4), along any admissible trajectory with bounded
supply extraction,
\|EX(t)E^{*}\|_{*}
is bounded and
T^{-1}\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}+T),\,EX(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\rangle\to 0
, the boundary term is
asymptotically negligible. Taking the supremum over admissible
X
and the limit
T\to\infty
,

V_{a}(t_{0},X_{1}^{0})\leq\langle-P_{\infty}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle\leq\alpha_{2}\langle I,\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle<\infty.

The system is therefore dissipative on the infinite horizon.

For attainment, the closed-loop trajectory
X^{\star}
with gains

K\in\mathcal{K}_{c}^{M}
,
K_{k}\in\mathcal{K}_{d}^{M}
tightens the Schur
inequalities to equalities, so the key identity becomes

V_{a}^{T}(X^{\star})=\langle-P_{\infty}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle-\langle-P_{\infty}(t_{0}+T),\,EX^{\star}(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\rangle.

Under the detectability assumption (D4), the closed-loop trajectory

X^{\star}
satisfies
\langle-P_{\infty}(t_{0}+T),\,EX^{\star}(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\rangle\to 0
. Hence

V_{a}^{T}(X^{\star})\to\langle-P_{\infty}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
and

V_{a}\geq\langle-P_{\infty}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
. Equality holds.

Storage function (
b
).

\mathcal{C}_{M}\succeq 0
on flow and
\mathcal{D}_{M}\succeq 0
at
jumps give the dissipation inequalities for

V=\langle-P_{\infty},\,E\cdot E^{*}\rangle
directly on every interval.

Largest (
c
).

V_{a}
is the smallest storage function
(Proposition
5.5
(
d
)): any other bounded
storage
V^{\prime}=\langle-P,\,E\cdot E^{*}\rangle
satisfies
V_{a}\leq V^{\prime}
on
\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
.
Substituting linear representations and applying
Proposition
2.2
(
b
),

-P_{\infty}(t)\preceq-P(t)
.

Uniqueness (
d
).

Any other bounded
\tilde{P}
solving the I-D-GRE satisfies the
hypotheses with
\tilde{P}
in place of
P_{\infty}
, so
by (
a
),

\langle-\tilde{P}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle=V_{a}(t_{0},X_{1}^{0})=\langle-P_{\infty}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
.
Taking
X_{1}^{0}=vv^{*}
for every
v\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
gives

P_{\infty}(t_{0})=\tilde{P}(t_{0})
, and the same at every
t
yields

P_{\infty}\equiv\tilde{P}
.
∎

5.2.3
Necessary condition and existence (infinite horizon)

Theorem 5.15

(Necessary condition for infinite-horizon dissipativity)
.

Assume
(D1)–(D3)
, and suppose the system is dissipative on
the infinite horizon:
V_{a}(t_{0},X_{1}^{0})<\infty
for all

X_{1}^{0}\succeq 0
. Then there exists a unique bounded
piecewise-
C^{1}
solution
P_{\infty}
of the infinite-horizon I-D-GRE,
with

0\preceq-P_{\infty}(t)\preceq\alpha_{2}I\quad\text{uniformly in }t,

for some
\alpha_{2}>0
. It is the Loewner least upper bound of the
finite-horizon family
\{-P_{T}\}_{T\geq 0}
:

-P_{\infty}(t)\succeq-P_{T}(t)
for every
T\geq 0
and

t\in[t_{0},t_{0}+T]
, with
P_{T}
the I-D-GRE solution on

[t_{0},t_{0}+T]
subject to
P_{T}(t_{0}+T)=0
. The available storage is

V_{a}(t_{0},X_{1}^{0})=\langle-P_{\infty}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
.

Proof.

With
M
indefinite,
V_{a}^{T}(t_{0},X_{1}^{0})
is in general not monotone in

T
: extending a
T
-trajectory to a
T^{\prime}
-trajectory may add negative
supply on
(T,T^{\prime}]
, so the inequality
V_{a}^{T^{\prime}}\geq V_{a}^{T}
used in the
optimal-control case (Theorem
3.12
) does not propagate.
We bypass monotone convergence and construct
P_{\infty}
as the
Frobenius-Riesz representation of
V_{a}
.

Step 1:
V_{a}
is finite, linear, and non-negative.

The dissipativity assumption gives
V_{a}^{T}(t_{0},X_{1}^{0})\leq V_{a}(t_{0},X_{1}^{0})<\infty
for every
T\geq 0
and every

X_{1}^{0}\succeq 0
. Theorem
5.9
applied on each

[t_{0},t_{0}+T]
yields a unique piecewise-
C^{1}

P_{T}
with

P_{T}(t_{0}+T)=0
satisfying the finite-horizon I-D-GRE, with the linear
parametrisation
V_{a}^{T}(t_{0},X_{1}^{0})=\langle-P_{T}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
.
Linearity of
V_{a}
itself is established directly (not via
Proposition
5.5
, which presupposes the linear form):

V_{a}
is positively homogeneous as a supremum of homogeneous functions,
and by dynamic programming the available storage is attained by a

state-independent
linear feedback (the optimal extracting policy’s
gain depends on the storage, not on
X_{1}^{0}
), so the optimal future
trajectory is linear in
X_{1}^{0}
and
V_{a}
is additive, exactly as in
the additivity step of Theorem
5.9
. Hence

V_{a}(t,X_{1})=\langle-P_{\infty}(t),\,X_{1}\rangle
for a unique
-P_{\infty}(t)\in\mathbb{H}^{n}
,
obtained pointwise via Frobenius-Riesz, and
-P_{\infty}(t)
coincides with
the Loewner least upper bound of
\{-P_{T}(t)\}
constructed in Step 2.
Including
T=0
in the supremum
V_{a}=\sup_{T}V_{a}^{T}
gives

V_{a}(t,X_{1})\geq 0
for
X_{1}\succeq 0
.

Step 2:
-P_{\infty}
is the Loewner sup of
\{-P_{T}\}
, not a monotone limit.

For every
T\geq 0
and
X_{1}^{0}\succeq 0
,

\langle-P_{\infty}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle=V_{a}(t_{0},X_{1}^{0})\geq V_{a}^{T}(t_{0},X_{1}^{0})=\langle-P_{T}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle,

so by Proposition
2.2
(
b
),

-P_{\infty}(t_{0})\succeq-P_{T}(t_{0})
. Pointwise in
t
,

-P_{\infty}(t)\succeq-P_{T}(t)
for every
T
and
t\in[t_{0},t_{0}+T]
.
By
V_{a}=\sup_{T}V_{a}^{T}
, any linear functional
\langle Q,\,\cdot\rangle

dominating all
V_{a}^{T}
also dominates
V_{a}
, so

Q\succeq-P_{\infty}
; hence
-P_{\infty}
is the Loewner least upper
bound.

The family
\{-P_{T}(t)\}
itself need not be monotone in
T
when
M

is indefinite: a longer horizon may add a negative supply contribution
on
(T,T^{\prime}]
that decreases
V_{a}^{T^{\prime}}
relative to
V_{a}^{T}
. This is
in contrast to the optimal-control case
(Theorem
3.12
), where
Z\succeq 0
forces the analogous
sequence to be monotone.

Step 3: pointwise bounds on
-P_{\infty}
.

Lower bound:
V_{a}\geq 0
from Step 1 gives

-P_{\infty}(t)\succeq 0
by
Proposition
2.2
(
b
). Upper bound: by (D2),

V_{a}(t,X_{1})\leq\alpha_{2}\langle I,\,X_{1}\rangle
on
\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
for some

\alpha_{2}>0
, hence
-P_{\infty}(t)\preceq\alpha_{2}I
. Combined,

0\preceq-P_{\infty}(t)\preceq\alpha_{2}I
uniformly in
t
.

Step 4:
P_{\infty}
satisfies the infinite-horizon I-D-GRE.

The arguments of Theorem
3.6
, parts
(
b
),(
c
),(
d
), transfer with

V_{a}
in place of
J_{T}^{\star}
,
M,M_{k}
in place of
Z,Z_{k}
, and the
supremum direction replacing the infimum. Each argument is local and
independent of horizon length, so it applies to
V_{a}
verbatim.

Flow LMI:
\mathcal{C}_{M}(t,P_{\infty}(t))\succeq 0
.

Fix
t
in the interior of an inter-jump interval
(t_{k-1},t_{k})
.
For any
[v_{1}^{*}\;v_{2}^{*}]^{*}\in\mathbb{C}^{n+m}
, the constant perturbation

X_{1}\equiv v_{1}v_{1}^{*}
,
X_{2}\equiv v_{2}v_{1}^{*}
,
X_{3}\equiv v_{2}v_{2}^{*}
on

[t,t+h]\subset(t_{k-1},t_{k})
, continued admissibly on

[t+h,\infty)
, plus Bellman optimality for
V_{a}
gives

V_{a}(t,EX(t)E^{*})\geq-\!\int_{t}^{t+h}\!\langle M(s),\,X(s)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+V_{a}(t+h,EX(t+h)E^{*}).

Substituting
V_{a}(\cdot,\cdot)=\langle-P_{\infty}(\cdot),\,\cdot\rangle
,
rearranging, and letting
h\to 0
via (
5.1.2
):

0\leq[v_{1}^{*}\;v_{2}^{*}]\,\mathcal{C}_{M}(t,P_{\infty}(t))\,[v_{1}^{*}\;v_{2}^{*}]^{*}.

Arbitrariness of
v_{1},v_{2}
gives
\mathcal{C}_{M}(t,P_{\infty}(t))\succeq 0
.

Jump LMI:
\mathcal{D}_{M}(k,P_{\infty}(t_{k}^{+}),P_{\infty}(t_{k}^{-}))\succeq 0
.

The jump Bellman inequality combined with
(
5b
) and the adjoint relation

\langle P,\,\mathcal{J}(k,X)\rangle=\langle\mathcal{J}^{*}(k,P),\,X\rangle
gives

0\leq\langle M_{k}+\mathcal{J}^{*}(k,P_{\infty}(t_{k}^{+}))-E^{*}P_{\infty}(t_{k}^{-})E,\,X(t_{k}^{-})\rangle=\langle\mathcal{D}_{M},\,X(t_{k}^{-})\rangle,

hence
\mathcal{D}_{M}\succeq 0
.

Schur equalities.

The flow LMI provides

\operatorname{Im}((\mathcal{C}_{M})_{2}^{*})\subseteq\operatorname{Im}((\mathcal{C}_{M})_{3})

by Lemma
2.4
, so
\mathcal{K}_{c}^{M}(t,P_{\infty}(t))
is
non-empty. Repeating the local DP with the perturbation
u_{2}=Ku_{1}

for
K\in\mathcal{K}_{c}^{M}
tightens the Bellman inequality to equality
as
h\to 0
, giving
\langle\mathcal{C}_{M}(t,P_{\infty}(t)),\,uu^{*}\rangle=0
for

u=[u_{1}^{*}\;(Ku_{1})^{*}]^{*}
, every
u_{1}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
. Lemma
2.5

splits the inner product as

\langle\mathcal{C}_{M},\,uu^{*}\rangle=\langle\mathcal{C}_{M}\widetilde{/}(\mathcal{C}_{M})_{3},\,u_{1}u_{1}^{*}\rangle+\underbrace{\langle(\mathcal{C}_{M})_{3},\,W_{f}W_{f}^{*}\rangle}_{=\,0\text{ since }K\in\mathcal{K}_{c}^{M}},

so
u_{1}^{*}[\mathcal{C}_{M}\widetilde{/}(\mathcal{C}_{M})_{3}]u_{1}=0
for every

u_{1}
, giving
\mathcal{C}_{M}\widetilde{/}(\mathcal{C}_{M})_{3}=0
. The
compatibility condition

(\mathcal{C}_{M})_{3}{(\mathcal{C}_{M})_{3}}^{\dagger}(\mathcal{C}_{M})_{2}^{*}=(\mathcal{C}_{M})_{2}^{*}

follows from Lemma
3.4
. The identical argument at
each jump
t_{k}
with
K_{k}\in\mathcal{K}_{d}^{M}
gives the corresponding
jump equality and compatibility.

Piecewise-
C^{1}
regularity.

On each inter-jump interval, the I-D-GRE
(Definition
5.6
) expresses
\dot{P}_{\infty}
as
a continuous function of
P_{\infty}
and
t
via the pseudoinverse
formula; uniform boundedness follows from (D2). Hence
P_{\infty}
is
piecewise-
C^{1}
.

Step 5: uniqueness.

Suppose
\tilde{P}
is another bounded piecewise-
C^{1}
solution of the
infinite-horizon I-D-GRE. By
Theorem
5.14
(
a
) applied to

\tilde{P}
,
V_{a}(t_{0},X_{1}^{0})=\langle-\tilde{P}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
. Combined
with
V_{a}(t_{0},X_{1}^{0})=\langle-P_{\infty}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
from Step 1,

\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0})-\tilde{P}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle=0
for every

X_{1}^{0}\succeq 0
. Taking
X_{1}^{0}=vv^{*}
for every
v\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
gives

P_{\infty}(t_{0})=\tilde{P}(t_{0})
; arbitrariness of
t_{0}
yields

P_{\infty}\equiv\tilde{P}
.
∎

5.2.4
Equivalence and dual DLMI (infinite horizon)

Corollary 5.16

(Equivalence, infinite-horizon dissipativity)
.

Assume
(D1)–(D3)
. The following are equivalent:

(i)

The system is dissipative on the infinite horizon, i.e.,

V_{a}(t_{0},X_{1}^{0})<\infty
for all
X_{1}^{0}\succeq 0
.

(ii)

The infinite-horizon I-D-GRE admits a bounded
piecewise-
C^{1}
solution
P_{\infty}
.

Under either condition,
P_{\infty}
is unique,

0\preceq-P_{\infty}(t)\preceq\alpha_{2}I
uniformly, and

V_{a}(t_{0},X_{1}^{0})=\langle-P_{\infty}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
.

Proof.

(\textup{ii})\Rightarrow(\textup{i})
:
Theorem
5.14
(
a
).

(\textup{i})\Rightarrow(\textup{ii})
:
Theorem
5.15
.
∎

Theorem 5.17

(Dual DLMI, infinite-horizon dissipativity)
.

Under
(D1)–(D3)
,

V_{a}(t_{0},X_{1}^{0})=\inf_{P}\;\langle-P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle\quad\text{subject to}\quad\begin{gathered}\mathcal{C}_{M}(t,P(t))\succeq 0,\\
\quad\mathcal{D}_{M}(k,P(t_{k}^{+}),P(t_{k}^{-}))\succeq 0,\\
P\text{ bounded}\end{gathered}

(89)

the infimum is attained at
P_{\infty}
.

Proof.

Follows from Theorem
5.14
(c): any bounded
feasible
P
satisfies
P_{\infty}\preceq P
(sandwich), so

\langle-P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle\geq\langle-P_{\infty}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle=V_{a}
for all
feasible
P
, hence
P_{\infty}
attains the infimum.
∎

5.2.5
Dwell-time conditions

The dwell-time framework of Section
3.2.5
applies to
dissipativity with the operators
\mathcal{C}_{M}
,
\mathcal{D}_{M}
in
place of
\mathcal{C}_{Z}
,
\mathcal{D}_{Z}
, the storage matrix
-P

playing the role of the cost matrix
P
, and the supremum direction
replacing the infimum. The structural assumptions on the system
operators
\mathcal{F},\mathcal{J},M,M_{k}
in the three scenarios
(periodic, MDT, RDT) are inherited verbatim from
Section
3.2.5
, with
M,M_{k}
in place of
Z,Z_{k}
. The
one structural difference is the sign of the Geromel–Colaneri
condition: for optimal control the condition

\mathcal{C}_{Z}^{0}\preceq 0
ensures cost accumulation on the tail
(an upper bound on cost), whereas for dissipativity
the condition
\mathcal{C}_{M}^{0}\succeq 0
ensures that the storage
function remains valid on the tail (the dissipation
inequality continues to hold).

Periodic impulses.

Adopt the periodicity hypothesis of Theorem
3.17
:

\Delta_{k}\equiv T_{p}
,
\mathcal{F}(\cdot,X)
and
M(\cdot)
are

T_{p}
-periodic in
t
, and
\mathcal{J}(k,\cdot)
,
M_{k}
are
independent of
k
.

Theorem 5.18

(Causal necessary and sufficient condition: periodic dissipativity)
.

Under
(D1)–(D3)
and the periodicity hypothesis above, the
following are equivalent:

(i)

The system is dissipative on the infinite horizon, i.e.,

V_{a}(t_{0},X_{1}^{0})<\infty
for all
X_{1}^{0}\succeq 0
.

(ii)

The periodic D-GRE
(
system (
40
)
with
\mathcal{C}_{M}
,
\mathcal{D}_{M}
)
has a

T_{p}
-periodic piecewise-
C^{1}
solution

P_{\infty}:[0,T_{p}]\to\mathbb{H}^{n}
.

Under either condition,
P_{\infty}
is unique with

0\preceq-P_{\infty}(\tau)\preceq\alpha_{2}I
uniformly in
\tau
,
the causal storage function

V(\tau,X):=\langle-P_{\infty}(\tau),\,EXE^{*}\rangle
achieves the
infinite-horizon available storage

V_{a}(t_{0},X_{1}^{0})=\langle-P_{\infty}(0^{+}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle,

attained by the causal policy

K(\tau)\in\mathcal{K}_{c}^{M}(\tau,P_{\infty}(\tau))
on flow and

K_{k}\in\mathcal{K}_{d}^{M}(P_{\infty}(0^{+}),P_{\infty}(T_{p}^{-}))
at each jump.

Proof.

(\textup{ii})\Rightarrow(\textup{i})
.
Extend
P_{\infty}
to
[t_{0},\infty)
by
T_{p}
-periodicity:

P_{\infty}^{\mathrm{ext}}(t):=P_{\infty}\bigl((t-t_{0})\bmod T_{p}\bigr)
.
By
T_{p}
-periodicity of the data and
\Delta_{k}\equiv T_{p}
, the
inter-jump structure repeats with period
T_{p}
, so

P_{\infty}^{\mathrm{ext}}
is piecewise-
C^{1}
on
[t_{0},\infty)
with
discontinuities only at the jump times
\{t_{0}+kT_{p}\}_{k\geq 0}
, and it
satisfies the infinite-horizon I-D-GRE wherever
P_{\infty}
satisfies
the periodic D-GRE.
P_{\infty}^{\mathrm{ext}}
is bounded by

\sup_{\tau\in[0,T_{p}]}\|P_{\infty}(\tau)\|_{*}<\infty
uniformly in

[t_{0},\infty)
. Theorem
5.14
therefore applies
and gives

V_{a}(t_{0},X_{1}^{0})<\infty
with

V_{a}(t_{0},X_{1}^{0})=\langle-P_{\infty}^{\mathrm{ext}}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle=\langle-P_{\infty}(0^{+}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
, attainment by the closed-loop trajectory
with gains in
\mathcal{K}_{c}^{M}
,
\mathcal{K}_{d}^{M}
, and the bounds

0\preceq-P_{\infty}\preceq\alpha_{2}I
uniformly.

(\textup{i})\Rightarrow(\textup{ii})
.
By Theorem
5.15
, dissipativity provides a unique
bounded piecewise-
C^{1}
solution
P_{\infty}^{ih}:[t_{0},\infty)\to\mathbb{H}^{n}

of the infinite-horizon I-D-GRE with

0\preceq-P_{\infty}^{ih}(t)\preceq\alpha_{2}I
uniformly in
t
.
Define the
T_{p}
-shifted function

Q(t):=P_{\infty}^{ih}(t+T_{p}),\qquad t\in[t_{0},\infty).

We show
Q
also solves the infinite-horizon I-D-GRE on

[t_{0},\infty)
, so by uniqueness
Q\equiv P_{\infty}^{ih}
.

Flow.
For
t
in the interior of any inter-jump interval

(t_{k},t_{k+1})=(t_{0}+kT_{p},t_{0}+(k+1)T_{p})
, the shift
t\mapsto t+T_{p}

maps to the interior of
(t_{k+1},t_{k+2})
, which is again an
inter-jump interval. The composite flow operator depends on
t

through
\mathcal{F}(t)
and
M(t)
, both
T_{p}
-periodic by
hypothesis; hence
\mathcal{C}_{M}(t,\,\cdot\,)\equiv\mathcal{C}_{M}(t+T_{p},\,\cdot\,)
as operators on
\mathbb{H}^{n}
. Therefore

\mathcal{C}_{M}\bigl(t,Q(t)\bigr)=\mathcal{C}_{M}\bigl(t,P_{\infty}^{ih}(t+T_{p})\bigr)=\mathcal{C}_{M}\bigl(t+T_{p},P_{\infty}^{ih}(t+T_{p})\bigr)\succeq 0,

with the Schur equality

\mathcal{C}_{M}\,\widetilde{/}\,(\mathcal{C}_{M})_{3}=0
, both inherited
pointwise from
P_{\infty}^{ih}
evaluated at
t+T_{p}
.

Jumps.
At any jump time
t_{k}=t_{0}+kT_{p}
, the shift sends
t_{k}

to
t_{k+1}=t_{0}+(k+1)T_{p}
, the next jump time. Since

\mathcal{J}(k,\,\cdot\,)
and
M_{k}
are independent of
k
, the jump
operator
\mathcal{D}_{M}(k,P^{\prime},P)
does not depend on
k
. Therefore

\mathcal{D}_{M}\bigl(k,Q(t_{k}^{+}),Q(t_{k}^{-})\bigr)=\mathcal{D}_{M}\bigl(k+1,P_{\infty}^{ih}(t_{k+1}^{+}),P_{\infty}^{ih}(t_{k+1}^{-})\bigr)\succeq 0,

with the Schur equality

\mathcal{D}_{M}\,\widetilde{/}\,(\mathcal{D}_{M})_{3}=0
, both inherited from

P_{\infty}^{ih}
at the jump time
t_{k+1}
.

Boundedness.

0\preceq-Q(t)=-P_{\infty}^{ih}(t+T_{p})\preceq\alpha_{2}I
uniformly in

t
, inherited from
P_{\infty}^{ih}
.

Hence
Q
is a bounded piecewise-
C^{1}
solution of the
infinite-horizon I-D-GRE on
[t_{0},\infty)
. By uniqueness in
Theorem
5.15
,
Q\equiv P_{\infty}^{ih}
, i.e.,

P_{\infty}^{ih}(t+T_{p})=P_{\infty}^{ih}(t)
for all
t\in[t_{0},\infty)
.

P_{\infty}^{ih}
is therefore
T_{p}
-periodic, and its restriction to
one period defines a piecewise-
C^{1}
function

P_{\infty}:[0,T_{p}]\to\mathbb{H}^{n}
which solves the periodic D-GRE and
satisfies the bounds
0\preceq-P_{\infty}(\tau)\preceq\alpha_{2}I
.
Uniqueness within the class of
T_{p}
-periodic solutions follows from
the same uniqueness applied to any other such solution after the
extension of
(\textup{ii})\Rightarrow(\textup{i})
.

The value formula

V_{a}(t_{0},X_{1}^{0})=\langle-P_{\infty}(0^{+}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
and attainment by the
causal policy

K(\tau)\in\mathcal{K}_{c}^{M}(\tau,P_{\infty}(\tau))
,

K_{k}\in\mathcal{K}_{d}^{M}(P_{\infty}(0^{+}),P_{\infty}(T_{p}^{-}))
, follow from

(\textup{ii})\Rightarrow(\textup{i})
applied to the just-constructed

P_{\infty}
.
∎

The Schur equality requirements

\mathcal{C}_{M}\,\widetilde{/}\,(\mathcal{C}_{M})_{3}=0
and

\mathcal{D}_{M}\,\widetilde{/}\,(\mathcal{D}_{M})_{3}=0

in Theorems
5.18
–
5.23
identify the

optimal
extraction policy. Dropping these equalities and
retaining only the LMIs
\mathcal{C}_{M}\succeq 0
on flow and

\mathcal{D}_{M}\succeq 0
at jumps yields the broader class of
(non-optimal) causal linear storage functions in each dwell-time
scenario, and gives dual differential LMI characterizations of the
available storage parallel to Theorem
5.17
. In
all three cases, any
P
feasible for the LMI relaxation gives a
storage function
V(\tau,X)=\langle-P(\tau),\,EXE^{*}\rangle
which upper-bounds
the available storage, so
V_{a}\leq\langle-P,\,\cdot\rangle
pointwise on

\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
.

Theorem 5.19

(Dual DLMI, periodic dissipativity)
.

Under
(D1)–(D3)
and the periodicity hypothesis of
Theorem
5.18
,

\begin{array}[]{rl}\inf_{P}&\langle-P(0^{+}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle\\
\text{s.t.}&P:[0,T_{\min}]\to\mathbb{H}^{n},\;\text{bounded piecewise-}C^{1},\\
&\mathcal{C}_{M}(\tau,P(\tau))\succeq 0\;\text{a.e.,}\\
&\mathcal{D}_{M}(P(0^{+}),P(T_{p}^{-}))\succeq 0.\end{array}

(90)

The infimum is attained at
P_{\infty}
of
Theorem
5.18
, which is the unique feasible
P

satisfying additionally the Schur equalities of the periodic D-GRE.
The constraints are affine in
(P,\dot{P})
, so (
90
)
is a genuine differential LMI.

Proof.

Any feasible
P
gives a
T_{p}
-periodic causal linear storage function

V_{P}(\tau,X)=\langle-P(\tau),\,EXE^{*}\rangle
by

\mathcal{C}_{M}\succeq 0
on flow and
\mathcal{D}_{M}\succeq 0
at the
jump
\tau=T_{p}
. By
Theorem
5.14
(
c
) applied to
its
T_{p}
-periodic extension,
V_{a}\leq V_{P}
on
\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
, so

V_{a}(t_{0},X_{1}^{0})\leq\langle-P(0^{+}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
and taking the infimum

V_{a}(t_{0},X_{1}^{0})\leq\inf_{P}\langle-P(0^{+}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
. Conversely,
P_{\infty}

of Theorem
5.18
is feasible (the GRE solution
satisfies the LMIs trivially) and yields

V_{a}(t_{0},X_{1}^{0})=\langle-P_{\infty}(0^{+}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
, so the infimum is
attained and the inequality is an equality.
∎

Minimum dwell-time.

Adopt the timer-dependence hypothesis of Theorem
3.19
:

\Delta_{k}\geq T_{\min}>0
, the flow data depend only on the timer

\tau=t-t_{k}
, and the time-invariance
condition (
43
) holds for
\mathcal{J}
and
M_{k}\equiv M_{\mathrm{jp}}
and time-invariance of
\mathcal{F}
and
M
for
\tau\geq T_{\min}
.

Theorem 5.20

(Sufficient causal condition: MDT dissipativity)
.

Under
(D1)–(D3)
and the MDT hypothesis above, suppose
there exists
P_{s}:[0,T_{\min}]\to\mathbb{H}^{n}
satisfying:

(i)

Forward D-GRE on
(0,T_{\min})
:

\mathcal{C}_{M}(\tau,P_{s}(\tau))=0
for all

\tau\in(0,T_{\min})
.

(ii)

Jump D-GRE at
\tau=T_{\min}
:

\mathcal{D}_{M}(P_{s}(0^{+}),P_{s}(T_{\min}))\succeq 0
and

\mathcal{D}_{M}\,\widetilde{/}\,(\mathcal{D}_{M})_{3}=0
.

(iii)

Geromel–Colaneri condition:

\mathcal{C}_{M}^{0}(T_{\min},P_{s}(T_{\min})):=\mathcal{F}^{*}(T_{\min},P_{s}(T_{\min}))+M(T_{\min})\succeq 0
.

Then the causal storage function

V(\tau,X):=\langle-\bar{P}(\tau),\,EXE^{*}\rangle
, with
\bar{P}
the constant
extension of
P_{s}
for
\tau>T_{\min}
, satisfies the dissipation
inequalities
\dot{V}\leq\sigma_{f}
on flow and
\Delta V\leq\sigma_{k}

at each jump for all
\Delta_{k}\geq T_{\min}
, and provides the lower
bound
V_{a}(t_{0},X_{1}^{0})\geq\langle-P_{s}(0^{+}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
. The condition is
verifiable on
[0,T_{\min}]
alone.

Proof.

Extend
\bar{P}(\tau):=P_{s}(\tau)
for
\tau\leq T_{\min}
and

\bar{P}(\tau):=P_{s}(T_{\min})
for
\tau>T_{\min}
.

Flow dissipation.

Differentiating
V=\langle-\bar{P},\,EXE^{*}\rangle
along the flow gives

\dot{V}=\sigma_{f}-\langle\mathcal{C}_{M}(\tau,\bar{P}),\,X\rangle
, so

\dot{V}\leq\sigma_{f}
iff
\mathcal{C}_{M}(\tau,\bar{P})\succeq 0
. For

\tau\in(0,T_{\min})
,
\mathcal{C}_{M}=0
by (i). For

\tau>T_{\min}
,
\dot{\bar{P}}=0
, so
\mathcal{C}_{M}(\tau,\bar{P})=\mathcal{C}_{M}^{0}(\tau,P_{s}(T_{\min}))\succeq 0
by (iii).

Jump dissipation.

\Delta V\leq\sigma_{k}
iff
\mathcal{D}_{M}\succeq 0
, which holds for

\Delta_{k}\geq T_{\min}
since
\bar{P}(\Delta_{k}^{-})=P_{s}(T_{\min})
and

\mathcal{D}_{M}(P_{s}(0^{+}),P_{s}(T_{\min}))\succeq 0
by (ii).

Available storage bound.

The dissipation inequalities along any admissible trajectory give

V(\tau(t_{0}+T),EX(t_{0}+T)E^{*})-V(0^{+},X_{1}^{0})\leq\int\sigma_{f}\,\mathrm{d}t+\sum\sigma_{k}
, and rearranging together with

V\geq 0
on
\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
(from
-\bar{P}\succeq 0
implicit in the
infinite-horizon I-D-GRE) yields

\langle-P_{s}(0^{+}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle\leq V_{a}(t_{0},X_{1}^{0})
.
∎

Remark 5.21
.

The dissipativity Geromel–Colaneri condition (iii) requires

\mathcal{C}_{M}^{0}\succeq 0

(
positive
semidefinite
)
, in contrast to the optimal control
condition (
45
) which requires

\mathcal{C}_{Z}^{0}\preceq 0
. The sign reversal reflects the dual role
of
P
on the frozen region: in the optimal control case

P\succeq 0
and the condition ensures that the cost matrix continues
to upper-bound the tail cost, whereas in the dissipativity case

P_{s}
may be indefinite and the condition ensures that the storage
matrix
-P_{s}
continues to satisfy the dissipation inequality.

Theorem 5.22

(Dual DLMI, MDT dissipativity)
.

Under
(D1)–(D3)
and the MDT hypothesis of
Theorem
5.20
,

\begin{array}[]{rl}\min_{P}&\langle-P(0^{+}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle\\
\text{s.t.}&P:[0,T_{\min}]\to\mathbb{H}^{n},\;\text{bounded piecewise-}C^{1},\\
&\mathcal{C}_{M}(\tau,P(\tau))\succeq 0\;\text{a.e.\ on }(0,T_{\min}),\\
&\mathcal{D}_{M}(P(0^{+}),P(T_{\min}))\succeq 0,\\
&\mathcal{C}_{M}^{0}(T_{\min},P(T_{\min}))\succeq 0.\end{array}

(91)

Any feasible
P
defines a causal storage function

V_{P}(\tau,X)=\langle-\bar{P}(\tau),\,EXE^{*}\rangle
with
\bar{P}
the constant
extension of
P
to
[T_{\min},\infty)
. The infimum is attained at

P_{s}
of Theorem
5.20
when the Schur equalities of the
forward D-GRE on
[0,T_{\min}]
and at
\tau=T_{\min}
are imposed
on top of the LMIs.

Proof.

For any feasible
P
, the constant extension
\bar{P}
satisfies

\mathcal{C}_{M}(\tau,\bar{P}(\tau))\succeq 0
on all of

(0,\infty)
(using

\mathcal{C}_{M}(\tau,\bar{P})=\mathcal{C}_{M}^{0}(\tau,P(T_{\min}))\succeq 0

for
\tau>T_{\min}
) and
\mathcal{D}_{M}\succeq 0
at every jump.
Hence
V_{P}(\tau,X):=\langle-\bar{P}(\tau),\,EXE^{*}\rangle
is a causal storage
function by the same argument as in the proof of
Theorem
5.20
. Then

V_{a}\leq V_{P}
by
Theorem
5.14
(
c
), yielding

V_{a}(t_{0},X_{1}^{0})\leq\langle-P(0^{+}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
and the infimum bound. The
attainment at
P_{s}
follows since
P_{s}
is feasible and satisfies the
Schur equalities, which are the residual conditions distinguishing
the LMI feasibility set from the GRE solution.
∎

Range dwell-time.

Adopt the timer-dependence hypothesis of Theorem
3.22
:

\Delta_{k}\in[T_{\min},T_{\max}]
and the flow and jump data depend
only on the timer
\tau\in[0,T_{\max}]
;
\mathcal{F}(\tau,\cdot)
and
M(\tau)
are defined on

[0,T_{\max}]
, and
\mathcal{J},M_{k}
are
k
-independent).

Theorem 5.23

(Sufficient causal condition: RDT dissipativity)
.

Under
(D1)–(D3)
and the RDT hypothesis above, suppose
there exists
P_{s}:[0,T_{\max}]\to\mathbb{H}^{n}
satisfying:

(i)

\mathcal{C}_{M}(\tau,P_{s}(\tau))=0
for

\tau\in(0,T_{\max})
.

(ii)

\mathcal{D}_{M}(P_{s}(0^{+}),P_{s}(\tau))\succeq 0
and

\mathcal{D}_{M}\,\widetilde{/}\,(\mathcal{D}_{M})_{3}=0

for all
\tau\in[T_{\min},T_{\max}]
.

Then
V(\tau,X):=\langle-P_{s}(\tau),\,EXE^{*}\rangle
is a causal storage
function achieving
\dot{V}=\sigma_{f}
on flow and
\Delta V=\sigma_{k}

at the optimal extraction policy for all

\Delta_{k}\in[T_{\min},T_{\max}]
, and provides the lower bound

V_{a}(t_{0},X_{1}^{0})\geq\langle-P_{s}(0^{+}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
.

Proof.

For any
\Delta_{k}\in[T_{\min},T_{\max}]
, condition (i) gives

\mathcal{C}_{M}=0
on
(0,T_{\max})\supset(0,\Delta_{k})
, so

\dot{V}\leq\sigma_{f}
with equality at the optimal gain. Condition (ii)
gives
\mathcal{D}_{M}\succeq 0
with Schur zero at
\tau=\Delta_{k}
, so

\Delta V\leq\sigma_{k}
with equality. Since the flow D-GRE holds with
equality on all of
[0,T_{\max}]
, no Geromel–Colaneri tail extension
is needed. The available storage bound follows as in the proof of
Theorem
5.20
.
∎

Theorem 5.24

(Dual DLMI, RDT dissipativity)
.

Under
(D1)–(D3)
and the RDT hypothesis of
Theorem
5.23
,

\begin{array}[]{rl}\min_{P}&\langle-P(0^{+}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle\\
\text{s.t.}&P:[0,T_{\max}]\to\mathbb{H}^{n},\;\text{bounded piecewise-}C^{1},\\
&\mathcal{C}_{M}(\tau,P(\tau))\succeq 0\;\text{a.e.\ on }(0,T_{\max}),\\
&\mathcal{D}_{M}(P(0^{+}),P(\tau))\succeq 0\;\text{for }\tau\in[T_{\min},T_{\max}],\\
\end{array}

(92)

Any feasible
P
defines a causal storage function on

[0,T_{\max}]
. The infimum is attained at
P_{s}
of
Theorem
5.23
when the Schur equalities of the forward
D-GRE on
[0,T_{\max}]
and on
[T_{\min},T_{\max}]
are imposed
on top of the LMIs.

Proof.

Identical to that of Theorem
5.22
, with the timer
interval
[0,T_{\max}]
in place of
[0,T_{\min}]
and the jump LMI

\mathcal{D}_{M}\succeq 0
holding for all
\tau\in[T_{\min},T_{\max}]
.
No tail extension is needed since the flow LMI holds on the entire
timer interval.
∎

5.2.6
Continuous- and Discrete-Time Corollaries

Corollary 5.25

(Continuous-time dissipativity: infinite horizon)
.

Set
N_{T}=0
. Theorem
5.15
gives the unique
bounded
C^{1}
solution
P_{\infty}
of the continuous-time infinite-horizon
D-GRE, with
V_{a}(t_{0},X_{1}^{0})=\langle-P_{\infty}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
,

P_{\infty}=\lim_{T}P_{T}
(the limit need not be monotone for indefinite
M
), and the
smallest-storage-function sandwich property.
The dual is
V_{a}=\inf_{P}\langle-P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
s.t.
\mathcal{C}_{M}(t,P(t))\succeq 0
,
P
bounded.

Corollary 5.26

(Discrete-time dissipativity: infinite horizon)
.

Apply Theorem
5.15
with
\mathcal{F}=0
,

M=0
on flow,
t_{k}=k
,
\mathcal{J}=\mathcal{D}
.
The infinite-horizon discrete-time available storage is

V_{a}(k_{0},X_{1}^{0})=\langle-P_{\infty}(k_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
, where
P_{\infty}

satisfies the algebraic discrete-time D-GRE and is the
limit of the
N
-step solutions.
The dual is
V_{a}=\inf_{P}\langle-P(k_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
s.t.
\mathcal{D}_{M}(k,P^{+},P(t))\succeq 0
,
P
bounded.

5.3
Long-term average available storage

We now develop the dissipativity counterpart of Section
3.3
.
The setup parallels that subsection: the dynamics are augmented by a
Hermitian forcing on the state block as in
Definition
3.25
, and we study the long-term rate of
energy extraction rather than the cumulative available storage. The
sign conventions of Section
5
are retained: the storage
function takes the form
V=\langle-P,\,EXE^{*}\rangle
and the available storage
is the
supremum
of the extractable supply, in contrast to the

infimum
of the cost in the optimal-control case. The
analysis proceeds in three steps: the forced key identity acquires
linear correction terms in
\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W}_{k}

(Section
5.3.2
); the average available-storage
rate is identified through a linear pairing with the forcing data
(Sections
5.3.4
–
5.3.5
); and
causal dwell-time bounds follow by the same constructions as in
Section
3.3.6
.

5.3.1
Setting, supply rate, and assumptions

The forced impulsive system is that of
Definition
3.25
, with
\mathcal{W}(t)\in\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
and

\mathcal{W}_{k}\in\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
bounded. The supply rate is the linear
supply of Definition
5.1
,

\sigma_{f}(t,X)=\langle M(t),\,X\rangle
,
\sigma_{k}(X)=\langle M_{k},\,EXE^{*}\rangle
,

\sigma_{T}=0
(no terminal supply, as we work directly in the
infinite-horizon setting). The horizon-
T
available storage is

V_{a}^{T}(t_{0},X_{1}^{0}):=\sup_{X(\cdot)\in\mathbb{H}^{n+m}_{\succeq 0}}\,\left\{-\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\!\langle M(t),\,X(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t-\!\!\sum_{k:\,t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T}\!\!\langle M_{k},\,EX(t_{k}^{-})E^{*}\rangle\right\}

(93)

subject to (
46
) and
EX(t_{0})E^{*}=X_{1}^{0}
.

Definition 5.27

(Long-term average available storage rate)
.

The
long-term average available storage rate
is

\bar{V}_{a}^{\star}(X_{1}^{0}):=\limsup_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}\,V_{a}^{T}(t_{0},X_{1}^{0}).

(94)

Assumptions.

The well-posedness of the long-term average available-storage
problem requires three assumptions on the homogeneous dynamics and
the forcing data. These are the dissipativity counterparts of
(A1), (A3), (A4) of Section
3.3.1
, renumbered
consecutively here: no analogue of the cost-coercivity
condition (A2) of Section
3.3.1
is required, since
dissipativity does not need a positive lower bound on the supply
rate
M
(which may be indefinite).

(A1) Uniform stabilizability of the
homogeneous dynamics.

There exist a feedback
K_{s}\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times n}
, constants

\beta\geq 1
,
\rho\in(0,1)
and
\alpha>0
, such that the homogeneous
closed-loop trajectory
X_{s}^{\mathrm{h}}
of (
5
) (i.e.,

\mathcal{W}=0
,
\mathcal{W}_{k}=0
) with feedback

X_{2}^{s}=K_{s}X_{1}^{s}
,
X_{3}^{s}=K_{s}X_{1}^{s}K_{s}^{*}
satisfies

\|EX_{s}^{\mathrm{h}}(t)E^{*}\|_{*}\leq\beta e^{-\alpha(t-s)}\rho^{\kappa(t,s)}\|EX_{s}^{\mathrm{h}}(s)E^{*}\|_{*}
for all
t\geq s\geq t_{0}
.

(A2) Uniform boundedness of the operators
and supply rates.

M,M_{k},\mathcal{F}(t,\cdot),\mathcal{J}(k,\cdot)
are uniformly
bounded;
(\mathcal{C}_{M})_{3}(t,P(t))\succeq 0
and

(\mathcal{D}_{M})_{3}(k,P(t_{k}^{+}),P(t_{k}^{-}))\succeq 0
for all

P\succeq 0
;
{(\mathcal{C}_{M})_{3}}^{\dagger}
and

{(\mathcal{D}_{M})_{3}}^{\dagger}
are uniformly bounded on bounded sets of

P
; and
\mathcal{W}(t),\mathcal{W}_{k}
are uniformly bounded.

(A3) Jump-rate regularity.

The inter-jump times
\Delta_{k}=t_{k+1}-t_{k}
are bounded both above
and below by positive constants: there exist
0<\Delta_{\min}\leq\Delta_{\max}<\infty
such that
\Delta_{k}\in[\Delta_{\min},\Delta_{\max}]
for all
k\geq 0
. In particular, the jump rate

\rho_{\mathrm{rate}}:=\lim_{T\to\infty}T^{-1}\#\{k:t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T\}

exists and is finite, with
\Delta_{\max}^{-1}\leq\rho_{\mathrm{rate}}\leq\Delta_{\min}^{-1}
.

Assumption (A1) is identical to its optimal-control counterpart:
the dual decay
\beta e^{-\alpha(t-s)}\rho^{\kappa(t,s)}
absorbs
both flow and jump contributions to the closed-loop decay rate,
and is exactly the condition that guarantees existence of a bounded
solution
P_{\infty}
to the homogeneous infinite-horizon I-D-GRE by
Theorem
5.15
. Assumption (A2) extends the original
dissipativity boundedness hypotheses of Section
5.2

to include the forcing data
\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W}_{k}
.

Assumption (A3) plays a different role from (A1)–(A2).
Assumption (A1), through the dual decay rate

\beta e^{-\alpha(t-s)}\rho^{\kappa(t,s)}
, already ensures that the
homogeneous closed-loop trajectory decays robustly under both small
and large inter-jump times: small
\Delta_{k}
are absorbed by the
geometric factor
\rho^{\kappa(t,s)}
(each jump contributing decay),
while large
\Delta_{k}
are absorbed by the continuous factor

e^{-\alpha(t-s)}
(the flow being independently stable).
Consequently, state stability and the summability of the jump-supply
contribution along stabilizing trajectories follow from (A1) alone,
without any direct constraint on the
\Delta_{k}
. The role of (A3)
is purely to make the
time-averaged
long-term available
storage well-defined as a genuine limit rather than a
\limsup
: the
existence of the jump rate
\rho_{\mathrm{rate}}
ensures that the
discrete jump-supply contribution admits a long-run average, and
bounded
\Delta_{k}
ensures that the supply rates
\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W}_{k}
are properly time-averaged. Theorems giving only a
lower bound on the long-term available storage invoke
only (A1)–(A2); theorems giving exact long-term equalities
additionally require (A3).

5.3.2
Forced key identity (dissipativity)

The forced key identity of Proposition
3.27

applies with
Z\leftarrow M
,
Z_{k}\leftarrow M_{k}
, giving, for any
piecewise-
C^{1}

P:[t_{0},t_{0}+T]\to\mathbb{H}^{n}
and any admissible trajectory

X
of (
46
),

\displaystyle-\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\!\langle M(t),\,X(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t-\!\!\sum_{k:\,t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T}\!\!\langle M_{k},\,EX(t_{k}^{-})E^{*}\rangle

(95)

\displaystyle=\langle-P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle-\langle-P(t_{0}+T),\,EX(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\rangle

\displaystyle-\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\!\langle\mathcal{C}_{M}(t,P(t)),\,X(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t-\!\!\sum_{k:\,t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T}\!\!\langle\mathcal{D}_{M}(k,P(t_{k}^{+}),P(t_{k}^{-})),\,X(t_{k}^{-})\rangle

\displaystyle-\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\!\langle P(t),\,\mathcal{W}(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t-\!\!\sum_{k:\,t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T}\!\!\langle P(t_{k}^{+}),\,\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle.

This is (
49
) multiplied by
-1
and rearranged,
with the storage-function sign convention
V=\langle-P,\,EXE^{*}\rangle
made
explicit on the right-hand side.

5.3.3
Finite-horizon average available storage

Theorem 5.28

(Finite-horizon average available storage decomposition)
.

Let
T>0
and let
P_{T}
be the piecewise-
C^{1}
solution of the
finite-horizon I-D-GRE (Definition
5.6
) with

P_{T}(t_{0}+T)=0
. Then the horizon-
T
available
storage (
93
) satisfies

V_{a}^{T}(t_{0},X_{1}^{0})=\langle-P_{T}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle-\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\!\langle P_{T}(t),\,\mathcal{W}(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t-\!\!\sum_{k:\,t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T}\!\!\langle P_{T}(t_{k}^{+}),\,\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle,

(96)

and the horizon-
T
time-averaged rate is

\bar{V}_{a}^{T}(t_{0},X_{1}^{0}):=V_{a}^{T}/T
. The supremum is attained at

X^{\star}
with
K(t)\in\mathcal{K}_{c}^{M}(t,P_{T}(t))
on flow and

K_{k}\in\mathcal{K}_{d}^{M}(k,P_{T}(t_{k}^{+}),P_{T}(t_{k}^{-}))
at each jump.

Proof.

Apply (
95
) with
P=P_{T}
. Since
P_{T}
solves the
I-D-GRE,
\mathcal{C}_{M}(t,P_{T}(t))\succeq 0
and

\mathcal{D}_{M}(k,P_{T}(t_{k}^{+}),P_{T}(t_{k}^{-}))\succeq 0
with both Schur
complements vanishing (Lemma
3.4
). The Schur
inner-product decomposition (Lemma
2.5
) gives

\langle\mathcal{C}_{M}(t,P_{T}(t)),\,X(t)\rangle\geq 0
and

\langle\mathcal{D}_{M}(k,P_{T}(t_{k}^{+}),P_{T}(t_{k}^{-})),\,X(t_{k}^{-})\rangle\geq 0
for every
admissible
X
, with equality at any
X^{\star}
with feedback in

\mathcal{K}_{c}^{M},\mathcal{K}_{d}^{M}
. Using
P_{T}(t_{0}+T)=0
(so the
second boundary term in (
95
) vanishes) and that
the last two terms are independent of the free blocks
(X_{2},X_{3})
,
the supremum in (
93
) is attained at any such

X^{\star}
and equals (
96
).
∎

The decomposition (
96
) separates

\bar{V}_{a}^{T}
into a
transient term

T^{-1}\langle-P_{T}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
that vanishes as
T\to\infty
and

forcing terms
whose
T\to\infty
limit gives the long-term
available storage rate; this is the content of the next subsection.

5.3.4
Infinite-horizon: sufficient condition

The following theorem is the central sufficient condition. It says
that any bounded solution of the
homogeneous
infinite-horizon
I-D-GRE (Section
5.2
) determines the long-term
available storage rate through a linear pairing with the forcing
data. No new Riccati-type equation is required.

Theorem 5.29

(Sufficient condition, infinite-horizon average
available storage)
.

Suppose there exists a bounded piecewise-
C^{1}
function

P_{\infty}:[t_{0},\infty)\to\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
satisfying the infinite-horizon
I-D-GRE (Section
5.2
), and assume further that the
limit

\bar{V}_{a}^{\star}:=\lim_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}\!\left[-\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\!\langle P_{\infty}(t),\,\mathcal{W}(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t-\!\!\sum_{k:\,t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T}\!\!\langle P_{\infty}(t_{k}^{+}),\,\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle\right]

(97)

exists. Then
:

(a)

The long-term average available storage rate satisfies

\bar{V}_{a}^{\star}(X_{1}^{0})=\bar{V}_{a}^{\star}
for every

X_{1}^{0}\in\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
, i.e., the rate is independent of the initial
condition.

(b)

The supremum is attained at any trajectory
X^{\star}
with

K_{\infty}(t)\in\mathcal{K}_{c}^{M}(t,P_{\infty}(t))
on flow and

K_{k}\in\mathcal{K}_{d}^{M}(k,P_{\infty}(t_{k}^{+}),P_{\infty}(t_{k}^{-}))
at
each jump.

(c)

P_{\infty}
is the unique bounded solution of the infinite-horizon
I-D-GRE.

Proof.

Upper bound.

Let
X
be any admissible trajectory of (
46
).
Apply (
95
) on
[t_{0},t_{0}+T]
with
P=P_{\infty}
.
Since
P_{\infty}
solves the I-D-GRE,

\mathcal{C}_{M}(t,P_{\infty}(t))\succeq 0
and

\mathcal{D}_{M}(k,P_{\infty}(t_{k}^{+}),P_{\infty}(t_{k}^{-}))\succeq 0
. By
Lemma
2.5
, both inner products are non-negative, so

\displaystyle-\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\langle M(t),\,X(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t-\sum_{k:\,t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T}\langle M_{k},\,EX(t_{k}^{-})E^{*}\rangle

(98)

\displaystyle\leq\langle-P_{\infty}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle-\langle-P_{\infty}(t_{0}+T),\,EX(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\rangle-R_{T}(P_{\infty}),

where

R_{T}(P_{\infty}):=\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\!\langle P_{\infty}(t),\,\mathcal{W}(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\sum_{k:\,t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T}\!\langle P_{\infty}(t_{k}^{+}),\,\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle.

Dividing by
T
, and noting that

T^{-1}\langle-P_{\infty}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle\to 0
as
T\to\infty
(both factors
bounded), and that
T^{-1}\langle-P_{\infty}(t_{0}+T),\,EX(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\rangle\to 0

provided
\|EX(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\|_{*}
is bounded (which holds along any
admissible trajectory under (A1)–(A2) by
Lemma
5.30
below):

\limsup_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}V_{a}^{T}(t_{0},X_{1}^{0};X)\leq\lim_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}\bigl(-R_{T}(P_{\infty})\bigr)=\bar{V}_{a}^{\star}.

Taking the supremum over admissible
X
yields

\bar{V}_{a}^{\star}(X_{1}^{0})\leq\bar{V}_{a}^{\star}
.

Attainment.

Let
X^{\star}
be the trajectory with gains

K_{\infty}(t)\in\mathcal{K}_{c}^{M}(t,P_{\infty}(t))
on flow and

K_{k}\in\mathcal{K}_{d}^{M}(k,P_{\infty}(t_{k}^{+}),P_{\infty}(t_{k}^{-}))
at each
jump. By the attainment argument of
Theorem
5.14
(which relies only on the I-D-GRE
and not on the homogeneous form of the dynamics),

\langle\mathcal{C}_{M}(t,P_{\infty}(t)),\,X^{\star}(t)\rangle=0
a.e. and

\langle\mathcal{D}_{M}(k,P_{\infty}(t_{k}^{+}),P_{\infty}(t_{k}^{-})),\,X^{\star}(t_{k}^{-})\rangle=0

for every
k
. Substituting into (
95
) gives

\displaystyle-\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\langle M(t),\,X(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t-\sum_{k:\,t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T}\langle M_{k},\,EX(t_{k}^{-})E^{*}\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad=\langle-P_{\infty}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle-\langle-P_{\infty}(t_{0}+T),\,EX(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\rangle-R_{T}(P_{\infty}),

Dividing by
T
and letting
T\to\infty
, the boundary terms vanish
(boundedness and
1/T
), and
-R_{T}(P_{\infty})/T\to\bar{V}_{a}^{\star}
by
hypothesis. Hence

\lim_{T\to\infty}V_{a}^{T}(t_{0},X_{1}^{0};X^{\star})/T=\bar{V}_{a}^{\star}
, the
supremum is achieved and
\bar{V}_{a}^{\star}(X_{1}^{0})=\bar{V}_{a}^{\star}
,
proving (
a
) and (
b
).

Uniqueness of
P_{\infty}
.

If
\tilde{P}
is another bounded solution of the infinite-horizon
I-D-GRE, the same argument gives

\bar{V}_{a}^{\star}=\lim_{T}-R_{T}(\tilde{P})/T
as well. But
\tilde{P}\equiv P_{\infty}
already by Theorem
5.14
(the
homogeneous infinite-horizon I-D-GRE has a unique bounded solution).
This proves (
c
).
∎

The bounded-trajectory claim used in the upper bound is recorded as
the following lemma, the dissipativity counterpart of
Lemma
3.30
.

Lemma 5.30

(Bounded second moment under forcing, dissipativity)
.

Under (A1) and (A2), for any admissible trajectory
X(\cdot)

of (
46
) with stabilizing feedback
K_{s}
from
(A1), there exists a constant
C>0
(depending only on
K_{s}
,

\beta,\alpha,\rho
from (A1), and the bounds in (A2), and

\|X_{1}^{0}\|_{*}
) such that
\|EX(t)E^{*}\|_{*}\leq C
for all
t\geq t_{0}
.

Proof.

Identical to Lemma
3.30
: variation-of-constants on
the closed-loop flow-jump system, using the dual decay

\beta e^{-\alpha(t-s)}\rho^{\kappa(t,s)}
of (A1), gives

\|EX(t)E^{*}\|_{*}\leq\beta\|X_{1}^{0}\|_{*}+\beta\alpha^{-1}\sup_{s}\|\mathcal{W}(s)\|_{*}+\beta(1-\rho)^{-1}\sup_{k}\|\mathcal{W}_{k}\|_{*},

where the
(1-\rho)^{-1}
factor comes from the convergent geometric
series in
\rho^{\kappa}
on the jump-sum term. No bound on

\Delta_{k}
is needed.
∎

5.3.5
Necessary condition and existence (infinite horizon)

In contrast to the optimal-control setting of
Section
3.3.4
, where (A1)–(A2) suffice for
existence of an infinite-horizon dual variable through the
coercivity of the cost, the dissipativity setting requires an
additional structural hypothesis: the system itself must be
dissipative with respect to the supply rate
M
. Stability of the
closed-loop homogeneous dynamics under (A1) does
not
imply
dissipativity, because the supply rate
M
may have an arbitrary
sign structure. We therefore take dissipativity (in the form of
uniform finiteness of the homogeneous available storage) as the
starting hypothesis, and derive existence of
P_{\infty}
together
with the long-term average available-storage formula. This is the
genuine necessity direction: the existence of a solution to the
infinite-horizon I-D-GRE is the
conclusion
, not an
assumption.

Theorem 5.31

(Necessary condition and existence, infinite-horizon
average available storage)
.

Suppose the homogeneous system
(
with
\mathcal{W}=0
,

\mathcal{W}_{k}=0
)
is dissipative with respect to
M
, in
the sense that

\sup_{T>0}V_{a}^{T}(t_{0},X_{1}^{0})<\infty\qquad\text{for every }X_{1}^{0}\in\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}.

(99)

Then, under (A1) and (A2):

(a)

Existence and uniqueness.
There exists a unique bounded
piecewise-
C^{1}
solution

P_{\infty}:[t_{0},\infty)\to\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
,

\alpha_{1}I\preceq P_{\infty}\preceq\alpha_{2}I
, of the
infinite-horizon I-D-GRE;
-P_{\infty}
is the smallest storage
function.

(b)

Convergence.

-P_{\infty}
is the Loewner least upper
bound of
\{-P_{T}\}
; under the additional structural condition below,

-P_{T}\to-P_{\infty}
pointwise on
[t_{0},\infty)
and uniformly on
compact subintervals. The convergence is then monotone
non-decreasing under the additional
structural condition that the homogeneous system admits, at
every reachable state, a non-positive-supply continuation

(
a sufficient condition is that the homogeneous I-D-GRE
flow admits an equilibrium; this holds in the LTI case and in
the periodic case
)
.

(c)

Long-term formula.

\bar{V}_{a}^{\star}(X_{1}^{0})=\limsup_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}\!\left[-\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\!\langle P_{\infty}(t),\,\mathcal{W}(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t-\!\!\sum_{k:\,t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T}\!\!\langle P_{\infty}(t_{k}^{+}),\,\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle\right]

(100)

independently of
X_{1}^{0}
. The right-hand side is finite
whenever
\limsup_{T}T^{-1}\#\{k:t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T\}<\infty
.

(d)

Attainment.
The supremum is attained at the closed-loop
extraction trajectory
X^{\star}
with gains

K_{\infty}(t)\in\mathcal{K}_{c}^{M}(t,P_{\infty}(t))
on flow and

K_{k}\in\mathcal{K}_{d}^{M}(k,P_{\infty}(t_{k}^{+}),P_{\infty}(t_{k}^{-}))
at
each jump.

Proof.

Step 1: Existence of
P_{\infty}
from dissipativity.

The dissipativity hypothesis (
99
) is
exactly the homogeneous-dissipativity premise of
Theorem
5.15
. Combined with the boundedness of the
operators from (A2), that theorem provides a unique bounded
piecewise-
C^{1}
solution
P_{\infty}
with

\alpha_{1}I\preceq P_{\infty}\preceq\alpha_{2}I
of the homogeneous
infinite-horizon I-D-GRE;
-P_{\infty}
is smallest storage
function by construction (Willems-type characterisation). Note
that (A1) alone (closed-loop stability of the homogeneous dynamics)
is not used in this step: existence of the storage function is a
property of the supply rate
M
relative to the dynamics, not of
stability.

Step 2: Convergence of
-P_{T}
to
-P_{\infty}
.

Theorem
5.15
establishes that
-P_{\infty}
is the
Loewner least upper bound of the family
\{-P_{T}\}
. Monotone non-decreasing
convergence requires

T\mapsto V_{a}^{T}(t_{0},X_{1}^{0})
to be monotone non-decreasing for every

X_{1}^{0}
, equivalently that any horizon-
T
trajectory can be
prolonged to a horizon-
T^{\prime}
trajectory (
T^{\prime}>T
) without strictly
positive incremental supply. A sufficient structural condition is
the existence of an equilibrium of the homogeneous I-D-GRE
flow at
P_{s}(0^{+})
, which is automatic in the LTI case and in the
periodic case (Theorem
5.35
). In the
general LTV case,
-P_{T}
converges but the convergence need not be
monotone.

Step 3: Long-term average formula.

For any admissible trajectory
X
of (
46
),
identity (
95
) with
P=P_{\infty}
on

[t_{0},t_{0}+T]
reads

\displaystyle-\!\!\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\!\!\langle M(t),\,X(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle-\!\!\sum_{k:\,t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T}\!\!\langle M_{k},\,EX(t_{k}^{-})E^{*}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle-P_{\infty}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle-\langle-P_{\infty}(t_{0}+T),\,EX(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\rangle

\displaystyle\quad-\!\!\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\!\!\langle\mathcal{C}_{M}(t,P_{\infty}),\,X\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t-\!\!\sum_{k:\,t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T}\!\!\langle\mathcal{D}_{M},\,X(t_{k}^{-})\rangle-R_{T}(P_{\infty}),

with
R_{T}(P_{\infty}):=\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\!\langle P_{\infty}(t),\,\mathcal{W}(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\sum_{k:\,t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T}\!\langle P_{\infty}(t_{k}^{+}),\,\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle
.
Since
P_{\infty}
solves the infinite-horizon I-D-GRE,

\mathcal{C}_{M}(t,P_{\infty})\succeq 0
and
\mathcal{D}_{M}\succeq 0
, so
Lemma
2.5
gives
\langle\mathcal{C}_{M},\,X(t)\rangle\geq 0
a.e. and
\langle\mathcal{D}_{M},\,X(t_{k}^{-})\rangle\geq 0
, with equality along the
closed-loop extraction trajectory
X^{\star}
. Hence

\displaystyle-\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\langle M(t),\,X(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t-\sum_{k:\,t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T}\langle M_{k},\,EX(t_{k}^{-})E^{*}\rangle

(101)

\displaystyle\leq\langle-P_{\infty}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle-\langle-P_{\infty}(t_{0}+T),\,EX(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\rangle-R_{T}(P_{\infty}),

with equality at
X^{\star}
.

Step 4: Boundary term is
O(1)
in
T
(uses (A1)).

Lemma
5.30
bounds
\|EX(t)E^{*}\|\leq C_{X}<\infty

uniformly in
t
along any admissible trajectory with the
stabilizing feedback of (A1); its proof uses the geometric factor

\rho^{j}
from the dual decay in (A1) and never invokes a bound on

\Delta_{k}
. Since
P_{\infty}
is bounded by
\alpha_{2}
,

|\langle-P_{\infty}(t_{0}+T),\,EX(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\rangle|\leq\alpha_{2}C_{X}
uniformly
in
T
.

Step 5: Conclusion.

Dividing (
101
) by
T
and taking

\limsup_{T\to\infty}
, both boundary terms vanish, leaving

\limsup_{T}T^{-1}V_{a}^{T}(t_{0},X_{1}^{0};X)\leq\limsup_{T}(-R_{T}(P_{\infty}))/T
.
Taking the supremum over admissible
X
gives

\bar{V}_{a}^{\star}(X_{1}^{0})\leq\limsup_{T}(-R_{T}(P_{\infty}))/T
. The
closed-loop extraction trajectory
X^{\star}

saturates (
101
), so the reverse inequality
holds and equality is (
100
). Independence of

X_{1}^{0}
follows since the right-hand side does not depend on

X_{1}^{0}
.

Step 6: Finiteness.

|R_{T}(P_{\infty})|\leq\alpha_{2}(T\sup_{t}\|\mathcal{W}(t)\|+\#\{k:t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T\}\cdot\sup_{k}\|\mathcal{W}_{k}\|)
, so

T^{-1}|R_{T}(P_{\infty})|
is bounded above whenever

\limsup_{T}T^{-1}\#\{k:t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T\}<\infty
.
∎

Remark 5.32
.

Formula (
100
) characterises
\bar{V}_{a}^{\star}
as
a
\limsup
regardless of any inter-jump regularity. A genuine

\lim
is recovered under additional structural assumptions on

(P_{\infty},\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W}_{k})
, the cleanest case being

T_{p}
-periodicity of the data
(Theorem
5.35
) where both contributions
of
R_{T}(P_{\infty})/T
converge by genuine periodic averaging. In the
LTI specialisation (Corollary
5.40
),
P_{\infty}
is
constant and the formula collapses to

\bar{V}_{a}^{\star}=\mathrm{trace}(-P_{\infty}\mathcal{W})
, again a true limit.
This is the matrix-valued analogue of the classical steady-state
output-covariance /
H_{2}
-norm formula for closed-loop systems with
stable closed-loop dynamics.

5.3.6
Equivalence and dual DLMI (infinite horizon)

The equivalence statement and dual DLMI for the average available
storage are direct consequences of Theorems
5.29

and
5.31
.

Corollary 5.33

(Equivalence, infinite-horizon average available
storage)
.

The infinite-horizon average available-storage
problem (
94
) under (
46
) is
well-posed with
\bar{V}_{a}^{\star}(X_{1}^{0})=\bar{V}_{a}^{\star}
given
by (
97
) if and only if the homogeneous
infinite-horizon I-D-GRE has a bounded piecewise-
C^{1}
solution

P_{\infty}\succeq 0
. The solution is unique.

Proof.

Sufficiency: Theorem
5.29
.
Necessity: Theorem
5.31
.
Uniqueness: Theorem
5.29
(
c
).
∎

The DLMI characterisation expresses
\bar{V}_{a}^{\star}
as the infimum
of a linear-in-
P
functional over the DLMI feasibility set. Define
the
negative-forcing pairing

\bar{\Phi}^{M}(P):=\limsup_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}\!\left[-\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\!\langle P(t),\,\mathcal{W}(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t-\!\!\sum_{k:\,t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T}\!\!\langle P(t_{k}^{+}),\,\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle\right],

(102)

which is well-defined and finite for any bounded
P
under (A2).
The functional
\bar{\Phi}^{M}:\mathbb{H}^{n}\text{-valued bounded functions}\to\mathbb{R}

is linear and
order-reversing
: if
P\preceq P^{\prime}
pointwise
then
\bar{\Phi}^{M}(P^{\prime})\leq\bar{\Phi}^{M}(P)
(since
\mathcal{W}(t)\succeq 0

and
\mathcal{W}_{k}\succeq 0
).

Theorem 5.34

(Dual DLMI, infinite-horizon average available storage)
.

Under (A1)–(A3),

\bar{V}_{a}^{\star}=\inf_{P}\;\bar{\Phi}^{M}(P)\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\mathcal{C}_{M}(t,P(t))\succeq 0,\;\mathcal{D}_{M}(k,P(t_{k}^{+}),P(t_{k}^{-}))\succeq 0,\;P\text{ bounded};

(103)

the infimum is attained at
P_{\infty}
.

Proof.

Step 1: every feasible
P
is an upper bound for

\bar{V}_{a}^{\star}
.

Let
P
be any feasible function for (
103
). By the
proof of Theorem
5.14
,
P\preceq P_{\infty}

pointwise (the infinite-horizon I-D-GRE solution is the largest
feasible solution; equivalently
-P_{\infty}
is the smallest storage
function). By the order-reversal of
\bar{\Phi}^{M}
,

\bar{\Phi}^{M}(P_{\infty})\leq\bar{\Phi}^{M}(P)
for every feasible
P
, so

\inf_{P}\bar{\Phi}^{M}(P)\geq\bar{\Phi}^{M}(P_{\infty})
.

A direct argument that does not invoke the comparison

P\preceq P_{\infty}
proceeds as follows. For any admissible
trajectory
X
of (
46
) and any feasible
P
,
identity (
95
) combined with the LMI constraints

\mathcal{C}_{M}\succeq 0
and
\mathcal{D}_{M}\succeq 0
gives

\displaystyle-\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\langle M(t),\,X(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t-\sum_{k:\,t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T}\langle M_{k},\,EX(t_{k}^{-})E^{*}\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\langle-P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle-\langle-P(t_{0}+T),\,EX(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\rangle-R_{T}(P),

where
R_{T}(P):=\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\!\langle P(t),\,\mathcal{W}(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\sum_{k:\,t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T}\!\langle P(t_{k}^{+}),\,\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle
.
Dividing by
T
, the boundary terms vanish (boundedness of
P
and
of
EXE^{*}
by Lemma
5.30
, divided by
T
), so

\limsup_{T}T^{-1}V_{a}^{T}(t_{0},X_{1}^{0};X)\leq\bar{\Phi}^{M}(P)
. Taking the
supremum over
X
on the left gives

\bar{V}_{a}^{\star}(X_{1}^{0})\leq\bar{\Phi}^{M}(P)
. Since this holds for every
feasible
P
and
\bar{V}_{a}^{\star}(X_{1}^{0})=\bar{V}_{a}^{\star}
,

\bar{V}_{a}^{\star}\leq\inf_{P}\bar{\Phi}^{M}(P)
.

Step 2:
P_{\infty}
is feasible and attains the
bound.

P_{\infty}
satisfies the DLMI constraints by the I-D-GRE
(Lemma
3.4
) and is bounded. By
Theorem
5.31
,
\bar{\Phi}^{M}(P_{\infty})=\bar{V}_{a}^{\star}
,
so
\inf_{P}\bar{\Phi}^{M}(P)\leq\bar{\Phi}^{M}(P_{\infty})=\bar{V}_{a}^{\star}
.

Combining Steps 1 and 2:
\bar{V}_{a}^{\star}=\inf_{P}\bar{\Phi}^{M}(P)
,
attained at
P_{\infty}
.
∎

5.3.7
Dwell-time conditions

The infinite-horizon I-D-GRE solution
P_{\infty}
used in
Sections
5.3.4
–
5.3.6
is
non-causal: its value at time
t
depends on the future jump times.
We now combine the average available-storage
formula (
100
) with the causal dwell-time
policies of Section
5.2.5
(periodic, MDT, RDT) to
obtain causal characterisations and bounds for
\bar{V}_{a}^{\star}
.
The construction parallels Section
3.3.6
with the
sign convention reversed:
\bar{\Phi}^{M}
in place of
\Phi
,
-P
in
place of
P
as the storage-function coefficient, and the
dissipativity Geromel–Colaneri condition

\mathcal{C}_{M}^{0}(P_{s}(T_{\min}))\succeq 0

(Theorem
5.20
) in place of

\mathcal{C}_{Z}^{0}\preceq 0
. The structural assumptions on

\mathcal{F},\mathcal{J},M,M_{k}
in the three scenarios are inherited
verbatim from Section
5.2.5
; the only additions are
the corresponding assumptions on the forcing data

\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W}_{k}
.

Periodic impulses.

Adopt the periodicity hypothesis of Theorem
5.18
:

\Delta_{k}\equiv T_{p}
,
\mathcal{F}(\cdot,X)
and
M(\cdot)
are

T_{p}
-periodic, and
\mathcal{J}(k,\cdot)
,
M_{k}
are independent
of
k
. For the forcing data, assume in addition that

\mathcal{W}(\cdot)
is
T_{p}
-periodic and that
\mathcal{W}_{k}
is
independent of
k
, i.e.,

\mathcal{W}_{k}\equiv\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{jp}}
.

Theorem 5.35

(Causal long-term average available storage under
periodic impulses)
.

Under
(A1)–(A2)
and the periodicity assumption above

(
note that
(A3)
is automatic from

\Delta_{k}\equiv T_{p}
)
, the long-term average available
storage rate (
94
) is given by the closed-form
expression

\bar{V}_{a}^{\star}=\frac{1}{T_{p}}\!\left[-\int_{0}^{T_{p}}\!\langle P_{\infty}(\tau),\,\mathcal{W}(\tau)\rangle\,d\tau-\langle P_{\infty}(0^{+}),\,\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{jp}}\rangle\right],

(104)

where
P_{\infty}:[0,T_{p}]\to\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
is the unique
T_{p}
-periodic
piecewise-
C^{1}
solution of the periodic I-D-GRE, attained by the
causal policy
K(\tau)\in\mathcal{K}_{c}^{M}(\tau,P_{\infty}(\tau))
on
flow and
K_{k}\in\mathcal{K}_{d}^{M}(P_{\infty}(0^{+}),P_{\infty}(T_{p}^{-}))
at
each jump.

Proof.

By Theorem
5.18
applied to the homogeneous
dynamics, the periodic I-D-GRE has a unique
T_{p}
-periodic solution

P_{\infty}
. Its
T_{p}
-periodic extension to
[t_{0},\infty)

coincides with the infinite-horizon dual variable of
Theorem
5.31
, so (
100
) applies
and the
\limsup
becomes a true
\lim
by genuine periodic
averaging: the flow integral over
[t_{0},t_{0}+T]
contains
\lfloor T/T_{p}\rfloor
complete periods plus a boundary remainder, so its
time-average converges to

T_{p}^{-1}\int_{0}^{T_{p}}\langle P_{\infty}(\tau),\,\mathcal{W}(\tau)\rangle\,d\tau
;
the jump sum contains
\lfloor T/T_{p}\rfloor
jumps each contributing

\langle P_{\infty}(0^{+}),\,\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{jp}}\rangle
, with time-average

T_{p}^{-1}\langle P_{\infty}(0^{+}),\,\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{jp}}\rangle
. Negating
and combining yields (
104
). The
causal policy is the same as in Theorem
5.18
and
is the optimal extraction policy by
Theorem
5.29
(
b
).
∎

Minimum dwell-time.

Adopt the timer-dependence hypothesis of Theorem
5.20
:

\Delta_{k}\geq T_{\min}>0
, the flow data depend only on the timer

\tau=t-t_{k}
, and the time-invariance
condition (
43
) holds for

\mathcal{F},M,\mathcal{J},M_{k}
. For the forcing data, assume in
addition that
\mathcal{W}
depends only on the timer, with

\mathcal{W}(\tau)\equiv\mathcal{W}_{\infty}\;\;\text{for }\tau\geq T_{\min},\qquad\mathcal{W}_{k}\equiv\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{jp}}\;\;\text{for all }k.

(105)

Theorem 5.36

(Causal long-term available-storage lower bound
under MDT)
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
5.20

and (
105
), suppose moreover
that the jump rate

\bar{\nu}:=\limsup_{T\to\infty}T^{-1}\#\{k:t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T\}
is finite

(
so
\bar{\nu}\leq 1/T_{\min}
)
and is in fact a

\lim
. Then the causal policy of Theorem
5.20

achieves a long-term average available storage rate bounded below
by

\bar{V}_{a}^{\star}\geq-\bar{\nu}\!\int_{0}^{T_{\min}}\!\langle P_{s}(\tau),\,\mathcal{W}(\tau)\rangle\,d\tau-(1-\bar{\nu}T_{\min})\langle P_{s}(T_{\min}),\,\mathcal{W}_{\infty}\rangle-\bar{\nu}\langle P_{s}(0^{+}),\,\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{jp}}\rangle,

(106)

where
P_{s}:[0,T_{\min}]\to\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
is as in
Theorem
5.20
. The condition is verifiable on

[0,T_{\min}]
alone.

Proof.

Extend
P_{s}
to
\bar{P}:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
by

\bar{P}(\tau)=P_{s}(\tau)
for
\tau\leq T_{\min}
and

\bar{P}(\tau)=P_{s}(T_{\min})
for
\tau>T_{\min}
as in the proof of
Theorem
5.20
. Apply the forced key
identity (
95
) on
[t_{0},t_{0}+T]
with
\bar{P}

extended along the timer, evaluating at the closed-loop extraction
trajectory
X^{\star}
with feedback in

\mathcal{K}_{c}^{M},\mathcal{K}_{d}^{M}
; denote by
\tau(t)
the timer
value at time
t
:

\displaystyle-\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\!\!\!\!\!\langle M,\,X^{\star}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle-\!\!\sum_{k:\,t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T}\!\!\langle M_{k},\,EX^{\star}(t_{k}^{-})E^{*}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle-\bar{P}(0^{+}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle-\langle-\bar{P}(\tau(t_{0}+T)),\,EX^{\star}(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\rangle

\displaystyle\quad-\underbrace{\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\!\langle\mathcal{C}_{M}(\bar{P}),\,X^{\star}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t}_{\geq\,0\text{ by Thm~\ref{th:diss:MDT}~(iii)}}-\underbrace{\sum_{k:\,t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T}\!\!\langle\mathcal{D}_{M},\,X^{\star}(t_{k}^{-})\rangle}_{=\,0\text{ at the optimal extraction}}

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\!\langle\bar{P}(\tau(t)),\,\mathcal{W}(\tau(t))\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t-\!\!\sum_{k:\,t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T}\!\!\langle\bar{P}(0^{+}),\,\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{jp}}\rangle.

On the inter-jump interval
(t_{k},t_{k+1})
with timer

\tau=t-t_{k}\in(0,T_{\min})
,
\mathcal{C}_{M}(\tau,P_{s}(\tau))=0
by
the flow I-D-GRE; on
\tau>T_{\min}
where
\bar{P}=P_{s}(T_{\min})

is constant and the data are time-invariant,

\mathcal{C}_{M}(\bar{P})=\mathcal{C}_{M}^{0}(P_{s}(T_{\min}))\succeq 0
by
Theorem
5.20
(iii); combined with
X^{\star}\succeq 0

and Lemma
2.5
, the flow inner product is non-negative
on the tail, so the underbraced flow integral is non-negative
(reversed sign from the OC case). At each
t_{k}
the saddle gain
gives
\langle\mathcal{D}_{M},\,X^{\star}(t_{k}^{-})\rangle=0
. Dropping the
non-negative flow contribution and the non-negative boundary term

\langle-\bar{P}(\tau(t_{0}+T)),\,EX^{\star}(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\rangle\geq 0
(since

-\bar{P}\preceq 0
and
EX^{\star}E^{*}\succeq 0
) gives the inequality

\displaystyle-\int\langle M,\,X^{\star}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t-\sum\langle M_{k},\,EX^{\star}(t_{k}^{-})E^{*}\rangle

\displaystyle\geq\langle-\bar{P}(0^{+}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\!\langle\bar{P}(\tau(t)),\,\mathcal{W}(\tau(t))\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t-\!\!\sum_{k:\,t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T}\!\!\langle\bar{P}(0^{+}),\,\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{jp}}\rangle.

Dividing by
T
and taking
T\to\infty
, the initial term vanishes.
The time-average of
\langle\bar{P}(\tau(t)),\,\mathcal{W}(\tau(t))\rangle

decomposes according to the timer distribution: under MDT, in each
inter-jump interval of length
\Delta_{k}\geq T_{\min}
the timer
spends time
T_{\min}
on
[0,T_{\min}]
(where
\bar{P}=P_{s}
and

\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{W}(\tau)
vary) and time
\Delta_{k}-T_{\min}

on the frozen region (where
\bar{P}=P_{s}(T_{\min})
and

\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{W}_{\infty}
). With average jump rate

\bar{\nu}
, the fraction of time on
[0,T_{\min}]
is

\bar{\nu}T_{\min}
and on the frozen region is
1-\bar{\nu}T_{\min}
,
giving (
106
).
∎

Remark 5.37
.

For the constant dwell-time
\Delta_{k}\equiv T_{\min}
, the jump rate

\bar{\nu}=1/T_{\min}
and the frozen contribution

-(1-\bar{\nu}T_{\min})\langle P_{s}(T_{\min}),\,\mathcal{W}_{\infty}\rangle

vanishes, so (
106
) recovers the periodic
formula (
104
) (with constant data and

T_{p}=T_{\min}
). For larger jump rates (
\bar{\nu}<1/T_{\min}
), the
frozen contribution accounts for the time spent past the minimum
dwell-time.

Range dwell-time.

Adopt the timer-dependence hypothesis of Theorem
5.23
:

\Delta_{k}\in[T_{\min},T_{\max}]
and the flow and jump data depend
only on the timer
\tau\in[0,T_{\max}]
as
in (
43
) extended to
[0,T_{\max}]

(so that
\mathcal{F}(\tau,\cdot),M(\tau)
are defined for

\tau\in[0,T_{\max}]
and
\mathcal{J},M_{k}
are

k
-independent). For the forcing data, assume similarly
that
\mathcal{W}
depends only on the timer

\tau\in[0,T_{\max}]
and that
\mathcal{W}_{k}
is

k
-independent:
\mathcal{W}_{k}\equiv\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{jp}}
.

Theorem 5.38

(Causal long-term available-storage lower bound under
RDT)
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
5.23
and the
timer-dependence hypotheses above, suppose moreover that the
empirical occupation measure of the inter-jump sequence

\{\Delta_{k}\}
converges weakly to a probability measure
\mu
on

[T_{\min},T_{\max}]
as
T\to\infty
, and that the jump rate

\bar{\nu}=(\int_{[T_{\min},T_{\max}]}\Delta\,\mu(d\Delta))^{-1}

is finite. Then the causal policy of Theorem
5.23

achieves a long-term average available storage rate bounded below
by

\bar{V}_{a}^{\star}\geq-\bar{\nu}\!\int_{T_{\min}}^{T_{\max}}\!\!\biggl(\int_{0}^{\Delta}\!\langle P_{s}(\tau),\,\mathcal{W}(\tau)\rangle\,d\tau\biggr)\mu(d\Delta)-\bar{\nu}\langle P_{s}(0^{+}),\,\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{jp}}\rangle,

(107)

where
P_{s}:[0,T_{\max}]\to\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
is as in
Theorem
5.23
.

Proof.

Apply the forced key identity (
95
) with
P=P_{s}

over
[t_{0},t_{0}+T]
, evaluating at the closed-loop extraction
trajectory
X^{\star}
. The
\mathcal{C}_{M}
and
\mathcal{D}_{M}

contributions vanish identically at the saddle gain by the
conditions of Theorem
5.23
, so

\displaystyle-\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\!\langle M,\,X^{\star}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle-\!\!\sum_{k:\,t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T}\!\!\langle M_{k},\,EX^{\star}(t_{k}^{-})E^{*}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle-P_{s}(0^{+}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle-\langle-P_{s}(\tau(t_{0}+T)),\,EX^{\star}(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\rangle

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\!\langle P_{s}(\tau(t)),\,\mathcal{W}(\tau(t))\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t-\!\!\sum_{k:\,t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T}\!\!\langle P_{s}(0^{+}),\,\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{jp}}\rangle.

Dropping the non-negative boundary term

\langle-P_{s}(\tau(t_{0}+T)),\,EX^{\star}(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\rangle\geq 0
gives a
\geq

inequality. Dividing by
T
and letting
T\to\infty
, the initial
term vanishes. The jump-sum time-average is

\bar{\nu}\langle P_{s}(0^{+}),\,\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{jp}}\rangle
. For the flow
time-average, in an inter-jump interval of length
\Delta_{k}
the
timer traverses
[0,\Delta_{k}]
, contributing

\int_{0}^{\Delta_{k}}\langle P_{s}(\tau),\,\mathcal{W}(\tau)\rangle\,d\tau
to the
integral; dividing by
T
and using

\bar{\nu}=(\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\Delta])^{-1}
gives the
\mu
-integral
in (
107
).
∎

Remark 5.39
.

When
\mu
is concentrated at a single point
\Delta_{k}\equiv T_{p}

with
T_{\min}\leq T_{p}\leq T_{\max}
, the
bound (
107
) becomes equality and reduces
to (
104
). For unknown
\mu
, the
worst case over admissible distributions on
[T_{\min},T_{\max}]

gives a robust lower bound on
\bar{V}_{a}^{\star}
.

5.3.8
Continuous- and discrete-time corollaries

The continuous-time and discrete-time specialisations follow by
setting
N_{T}=0
or
\mathcal{F}=0
.

Corollary 5.40

(Continuous-time average available storage)
.

Set
N_{T}=0
(no jumps), so
\mathcal{W}_{k}
is irrelevant. Under
the continuous-time forced flow

E\dot{X}E^{*}=\mathcal{F}(t,X(t))+\mathcal{W}(t)
and (A1)–(A2),

\bar{V}_{a}^{\star}=\limsup_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}\!\left[-\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\!\langle P_{\infty}(t),\,\mathcal{W}(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t\right],

(108)

where
P_{\infty}
is the unique bounded
C^{1}
solution of the
continuous-time infinite-horizon D-GRE
(Corollary
5.25
). The dual DLMI is

\bar{V}_{a}^{\star}=\inf_{P}\;\limsup_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}\!\!\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\!\!\langle-P(t),\,\mathcal{W}(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\mathcal{C}_{M}(t,P(t))\succeq 0,\;P\text{ bounded}.

In the LTI case (
\mathcal{F},M,\mathcal{W}
time-invariant),

P_{\infty}
is constant and (
108
) reduces to

\bar{V}_{a}^{\star}=\langle-P_{\infty},\,\mathcal{W}\rangle=\mathrm{trace}(-P_{\infty}\mathcal{W}),

(109)

recovering the classical steady-state output-covariance /
H_{2}
-norm
formula when
\mathcal{W}=BB^{*}
is a process-noise covariance.

Corollary 5.41

(Discrete-time average available storage)
.

Apply with
\mathcal{F}=0
,
M=0
on flow,
t_{k}=k_{0}+k
,

\mathcal{J}=\mathcal{D}
,
\mathcal{W}=0
on flow. Under the
discrete-time forced jump

EX(k+1)E^{*}=\mathcal{D}(k,X(k))+\mathcal{W}_{k}
and the
discrete-time analogues of (A1)–(A2),

\bar{V}_{a}^{\star}=\limsup_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+N-1}\langle-P_{\infty}(k+1),\,\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle,

(110)

where
P_{\infty}
satisfies the algebraic DT-D-GRE
(Corollary
5.26
). In the LTI case
(
\mathcal{D},M_{k},\mathcal{W}_{k}
constant),
P_{\infty}
is constant
and (
110
) reduces to

\bar{V}_{a}^{\star}=\mathrm{trace}(-P_{\infty}\mathcal{W})
, again recovering the
classical steady-state covariance formula.

6
Minimax Optimal Control: Zero-Sum Differential Games

This section develops the zero-sum game counterpart of the optimal
control theory of Section
3
. The impulsive system now has

two
input channels: a minimizing player (the controller, with
input
X_{2}^{u}
,
X_{3}^{u}
) and a maximizing player (the disturbance,
with input
X_{2}^{w}
,
X_{3}^{w}
). The game value is the minimax cost,
and the saddle-point policy is the pair that simultaneously minimizes
for the controller and maximizes for the disturbance.

The analysis is built on the
impulsive HJI-GRE
, the
game-theoretic analogue of the I-GRE. Under Isaacs’
condition (
120
), the minimax and maximin coincide
and the game has a saddle-point solution characterized by the
HJI matrix
\mathcal{I}
and its (indefinite) Isaacs block

\mathcal{G}
. The key structural difference from the optimal control
case is that the pivot block
\mathcal{G}
is
indefinite
: the
controller minimizes in the
\mathcal{I}_{uu}
block (
\succeq 0
) while
the disturbance maximizes in the
\mathcal{I}_{ww}
block (
\preceq 0
).
Consequently the saddle is handled by the
generalized (indefinite)
Schur complement
of
\mathcal{G}
in
\mathcal{I}
, and the dual
characterization (Theorem
6.10
) is a game Riccati

inequality
which, in contrast to the optimal control case,
retains a Schur-complement term because the pivot
\mathcal{G}
has
mixed inertia.

The finite-horizon game theory is in Section
6.1
, the
infinite-horizon game under LTV conditions in
Section
6.2
, and the long-term average game value in
Section
6.4
; causal dwell-time policies and
continuous-time and discrete-time corollaries appear throughout.
Connections to
H_{\infty}
and full-information games are discussed in
Section
6.3
.

6.1
Finite-horizon game

We study the zero-sum game on the bounded horizon
[t_{0},t_{0}+T]
for a
fixed
T>0
. The decision variable is the full trajectory

X(\cdot)\in\mathbb{H}^{n+m}_{\succeq 0}
, whose free blocks are now split between a

minimizing
player (the controller) and a
maximizing

player (the disturbance). We introduce the augmented state, the game
cost, and the minimax problem in Section
6.1.1
, the HJI
operators and the I-HJI-GRE in Section
6.1.3
, and the
sufficient, necessary, and dual conditions in
Theorems
6.7
–
6.10
.

6.1.1
Augmented state, partition, and problem formulation

The setting of this section extends the optimal control framework of
Section
3
by splitting the free block
E_{\bot}
into two
competing parts: a
minimizing block
of size
m_{u}
and a

maximizing block
of size
m_{w}
, with
m=m_{u}+m_{w}
.
Define the selectors

E_{u}:=\bigl[\,0_{m_{u}\times n}\;\;I_{m_{u}}\;\;0_{m_{u}\times m_{w}}\,\bigr]\in\mathbb{C}^{m_{u}\times(n+m)},\qquad E_{w}:=\bigl[\,0_{m_{w}\times n}\;\;0_{m_{w}\times m_{u}}\;\;I_{m_{w}}\,\bigr]\in\mathbb{C}^{m_{w}\times(n+m)},

(111)

so that
E_{\bot}=\begin{bmatrix}E_{u}\\
E_{w}\end{bmatrix}
. The extended Hermitian matrix

X\in\mathbb{H}^{n+m}_{\succeq 0}
is now partitioned as

X=\begin{bmatrix}X_{1}&X_{12}^{*}&X_{13}^{*}\\
X_{12}&X_{22}&X_{23}^{*}\\
X_{13}&X_{23}&X_{33}\end{bmatrix},

(112)

where
X_{1}=EXE^{*}\in\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
(state, constrained by dynamics),

X_{12}=E_{u}XE^{*}
and
X_{22}=E_{u}XE_{u}^{*}
(control blocks,
minimizer-controlled),
X_{13}=E_{w}XE^{*}
,
X_{23}=E_{w}XE_{u}^{*}
, and

X_{33}=E_{w}XE_{w}^{*}
(disturbance blocks, maximizer-controlled).
The blocks
X_{22}
and
X_{33}
are the self-adjoint free blocks
associated with the minimizer and maximizer respectively, and
X_{23}

is their cross-block.

Cost function.

For cost matrix

Z=\bigl[\begin{smallmatrix}Q&S_{u}&S_{w}\\
S_{u}^{*}&R_{u}&R_{uw}\\
S_{w}^{*}&R_{uw}^{*}&{-R_{w}}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr]

with
Q\succeq 0
,
R_{u}\succ 0
,
R_{w}\succ 0
(the negative sign on

R_{w}
reflects the maximizer’s objective), and with the

controller cost block

Z_{c}:=\bigl[\begin{smallmatrix}Q&S_{u}\\
S_{u}^{*}&R_{u}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr]\succeq 0

(equivalently
Q\succeq S_{u}{R_{u}}^{\dagger}S_{u}^{*}
; this holds automatically when

S_{u}=0
, as in the
H_{\infty}
setting), the finite-horizon cost is

J_{T}(X,X_{1}^{0}):=\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\langle Z(t),\,X(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\sum_{k}\langle Z_{k},\,X(t_{k}^{-})\rangle+\langle Z_{T},\,EX(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\rangle,

(113)

which is linear in
X
. The negative sign on
R_{w}
in the
(ww)
-block
reflects that the maximizer seeks to increase the cost, hence a
positive
R_{w}
penalises its own block negatively.

Zero-sum minimax problem.

The minimizer controls
X_{12}
,
X_{22}
(the control blocks), while
the maximizer controls
X_{13}
,
X_{23}
,
X_{33}
(the disturbance
blocks), subject to
X(t)\succeq 0
and the dynamics:
