J_{T}^{\circ}(X_{1}^{0}):=\underset{X_{12},X_{22}}{\min}\;\underset{X_{13},X_{23},X_{33}}{\max}\;J_{T}(X,X_{1}^{0})\quad\text{s.t.~\eqref{eq:sys:IMP},}\;EX(t_{0})E^{*}=X_{1}^{0}.

(114)

When the minimax equals the maximin (guaranteed by Isaacs’ condition,
Definition
6.2
below),
J_{T}^{\circ}
is the

saddle-point value
, also called the
game value
.

6.1.2
Composite operators and composite HJI matrix (finite horizon)

The finite-horizon game analysis follows the same structure as
Section
3.1
but with an indefinite Isaacs block that
reflects the opposing objectives of the two players. The HJI operator

\mathcal{I}(t,P(t))
is the game analogue of
\mathcal{C}_{Z}(t,P(t))
;
its bottom-right
(m_{u}+m_{w})
block, the
Isaacs block

\mathcal{G}
, is now
indefinite
(the minimizer’s block

\mathcal{I}_{uu}\succeq 0
, the maximizer’s block

\mathcal{I}_{ww}\preceq 0
), and is the central quantity. Isaacs’
condition is an inertia condition on
\mathcal{G}
ensuring the
existence of a saddle-point, and the I-HJI-GRE, the generalized
Schur complement of
\mathcal{G}
in
\mathcal{I}
set to zero, is
the resulting Riccati equation.

For piecewise-
C^{1}

P:[t_{0},t_{0}+T]\to\mathbb{H}^{n}
, define the

HJI flow operator

\mathcal{I}(t,P(t)):=E^{*}\dot{P}(t)E+\mathcal{F}^{*}(t,P(t))+Z(t)\in\mathbb{H}^{n+m},

(115)

blocked conformally with (
112
) as

\mathcal{I}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathcal{I}_{11}&\mathcal{I}_{1u}&\mathcal{I}_{1w}\\
\mathcal{I}_{1u}^{*}&\mathcal{I}_{uu}&\mathcal{I}_{uw}\\
\mathcal{I}_{1w}^{*}&\mathcal{I}_{uw}^{*}&\mathcal{I}_{ww}\end{bmatrix},

(116)

and the
HJI jump operator

\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}(k,P(t_{k}^{+}),P(t_{k}^{-})):=\mathcal{J}^{*}(k,P(t_{k}^{+}))-E^{*}P(t_{k}^{-})E+Z_{k},

(117)

blocked analogously as

\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}_{11}&\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}_{1u}&\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}_{1w}\\
\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}*}_{1u}&\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}_{uu}&\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}_{uw}\\
\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}*}_{1w}&\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}*}_{uw}&\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}_{ww}\end{bmatrix}.

(118)

Remark 6.1
.

The HJI operator (
115
) is structurally identical to
the flow operator (
10
) and the dissipativity
operator (
5.1.2
), but now the cost matrix
Z
has the
mixed sign structure
-R_{w}\prec 0
in the
(ww)
-block. The key
identity (Proposition
3.1
) holds verbatim with

\mathcal{C}_{Z}(t,P(t))\leftarrow\mathcal{I}(t,P(t))
.

6.1.3
Isaacs’ condition and the I-HJI-GRE (finite horizon)

Definition 6.2

(Generalized Isaacs’ condition and Isaacs block)
.

Define the
Isaacs block
as the bottom-right

(m_{u}+m_{w})\times(m_{u}+m_{w})
block of
\mathcal{I}
,

\mathcal{G}(t,P(t)):=\begin{bmatrix}\mathcal{I}_{uu}(t,P(t))&\mathcal{I}_{uw}(t,P(t))\\
\mathcal{I}_{uw}(t,P(t))^{*}&\mathcal{I}_{ww}(t,P(t))\end{bmatrix},

(119)

and the selection block

\mathcal{I}_{1g}:=\begin{bmatrix}\mathcal{I}_{1u}&\mathcal{I}_{1w}\end{bmatrix}
.
Unlike the optimal control pivot
(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}
, the Isaacs block

\mathcal{G}
is
indefinite
.
Isaacs’ condition
, a
saddle-inertia condition on
\mathcal{G}
, holds at
(t,P(t))
if

\mathcal{I}_{uu}\succeq 0,\quad\mathcal{I}_{ww}\preceq 0,\quad\mathcal{I}_{ww}-\mathcal{I}_{uw}^{*}{\mathcal{I}_{uu}}^{\dagger}\mathcal{I}_{uw}\preceq 0,\quad\mathcal{I}_{uu}-\mathcal{I}_{uw}{\mathcal{I}_{ww}}^{\dagger}\mathcal{I}_{uw}^{*}\succeq 0,

(120)

together with the range conditions

\operatorname{Im}(\mathcal{I}_{uw})\subseteq\operatorname{Im}(\mathcal{I}_{uu})

and

\operatorname{Im}(\mathcal{I}_{uw}^{*})\subseteq\operatorname{Im}(\mathcal{I}_{ww})
.
Equivalently, on its range
\mathcal{G}
has inertia
(m_{u},0,m_{w})
:

m_{u}
nonnegative directions (the minimizer’s) and
m_{w}
nonpositive
directions (the maximizer’s). These conditions allow
\mathcal{I}_{uu}

and
\mathcal{I}_{ww}
to be singular, in analogy with the optimal
control condition
(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}\succeq 0
. When

\mathcal{I}_{uu}\succ 0
and
\mathcal{I}_{ww}\prec 0
,
\mathcal{G}
is
nonsingular with exactly
m_{u}
positive and
m_{w}
negative eigenvalues,
the range conditions hold automatically, and Isaacs’ condition reduces
to
\mathcal{I}_{uu}\succ 0
,
\mathcal{I}_{ww}\prec 0
.

Remark 6.3
.

Write
X=UU^{*}
with
U=[U_{1}^{*}\ U_{u}^{*}\ U_{w}^{*}]^{*}
. The generalized
Schur identity (the algebraic part of Lemma
2.5
, which
requires only the compatibility/range condition and
not

\mathcal{G}\succeq 0
) gives

\langle\mathcal{I},\,UU^{*}\rangle=\langle\mathcal{I}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G},\,U_{1}U_{1}^{*}\rangle+\langle\mathcal{G},\,WW^{*}\rangle,\qquad W:={\mathcal{G}}^{\dagger}\begin{bmatrix}\mathcal{I}_{1u}^{*}\\
\mathcal{I}_{1w}^{*}\end{bmatrix}U_{1}+\begin{bmatrix}U_{u}\\
U_{w}\end{bmatrix},

(121)

where
\mathcal{I}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{I}_{11}-\mathcal{I}_{1g}{\mathcal{G}}^{\dagger}\mathcal{I}_{1g}^{*}

is the generalized Schur complement of the
indefinite
block

\mathcal{G}
. Under Isaacs’ condition,

\langle\mathcal{G},\,WW^{*}\rangle=\operatorname{tr}(W^{*}\mathcal{G}W)
is a genuine

saddle
in the free blocks
(U_{u},U_{w})
: convex in the minimizer’s
block
U_{u}
(since
\mathcal{I}_{uu}\succeq 0
) and concave in the
maximizer’s block
U_{w}
(since

\mathcal{I}_{ww}-\mathcal{I}_{uw}^{*}{\mathcal{I}_{uu}}^{\dagger}\mathcal{I}_{uw}\preceq 0
),
with saddle value
0
attained at
W=0
. Hence

\min_{U_{u}}\max_{U_{w}}\langle\mathcal{I},\,UU^{*}\rangle=\max_{U_{w}}\min_{U_{u}}\langle\mathcal{I},\,UU^{*}\rangle=\langle\mathcal{I}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G},\,U_{1}U_{1}^{*}\rangle:

the saddle of the cost integrand is the indefinite Schur complement

\mathcal{I}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G}
.

Definition 6.4

(Impulsive HJI-GRE)
.

Under Isaacs’ condition (
120
), the
impulsive
Hamilton-Jacobi-Isaacs generalized Riccati equation
(I-HJI-GRE)
for piecewise-
C^{1}

P:[t_{0},t_{0}+T]\to\mathbb{H}^{n}
with
P(t_{0}+T)=Z_{T}

consists of:

•

Flow conditions,
for a.e.
t
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{11}-\begin{bmatrix}\mathcal{I}_{1u}&\mathcal{I}_{1w}\end{bmatrix}\,{\mathcal{G}}^{\dagger}\,\begin{bmatrix}\mathcal{I}_{1u}^{*}\\
\mathcal{I}_{1w}^{*}\end{bmatrix}=0,

(122a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}\,{\mathcal{G}}^{\dagger}\,\begin{bmatrix}\mathcal{I}_{1u}^{*}\\
\mathcal{I}_{1w}^{*}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathcal{I}_{1u}^{*}\\
\mathcal{I}_{1w}^{*}\end{bmatrix},

(122b)

\displaystyle\text{Isaacs' condition~\eqref{eq:Isaacs:gen} holds at }(t,P(t)).

(122c)

Equation (
122a
) is

\mathcal{I}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G}=0
(the generalized Schur
complement of the indefinite block
\mathcal{G}
),
(
122b
) is the compatibility (range) condition, and
(
122c
) is the saddle inertia of
\mathcal{G}
.
Because
\mathcal{G}
is indefinite, these conditions

cannot
be collapsed into a single semidefinite condition

\mathcal{I}\succeq 0
(the optimal control situation); the saddle
inertia must be imposed separately.

•

Jump conditions,
at each
t_{k}
(
k=1,\ldots,N_{T}
):
write
\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}:=\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}(k,P(t_{k}^{+}),P(t_{k}^{-}))

for the HJI jump operator (
117
), blocked as
in (
117
), and define the
Isaacs jump block

as its bottom-right block

\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{j}}(k,P(t_{k}^{+}),P(t_{k}^{-})):=\begin{bmatrix}\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}_{uu}&\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}_{uw}\\
(\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}_{uw})^{*}&\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}_{ww}\end{bmatrix},\qquad\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}_{1g}:=\begin{bmatrix}\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}_{1u}&\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}_{1w}\end{bmatrix}.

(123)

The jump conditions are

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}_{11}-\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}_{1g}\,{(\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{j}})}^{\dagger}\,(\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}_{1g})^{*}=0,

(124a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{j}}\,{(\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{j}})}^{\dagger}\,(\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}_{1g})^{*}=(\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}_{1g})^{*},

(124b)

\displaystyle\text{Isaacs' condition~\eqref{eq:Isaacs:gen} holds for }\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}.

(124c)

Equivalently, (
124a
) is

\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{j}}=0
with
(
124b
) the range condition and (
124c
)
the saddle inertia of
\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{j}}
.

The
saddle-point optimal sets
are, for the flow,

\bigl(\mathcal{K}_{u}^{s}\times\mathcal{K}_{w}^{s}\bigr)(t,P(t)):=\left\{(K_{u},K_{w})\,:\;\mathcal{G}\begin{bmatrix}K_{u}^{*}\\
K_{w}^{*}\end{bmatrix}+\begin{bmatrix}\mathcal{I}_{1u}^{*}\\
\mathcal{I}_{1w}^{*}\end{bmatrix}=0\right\},

(125)

with general element

\begin{bmatrix}K_{u}^{*}\\
K_{w}^{*}\end{bmatrix}=-{\mathcal{G}}^{\dagger}\begin{bmatrix}\mathcal{I}_{1u}^{*}\\
\mathcal{I}_{1w}^{*}\end{bmatrix}+(I-{\mathcal{G}}^{\dagger}\mathcal{G})\,F

for arbitrary
F\in\mathbb{C}^{(m_{u}+m_{w})\times n}
.
At each jump
t_{k}
, the
jump (discrete-time) saddle-point optimal
set
is, with all blocks evaluated at
(k,P(t_{k}^{+}),P(t_{k}^{-}))
,

\bigl(\mathcal{K}_{u}^{s}\times\mathcal{K}_{w}^{s}\bigr)(k,P(t_{k}^{+}),P(t_{k}^{-})):=\left\{(K_{u},K_{w})\,:\;\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{j}}\begin{bmatrix}K_{u}^{*}\\
K_{w}^{*}\end{bmatrix}+(\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}_{1g})^{*}=0\right\},

(126)

with general element

\begin{bmatrix}K_{u}^{*}\\
K_{w}^{*}\end{bmatrix}=-{(\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{j}})}^{\dagger}(\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}_{1g})^{*}+(I-{(\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{j}})}^{\dagger}\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{j}})\,F^{\mathrm{j}}\quad\text{for arbitrary }F^{\mathrm{j}}\in\mathbb{C}^{(m_{u}+m_{w})\times n}.

Remark 6.5
.

When
\mathcal{I}_{uu}\succ 0
and
\mathcal{I}_{ww}\prec 0
,

\mathcal{G}
is nonsingular (
{\mathcal{G}}^{\dagger}=\mathcal{G}^{-1}
),
the compatibility condition (
122b
) holds trivially,
and the saddle-point gains reduce to

(K_{u}^{*},K_{w}^{*})^{\top}=-\mathcal{G}^{-1}[\mathcal{I}_{1u}^{*};\,\mathcal{I}_{1w}^{*}]
.
Note
\mathcal{G}
is indefinite (inertia
(m_{u},0,m_{w})
), so this is
the stationary point of an indefinite quadratic, a saddle, not a
minimum. In the singular case, the kernel term

(I-{\mathcal{G}}^{\dagger}\mathcal{G})F

parametrises all saddle-point strategies not uniquely determined by
the HJI-GRE, exactly as in the optimal control and dissipativity
sections.

Remark 6.6

(Separation and state-feedback)
.

When
\mathcal{I}_{uw}(t,P(t))=0
,
\mathcal{G}
is block-diagonal:

\mathcal{G}=\mathrm{diag}(\mathcal{I}_{uu},\mathcal{I}_{ww})
,
and the saddle-point equations decouple:

\displaystyle K_{u}^{*}

\displaystyle=-{\mathcal{I}_{uu}}^{\dagger}\mathcal{I}_{1u}^{*}\quad(\text{minimizer, depends only on }X_{1}),

\displaystyle K_{w}^{*}

\displaystyle=-{\mathcal{I}_{ww}}^{\dagger}\mathcal{I}_{1w}^{*}\quad(\text{maximizer, depends only on }X_{1}).

In this case the optimal minimizing strategy depends only on the
constrained block
X_{1}
and not on the maximizing free blocks

(X_{13},X_{23},X_{33})
. This is the
separation condition

for the game: the minimizer does not need to observe the maximizing
blocks. It holds whenever
Z_{uw}=0
(no cross-penalty between
the minimizing and maximizing inputs) and there are no cross
terms in the flow adjoint
\mathcal{F}^{*}
. When

\mathcal{I}_{uw}\neq 0
, the optimal minimizer depends on
the maximizer’s current free blocks; the full-information
formulation (where
X_{33}
and
X_{23}
are available) then
applies.

6.1.4
Sufficient condition (finite horizon)

Theorem
6.7
below shows that any piecewise-
C^{1}

solution
P\succeq 0
of the I-HJI-GRE is the unique solution, and that

\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
is simultaneously the saddle-point game value and
the cost attained by the saddle-point policy with gains in the optimal
sets
\mathcal{K}_{u}^{s}
and
\mathcal{K}_{w}^{s}
of (
125
).
The proof applies the key identity (Proposition
3.1
) with

\mathcal{I}
in place of
\mathcal{C}_{Z}
, then uses the genuine
saddle decomposition (
121
) of
Remark
6.3
: along any trajectory the cost integrand
saddles to the indefinite Schur complement

\mathcal{I}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G}
, which vanishes on the I-HJI-GRE.

Theorem 6.7

(Sufficiency of the I-HJI-GRE)
.

Let
T>0
and suppose Isaacs’ condition holds for all

t\in[t_{0},t_{0}+T]
. Suppose there exists a piecewise-
C^{1}

solution
P:[t_{0},t_{0}+T]\to\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
with
P(t_{0}+T)=Z_{T}
satisfying the
I-HJI-GRE. Then:

(a)

The saddle-point value is

J_{T}^{\circ}(X_{1}^{0})=\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
.

(b)

The saddle-point is attained at
(K_{u}^{s},K_{w}^{s})
from

\mathcal{K}_{u}^{s}
and
\mathcal{K}_{w}^{s}
.

(c)

The saddle-point value equals the minimax and the maximin:

J_{T}^{\circ}(X_{1}^{0})=\min_{X_{u}}\max_{X_{w}}J_{T}=\max_{X_{w}}\min_{X_{u}}J_{T}=\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle.

(127)

(d)

The solution
P
is unique.

Proof.

Key identity.

By Proposition
3.1
(with
\mathcal{I}
in place of

\mathcal{F}
) and
P(t_{0}+T)=Z_{T}
:

J_{T}(X,X_{1}^{0})=\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle+\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\langle\mathcal{I}(t,P(t)),\,X\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\sum_{k}\langle\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}(k,P(t_{k}^{+}),P(t_{k}^{-})),\,X(t_{k}^{-})\rangle.

(128)

Saddle-point decomposition.

Write
X=UU^{*}
with
U=[U_{1}^{*}\ U_{u}^{*}\ U_{w}^{*}]^{*}
. Isaacs’ condition
and the I-HJI-GRE give the compatibility condition (
122b
)
and
\mathcal{I}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G}=0
, so the generalized Schur
identity (
121
) reduces to

\langle\mathcal{I},\,UU^{*}\rangle=\underbrace{\langle\mathcal{I}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G},\,U_{1}U_{1}^{*}\rangle}_{=\,0}+\langle\mathcal{G},\,WW^{*}\rangle=\operatorname{tr}(W^{*}\mathcal{G}W),\qquad W=\begin{bmatrix}W_{u}\\
W_{w}\end{bmatrix}={\mathcal{G}}^{\dagger}\begin{bmatrix}\mathcal{I}_{1u}^{*}\\
\mathcal{I}_{1w}^{*}\end{bmatrix}U_{1}+\begin{bmatrix}U_{u}\\
U_{w}\end{bmatrix}.

(129)

By Isaacs’ condition this is a
saddle
in
(U_{u},U_{w})
(equivalently
in
(W_{u},W_{w})
): for fixed
W_{u}
, the maximum over
W_{w}
is

\operatorname{tr}\!\bigl(W_{u}^{*}(\mathcal{I}_{uu}-\mathcal{I}_{uw}{\mathcal{I}_{ww}}^{\dagger}\mathcal{I}_{uw}^{*})W_{u}\bigr)\geq 0
,
attained at
W_{w}=-{\mathcal{I}_{ww}}^{\dagger}\mathcal{I}_{uw}^{*}W_{u}
and equal
to
0
iff
W_{u}=0
; for fixed
W_{w}
, the minimum over
W_{u}
is

\operatorname{tr}\!\bigl(W_{w}^{*}(\mathcal{I}_{ww}-\mathcal{I}_{uw}^{*}{\mathcal{I}_{uu}}^{\dagger}\mathcal{I}_{uw})W_{w}\bigr)\leq 0
,
attained at
W_{u}=-{\mathcal{I}_{uu}}^{\dagger}\mathcal{I}_{uw}W_{w}
and equal to

0
iff
W_{w}=0
. The same statements hold for
\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}

and
\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{j}}
at each jump.

Saddle-point value.

Upper bound on the minimax.
Let the minimizer play the
saddle gain
K_{u}^{s}
of (
125
), i.e.
U_{u}=K_{u}^{s}U_{1}
, which is
the
u
-block of
-{\mathcal{G}}^{\dagger}[\mathcal{I}_{1u}^{*};\mathcal{I}_{1w}^{*}]U_{1}

and hence forces
W_{u}=0
. Then for
every
disturbance choice

X_{w}
,
\langle\mathcal{I},\,X\rangle=\operatorname{tr}(W_{w}^{*}\mathcal{I}_{ww}W_{w})\leq 0

is maximized at
W_{w}=0
with value
0
; the analogous statement holds at
each jump. By the key identity (
128
),

\max_{X_{w}}J_{T}(\cdot,X_{w})=\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
under this minimizer
policy, so
\min_{X_{u}}\max_{X_{w}}J_{T}\leq\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
.

Lower bound on the maximin.
Symmetrically, let the maximizer play

K_{w}^{s}
, forcing
W_{w}=0
. Then for every
X_{u}
,

\langle\mathcal{I},\,X\rangle=\operatorname{tr}(W_{u}^{*}\mathcal{I}_{uu}W_{u})\geq 0
is
minimized at
W_{u}=0
with value
0
, so

\min_{X_{u}}J_{T}(\cdot,X_{w}^{\circ})=\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
and

\max_{X_{w}}\min_{X_{u}}J_{T}\geq\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
.

Since
\min_{X_{u}}\max_{X_{w}}J_{T}\geq\max_{X_{w}}\min_{X_{u}}J_{T}
always, the two
bounds force all of (
127
) to coincide with

\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
.

Attainment.

At the saddle-point gains
(K_{u}^{s},K_{w}^{s})
from (
125
),

W=0
(since
[U_{u};U_{w}]=-{\mathcal{G}}^{\dagger}[\mathcal{I}_{1u}^{*};\mathcal{I}_{1w}^{*}]U_{1}
), so

\langle\mathcal{I},\,X^{\circ}\rangle=0
at each flow instant and at each
jump. Identity (
128
) then gives

J_{T}(X^{\circ},X_{1}^{0})=\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle=J_{T}^{\circ}(X_{1}^{0})
.

Positivity and uniqueness.

Since
P\succeq 0
by hypothesis,

J_{T}^{\circ}(X_{1}^{0})=\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle\geq 0
for every
X_{1}^{0}\succeq 0
.
Uniqueness follows from the
same inner-product argument as Theorem
3.5
.
∎

6.1.5
Necessary condition (finite horizon)

Conversely, whenever the game is well-posed (a saddle-point exists for
every
X_{1}^{0}\succeq 0
and Isaacs’ condition holds), the game value is
necessarily linear in
X_{1}^{0}
and the associated dual variable must
satisfy the I-HJI-GRE. Theorem
6.8
establishes this via
a two-sided dynamic programming argument: the minimizer’s and
maximizer’s flow and jump inequalities give the Isaacs
inertia (
120
) for
\mathcal{I}
and

\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}
, while the vanishing of the key
identity along the saddle-point trajectory yields the Schur equalities,
exactly as in Theorem
3.6
.

Theorem 6.8

(Necessity of the I-HJI-GRE)
.

Suppose (
114
) is well-posed, a saddle-point exists
for every
X_{1}^{0}\succeq 0
, and Isaacs’ condition holds. Then the
unique piecewise-
C^{1}

P
with

J_{T}^{\circ}(X_{1}^{0})=\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
satisfies the full
I-HJI-GRE.

Proof.

Linearity of the value function.

For
\lambda\geq 0
:
J_{T}^{\circ}(\lambda X_{1}^{0})=\lambda J_{T}^{\circ}(X_{1}^{0})

(scale the saddle-point trajectory).
For
X_{1}^{0},\tilde{X}_{1}^{0}\succeq 0
: superimposing the two
saddle-point trajectories gives a valid trajectory for

X_{1}^{0}+\tilde{X}_{1}^{0}
achieving cost
J_{T}^{\circ}(X_{1}^{0})+J_{T}^{\circ}(\tilde{X}_{1}^{0})
, so
J_{T}^{\circ}(X_{1}^{0}+\tilde{X}_{1}^{0})\leq J_{T}^{\circ}(X_{1}^{0})+J_{T}^{\circ}(\tilde{X}_{1}^{0})
.
The reverse inequality holds by the minimax–maximin equality (each
player’s best response to the sum is at least as good as their
responses to the summands separately).
Hence
J_{T}^{\circ}
is linear, and the Riesz representation gives unique
piecewise-
C^{1}

P
with
J_{T}^{\circ}=\langle P(t_{0}),\,\cdot\rangle
.

Flow: Isaacs inertia of
\mathcal{G}
.

Fix a flow instant
t\in(t_{k-1},t_{k})
. Probe the players’ blocks
separately by holding the state block
X_{1}=0
.

(minimizer.)
With only the control block active,

X=[0;v_{u};0][0;v_{u};0]^{*}
on
[t,t+h]
, the minimizer’s dynamic
programming inequality (the value is the
min
over
X_{u}
) forces,
after dividing by
h
and
h\to 0
,
v_{u}^{*}\mathcal{I}_{uu}(t,P(t))v_{u}\geq 0
;
hence
\mathcal{I}_{uu}\succeq 0
(otherwise the minimizer drives the cost
to
-\infty
and no saddle exists).

(maximizer.)
Symmetrically, with only the disturbance block active,

X=[0;0;v_{w}][0;0;v_{w}]^{*}
, the maximizer’s dynamic programming inequality
(the value is the
max
over
X_{w}
) forces

v_{w}^{*}\mathcal{I}_{ww}(t,P(t))v_{w}\leq 0
; hence
\mathcal{I}_{ww}\preceq 0
.

(Cross/saddle.)
Existence of a finite saddle for the joint
quadratic
\operatorname{tr}(W^{*}\mathcal{G}W)
requires, in addition, the
two complementary Schur conditions and range conditions
of (
120
): if the maximizer’s reduced (Schur-complemented)
block
\mathcal{I}_{ww}-\mathcal{I}_{uw}^{*}{\mathcal{I}_{uu}}^{\dagger}\mathcal{I}_{uw}

were not
\preceq 0
(resp. the minimizer’s reduced block not
\succeq 0
),
the inner max (resp. min) would be unbounded. Thus Isaacs’
condition (
120
) holds. The same argument applied at each
jump gives Isaacs’ condition for
\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}
.

Schur equality along the saddle.

Along the saddle-point trajectory
X^{\circ}
, identity (
128
)
and the saddle-point value
J_{T}^{\circ}=\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
give

0=\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\langle\mathcal{I}(t,P(t)),\,X^{\circ}(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\sum_{k}\langle\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}(k,P(t_{k}^{+}),P(t_{k}^{-})),\,X^{\circ}(t_{k}^{-})\rangle.

(130)

By the saddle decomposition (
129
) (now established to be
a genuine saddle), along
X^{\circ}
each integrand equals the saddle value

\langle\mathcal{I}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G},\,X_{1}^{\circ}(t)\rangle
(the term

\langle\mathcal{G},\,W^{\circ}W^{\circ*}\rangle
vanishes at the saddle,
W^{\circ}=0
),
and similarly at each jump. Hence

0=\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\langle\mathcal{I}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G},\,X_{1}^{\circ}(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\sum_{k}\langle\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{j}},\,X_{1}^{\circ}(t_{k}^{-})\rangle.

(131)

Choosing
X_{1}^{0}
supported on each coordinate direction makes

X_{1}^{\circ}(t)
range over a generating set of
\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
, and (by the
optimality of the same saddle policy for every
X_{1}^{0}
and the
sign-definiteness of the two Schur complements established above) each
term must vanish individually. Therefore

\mathcal{I}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G}=0
a.e. (the flow Riccati
equation (
122a
)) and

\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{j}}=0
at each
jump; the well-definedness of
W^{\circ}
gives the compatibility
conditions (
122b
). Together with Isaacs’ condition this
is the full I-HJI-GRE (Definition
6.4
).
∎

6.1.6
Equivalence and dual DLMI (finite horizon)

Combining Theorems
6.7
and
6.8
gives the
equivalence Corollary
6.9
: under Isaacs’ condition,
the existence of a positive solution of the I-HJI-GRE is both necessary
and sufficient for the game to be well-posed.
Theorem
6.10
then provides an alternative
characterization of the game value as a maximization of

\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
over the solutions of a game Riccati

inequality
. Unlike the optimal control dual
(Theorem
3.8
), this is
not
a plain LMI: because
the Isaacs block
\mathcal{G}
is indefinite, the characterization
retains the (indefinite) Schur-complement term

\mathcal{I}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G}\succeq 0
together with the
saddle-inertia condition on
\mathcal{G}
, which are not jointly affine
in
P
.

Corollary 6.9

(Equivalence)
.

Under Isaacs’ condition, problem (
114
) has a
saddle-point for every
X_{1}^{0}\succeq 0
if and only if the
I-HJI-GRE has a piecewise-
C^{1}
solution
P\succeq 0
with

P(t_{0}+T)=Z_{T}
.

Proof.

Sufficiency is Theorem
6.7
; necessity is
Theorem
6.8
.
∎

Theorem 6.10

(Dual game Riccati-inequality characterization)
.

Suppose the I-HJI-GRE has a solution
P^{*}
. Then

J_{T}^{\circ}(X_{1}^{0})=\max_{P\succeq 0}\;\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\begin{gathered}\textnormal{Isaacs cond.~\eqref{eq:Isaacs:gen} for }\mathcal{I},\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}};\;\mathcal{I}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G}\succeq 0,\;\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{j}}\succeq 0;\\
P(t_{0}+T)\preceq Z_{T},\end{gathered}

(132)

attained at
P^{*}
.

Remark 6.11
.

In contrast to the optimal control dual
(Theorem
3.8
), (
132
) is
not
a
plain LMI. Because
\mathcal{G}
is indefinite,
\mathcal{I}\succeq 0

fails (it would force
\mathcal{I}_{ww}\succeq 0
, contradicting Isaacs’

\mathcal{I}_{ww}\preceq 0
), so the constraint cannot be reduced to a
single semidefinite condition affine in
P
. The game Riccati
inequality
\mathcal{I}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G}\succeq 0
involves the
generalized Schur complement of the indefinite block
\mathcal{G}

(rational in
P
through
{\mathcal{G}}^{\dagger}
) and is paired with the
separate saddle-inertia condition (
120
); together they
are the correct game analogue of
\mathcal{C}_{Z}\succeq 0
. In the
separated case
\mathcal{I}_{uw}=0
(e.g. the
H_{\infty}
setting of
Section
6.3
),
\mathcal{I}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{I}_{11}-\mathcal{I}_{1u}{\mathcal{I}_{uu}}^{\dagger}\mathcal{I}_{1u}^{*}-\mathcal{I}_{1w}{\mathcal{I}_{ww}}^{\dagger}\mathcal{I}_{1w}^{*}
, the
disturbance term
-\mathcal{I}_{1w}{\mathcal{I}_{ww}}^{\dagger}\mathcal{I}_{1w}^{*}=\mathcal{I}_{1w}{(-\mathcal{I}_{ww})}^{\dagger}\mathcal{I}_{1w}^{*}\succeq 0

entering with the opposite sign to the control term, exactly as in the
bounded-real/
H_{\infty}
Riccati inequality of
[
53
]
.

Proof.

Step 1: Every feasible
P
gives a lower bound.

Let
P
satisfy Isaacs’ condition, the Riccati inequalities

\mathcal{I}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G}\succeq 0
and

\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{j}}\succeq 0
, and

P(t_{0}+T)\preceq Z_{T}
. Let the maximizer play the saddle gain
K_{w}^{s}

(forcing
W_{w}=0
pointwise, as in the proof of
Theorem
6.7
). Then for every minimizer response
X_{u}
,
the saddle decomposition (
129
) gives

\langle\mathcal{I}(t,P(t)),\,X(t)\rangle=\langle\mathcal{I}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G},\,X_{1}(t)\rangle+\operatorname{tr}\!\bigl(W_{u}^{*}\mathcal{I}_{uu}W_{u}\bigr)\;\geq\;\langle\mathcal{I}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G},\,X_{1}(t)\rangle\;\geq\;0,

(133)

since
\mathcal{I}_{uu}\succeq 0
,
\mathcal{I}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G}\succeq 0
,
and
X_{1}\succeq 0
; each jump term is likewise
\geq 0
. By the key
identity (
128
) and
P(t_{0}+T)\preceq Z_{T}
,

J_{T}(X,X_{1}^{0})\big|_{X_{w}=K_{w}^{s}}\geq\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle+\underbrace{\langle Z_{T}-P(t_{0}+T),\,EX(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\rangle}_{\geq 0}\geq\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle.

minimizing over
X_{u}
and noting that
K_{w}^{s}
is one admissible maximizer
policy,

\max_{X_{w}}\min_{X_{u}}J_{T}\geq\min_{X_{u}}J_{T}(\cdot,K_{w}^{s})\geq\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
;
since
\min_{X_{u}}\max_{X_{w}}J_{T}\geq\max_{X_{w}}\min_{X_{u}}J_{T}
, we get

J_{T}^{\circ}(X_{1}^{0})\geq\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
. Hence

\max_{P\,\text{feasible}}\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle\leq J_{T}^{\circ}
.

Step 2:
P^{*}
is feasible and attains the bound.

The I-HJI-GRE solution
P^{*}
satisfies Isaacs’ condition and

\mathcal{I}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G}=0\succeq 0
,

\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{j}}=0\succeq 0
, and

P^{*}(t_{0}+T)=Z_{T}\preceq Z_{T}
, so it is feasible. By
Theorem
6.7
,
\langle P^{*}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle=J_{T}^{\circ}(X_{1}^{0})
, so
the maximum is attained at
P^{*}
and equals
J_{T}^{\circ}
.
∎

Corollary 6.12

(Continuous-time game: finite horizon)
.

Set
N_{T}=0
. The jump conditions are vacuous, and
Theorems
6.7
,
6.8
,
and
6.10
hold with the
continuous-time HJI-GRE

\textnormal{Isaacs cond.~\eqref{eq:Isaacs:gen} for }\mathcal{I},\qquad\mathcal{I}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G}=0,\qquad P(t_{0}+T)=Z_{T},

(134)

the continuous-time saddle-point controllers (
125
), and
the dual characterization

J_{T}^{\circ}(X_{1}^{0})=\max_{P\succeq 0}\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\textnormal{Isaacs cond.~\eqref{eq:Isaacs:gen},}\;\mathcal{I}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G}\succeq 0,\;P(t_{0}+T)\preceq Z_{T},

(135)

recovering the stochastic
H_{\infty}
Riccati equation
of
[
53
]
.

Corollary 6.13

(Discrete-time game: finite horizon)
.

Apply Definition
2.8
: set
\mathcal{F}=0
and
Z=0
on
flow,
t_{k}=k_{0}+k
, and
Z_{k}=Z(k)
with the game block structure of
Section
6.1.1
. With
P
constant on each interval

(k,k+1)
the flow conditions are vacuous, and the I-HJI-GRE reduces
to the
discrete-time HJI-GRE
at each step. Define the

discrete-time HJI operator

\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}(k,P(k+1),P(k)):=\mathcal{J}^{*}(k,P(k+1))-E^{*}P(k)E+Z(k)\in\mathbb{H}^{n+m},

(136)

blocked as in (
117
), with Isaacs block

\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{d}}:=\begin{bmatrix}(\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}})_{uu}&(\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}})_{uw}\\
(\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}})_{uw}^{*}&(\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}})_{ww}\end{bmatrix}

the bottom-right
(m_{u}+m_{w})\times(m_{u}+m_{w})
block of

\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}
(cf. (
123
)) and selection block

(\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}})_{1g}:=[(\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}})_{1u}\ (\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}})_{1w}]
.
Under the discrete-time Isaacs condition (
120
) for

\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}
, the discrete-time HJI-GRE is, with
P(k_{0}+N)=Z_{N}
,

\textnormal{Isaacs cond.~\eqref{eq:Isaacs:gen} for }\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}},\qquad\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{d}}=0,

(137)

and the discrete-time saddle-point controllers
(K_{u},K_{w})
are

\begin{bmatrix}K_{u}^{*}\\
K_{w}^{*}\end{bmatrix}=-{(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{d}})}^{\dagger}(\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}})_{1g}^{*}+(I-{(\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{d}})}^{\dagger}\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{d}})\,F,\qquad F\in\mathbb{C}^{(m_{u}+m_{w})\times n},

(138)

where
(\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}})_{1u},(\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}})_{1w}
are
the corresponding blocks of
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}
. The game value is

J_{N}^{\circ}(X_{1}^{0})=\langle P(k_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
, with discrete-time key identity

J_{N}^{\circ}(X_{1}^{0})=\langle P(k_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle+\sum_{k=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+N-1}\langle\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}(k,P(k+1),P(k)),\,X(k)\rangle,

and dual characterization

J_{N}^{\circ}(X_{1}^{0})=\max_{P\succeq 0}\langle P(k_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\textnormal{Isaacs cond.~\eqref{eq:Isaacs:gen} for }\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}},\;\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{d}}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{d}}\succeq 0,\;P(k_{0}+N)\preceq Z_{N},

(139)

recovering the discrete-time stochastic
H_{\infty}
/zero-sum game
Riccati equation.

6.2
Infinite-horizon game

We now study the zero-sum game on the infinite horizon
[t_{0},\infty)
.
The setup mirrors the optimal control case (Section
3.2
):
there is no terminal cost, and the dual variable
P_{\infty}
is no
longer fixed by a terminal condition but is characterized as the
bounded monotone limit of the finite-horizon solutions
P_{T}
as

T\to\infty
. As in the optimal-control case, the game value is
monotone
non-decreasing
in the horizon
T
and
P_{T}
converges to

P_{\infty}

from below
. Although a longer horizon gives the
maximizing player more options, the disturbance is penalised (
-R_{w}

block) and enters the value symmetrically with the control; with zero
terminal cost and a nonnegative stage value (guaranteed by

Z_{c}\succeq 0
), extending the horizon only adds nonnegative value.
Concretely, by dynamic programming the horizon-
T^{\prime}
value equals the
horizon-
T
game with the nonnegative terminal weight

P_{T^{\prime}}(t_{0}+T)\succeq 0
, which dominates the zero terminal weight of the
horizon-
T
value; this is made precise in the proof of
Theorem
6.15
. As in
Section
3.2
, no
time-invariance or periodicity of
\mathcal{F}
,
\mathcal{J}
, or
Z

is assumed.

6.2.1
Setting and assumptions (infinite horizon)

The infinite-horizon game removes the terminal penalty from the
finite-horizon cost (
113
), leaving

J_{\infty}(X,X_{1}^{0}):=\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}\langle Z(t),\,X(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\langle Z_{k},\,X(t_{k}^{-})\rangle,

(140)

with game value

J_{\infty}^{\circ}(X_{1}^{0}):=\min_{X_{12},X_{22}}\;\max_{X_{13},X_{23},X_{33}}\;J_{\infty}(X,X_{1}^{0})\quad\text{s.t.~\eqref{eq:sys:IMP},}\;EX(t_{0})E^{*}=X_{1}^{0}.

Well-posedness requires the game analogues of the
assumptions (H1)–(H3) of Section
3.2
, with
uniform stabilizability (H1) imposed on the
saddle-point

closed-loop, uniform coercivity (H2)
EZ(t)E^{*}\succeq\delta I
, and
uniform boundedness (H3) of the data, together with Isaacs’
condition (
120
) holding uniformly in
t
.

6.2.2
Sufficient condition (infinite horizon)

The following theorem is the infinite-horizon analogue of
Theorem
6.7
: any bounded positive solution of the
infinite-horizon I-HJI-GRE is the unique such solution and directly
provides the game value and the saddle-point policy. The proof mirrors
Theorem
3.11
, with the finite-horizon terminal
correction replaced by a stability argument that forces

\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}+T),\,EX^{\circ}(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\rangle\to 0
.

Theorem 6.14

(Sufficient condition, infinite-horizon game)
.

Suppose there exists a bounded piecewise-
C^{1}
function

P_{\infty}:[t_{0},\infty)\to\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
satisfying the infinite-horizon
I-HJI-GRE with Isaacs’ condition holding uniformly. Then
:

(a)

J_{\infty}^{\circ}(X_{1}^{0})=\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
, attained
at the saddle-point policy with

(K_{u},K_{w})\in(\mathcal{K}_{u}^{s}\times\mathcal{K}_{w}^{s})(t,P_{\infty}(t))

on flow and the analogous saddle gains at each jump.

(b)

P_{\infty}
is the unique bounded solution of the
infinite-horizon I-HJI-GRE.

Proof.

The argument follows Theorem
3.11
, with the genuine saddle decomposition (
129
) replacing the
minimization and the finite-horizon terminal correction
controlled by the saddle-point closed-loop stability.

Lower bound.

Let the maximizer play the saddle gain
K_{w}^{s}
(forcing
W_{w}=0
),
and let
X_{u}
be any admissible minimizer response. Apply the key
identity (
128
) on
[t_{0},t_{0}+T]
with
P_{\infty}
and no
terminal cost. By Isaacs’ condition and the infinite-horizon I-HJI-GRE
(
\mathcal{I}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G}=0
), the saddle
decomposition (
129
) with
W_{w}=0
gives

\langle\mathcal{I}(t,P_{\infty}),\,X\rangle=\operatorname{tr}(W_{u}^{*}\mathcal{I}_{uu}W_{u})\geq 0

a.e. on flow and the analogous
\geq 0
at each jump. Hence

\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\!\!\langle Z,\,X\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\!\!\sum_{k:\,t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T}\!\!\langle Z_{k},\,X(t_{k}^{-})\rangle\geq\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle-\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}+T),\,EX(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\rangle.

Under the game analogue of (H1) the saddle-point closed-loop is
uniformly exponentially stable, so
\|EX(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\|_{*}\to 0
for any
finite-cost trajectory; since
P_{\infty}\preceq\alpha_{2}I
is bounded,
the terminal term vanishes as
T\to\infty
. minimizing over
X_{u}

and using that
K_{w}^{s}
is one admissible maximizer policy, then

T\to\infty
, gives

J_{\infty}^{\circ}(X_{1}^{0})\geq\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
.

Attainment.

For the saddle-point policy

(K_{u},K_{w})\in(\mathcal{K}_{u}^{s}\times\mathcal{K}_{w}^{s})(t,P_{\infty}(t))
on
flow and the jump gains from (
126
) at each jump,

W=0
in (
129
), so

\langle\mathcal{I}(t,P_{\infty}),\,X^{\circ}\rangle=0
a.e. and

\langle\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}},\,X^{\circ}(t_{k}^{-})\rangle=0
at every jump. The key
identity on
[t_{0},t_{0}+T]
then gives

\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\!\!\langle Z,\,X^{\circ}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\!\!\sum_{k:\,t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T}\!\!\langle Z_{k},\,X^{\circ}(t_{k}^{-})\rangle=\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle-\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}+T),\,EX^{\circ}(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\rangle.

Since the saddle-point trajectory satisfies the uniform exponential
stability (H1),
\|EX^{\circ}(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\|_{*}\to 0
, and as
P_{\infty}
is
bounded the terminal term tends to
0
. Taking
T\to\infty
yields

J_{\infty}(X^{\circ},X_{1}^{0})=\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle=J_{\infty}^{\circ}(X_{1}^{0})
,
so the policy is optimal and the saddle is attained.

Uniqueness.

If
\tilde{P}
is another bounded solution, the lower-bound and
attainment arguments give

J_{\infty}^{\circ}(X_{1}^{0})=\langle\tilde{P}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
for all

X_{1}^{0}\succeq 0
. Since also

J_{\infty}^{\circ}(X_{1}^{0})=\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
, taking
X_{1}^{0}=vv^{*}

for every
v
gives
P_{\infty}\equiv\tilde{P}
.
∎

6.2.3
Necessary condition and existence (infinite horizon)

Under the game analogues of (H1)–(H3), existence of a bounded
solution of the infinite-horizon I-HJI-GRE follows from a monotone
convergence argument: the finite-horizon solutions
P_{T}
form a

non-decreasing
bounded sequence (the game value
increases as the
horizon lengthens) whose pointwise limit satisfies the I-HJI-GRE. This
is the game counterpart of Theorem
3.12
.

Theorem 6.15

(Necessary condition and existence, infinite-horizon game)
.

Assume Isaacs’ condition holds uniformly and the analogues of

(H1)–(H3)
hold for the saddle-point closed-loop.
Then there exists a unique bounded piecewise-
C^{1}
solution

P_{\infty}\in\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
of the infinite-horizon I-HJI-GRE, satisfying

P_{\infty}=\lim_{T\to\infty}P_{T}
(monotone non-decreasing), where

P_{T}
solves the finite-horizon I-HJI-GRE with
P_{T}(t_{0}+T)=0
,
and
J_{\infty}^{\circ}(X_{1}^{0})=\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
.

Proof.

Nonnegativity
P_{T}\succeq 0
.

\langle P_{T}(t),\,X_{1}\rangle
is the game value over
[t,t_{0}+T]
from
X_{1}
with
zero terminal cost. Letting the maximizer play
X_{w}=0
is one
admissible choice, so

\langle P_{T}(t),\,X_{1}\rangle=\min_{X_{u}}\max_{X_{w}}\!\int_{t}^{t_{0}+T}\!\!\langle Z,\,X\rangle\,\mathrm{d}s\;\geq\;\min_{X_{u}}\!\int_{t}^{t_{0}+T}\!\!\langle Z,\,X\rangle\,\mathrm{d}s\Big|_{X_{w}=0}.

With
X_{w}=0
the integrand is
\langle Z_{c},\,X_{c}\rangle\geq 0
(since

Z_{c}\succeq 0
and the control block
X_{c}=\bigl[\begin{smallmatrix}X_{1}&X_{12}^{*}\\
X_{12}&X_{22}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr]\succeq 0
),
so the right-hand side is
\geq 0
. Hence
P_{T}(t)\succeq 0
for all

t,T
.

Monotone limit (non-decreasing).

Fix
T^{\prime}>T
. By Isaacs’ condition the saddle exists at every stage, so
dynamic programming gives the nesting

\langle P_{T^{\prime}}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle=\min_{X_{u}}\max_{X_{w}}\Bigl[\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\!\!\langle Z,\,X\rangle\,\mathrm{d}s+\langle P_{T^{\prime}}(t_{0}+T),\,EX(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\rangle\Bigr],

i.e.
J_{T^{\prime}}^{\circ}
is the horizon-
T
game with terminal weight

\Phi:=P_{T^{\prime}}(t_{0}+T)
, whereas
J_{T}^{\circ}
is the same game with
terminal weight
0
. The game value is monotone in the terminal
weight: if
\Phi_{1}\succeq\Phi_{2}
then

\int\langle Z,\,X\rangle+\langle\Phi_{1},\,EX(T)E^{*}\rangle\geq\int\langle Z,\,X\rangle+\langle\Phi_{2},\,EX(T)E^{*}\rangle

pointwise (as
EX(T)E^{*}\succeq 0
), and
\min_{X_{u}}\max_{X_{w}}
preserves
pointwise inequalities. Since
\Phi=P_{T^{\prime}}(t_{0}+T)\succeq 0
by the
nonnegativity above,
\langle P_{T^{\prime}}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle\geq\langle P_{T}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle

for all
X_{1}^{0}\succeq 0
, i.e.
P_{T^{\prime}}(t_{0})\succeq P_{T}(t_{0})
; the same
argument from any
t
gives
P_{T^{\prime}}(t)\succeq P_{T}(t)
. Thus

\{P_{T}(t)\}
is non-decreasing in
T
. The disturbance does not
reverse this direction; it only requires the value to remain finite,
which is the uniform Isaacs condition together with the upper bound
next. The bounds
0\preceq P_{T}\preceq\alpha_{2}I
follow
from saddle-point stability (H1) (the minimizer’s stabilizing
policy bounds
\max_{X_{w}}
from above since the disturbance is
penalised by
-R_{w}\prec 0
). Equicontinuity from (H3) and
Isaacs’ condition, together with monotone convergence and
Arzelà-Ascoli, give

C^{1}
-convergence on compact subintervals; passing to the limit
in the HJI equation preserves all conditions.

Optimality.

P_{\infty}
is bounded and satisfies the infinite-horizon I-HJI-GRE,
so Theorem
6.14
gives

J_{\infty}^{\circ}(X_{1}^{0})=\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
.
∎

6.2.4
Equivalence and dual DLMI (infinite horizon)

Theorems
6.14
and
6.15
together give the
complete characterization. The dual game Riccati-inequality
characterization extends the finite-horizon
version (Theorem
6.10
) by replacing the terminal
constraint
P(t_{0}+T)\preceq Z_{T}
with a boundedness condition; as
there, the indefinite Isaacs block
\mathcal{G}
makes it a Riccati
inequality (with Schur-complement term), not a plain LMI.

Corollary 6.16

(Equivalence, infinite-horizon game)
.

The infinite-horizon game is well-posed with value

\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
if and only if the infinite-horizon
I-HJI-GRE has a bounded positive solution. The solution is unique.

Proof.

Sufficiency: Theorem
6.14
.
Necessity: Theorem
6.15
.
∎

Theorem 6.17

(Dual game Riccati inequality, infinite-horizon game)
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
6.15
,

J_{\infty}^{\circ}(X_{1}^{0})=\sup_{P}\;\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle\quad\text{subject to}\quad\begin{gathered}\textnormal{Isaacs cond.~\eqref{eq:Isaacs:gen} for }\mathcal{I},\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}};\\
\mathcal{I}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G}\succeq 0,\;\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{j}}\succeq 0,\;P\text{ bounded};\end{gathered}

(141)

the supremum is attained at
P_{\infty}
.

Proof.

For any bounded feasible
P
, let the maximizer play
K_{w}^{s}

(forcing
W_{w}=0
). By the Riccati inequalities and Isaacs’ condition,
the saddle decomposition (
133
) gives

\langle\mathcal{I}(t,P),\,X\rangle=\langle\mathcal{I}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G},\,X_{1}\rangle+\operatorname{tr}(W_{u}^{*}\mathcal{I}_{uu}W_{u})\geq 0
a.e. for every
minimizer response, and each jump term
\geq 0
. The key identity on

[t_{0},t_{0}+T]
then yields, for the maximizer policy
K_{w}^{s}
and any

X_{u}
,

\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\!\!\langle Z,\,X\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\!\!\sum_{k:\,t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T}\!\!\langle Z_{k},\,X(t_{k}^{-})\rangle\geq\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle-\langle P(t_{0}+T),\,EX(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\rangle.

As
P
is bounded and
\|EX(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\|_{*}\to 0
under the saddle-point
closed-loop stability (H1), minimizing over
X_{u}
, using that

K_{w}^{s}
is one maximizer policy, and letting

T\to\infty
gives
J_{\infty}^{\circ}(X_{1}^{0})\geq\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
. Since

P_{\infty}
is feasible (Theorem
6.15
) and attains

J_{\infty}^{\circ}(X_{1}^{0})=\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle

(Theorem
6.14
), the supremum equals

J_{\infty}^{\circ}
and is attained at
P_{\infty}
.
∎

6.2.5
Dwell-time conditions

The dwell-time framework of Section
3.2.5
extends to
the zero-sum game setting by replacing all flow and jump composite
operators by their HJI counterparts
\mathcal{I}
and

\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}
and the optimal control pivot
(\mathcal{C}_{Z})_{3}

by the indefinite Isaacs block
\mathcal{G}
. The forward Riccati
ODE (
39
) generalizes to the forward
HJI-GRE:

E^{*}\tfrac{d\tilde{P}}{d\tau}E=-\mathcal{F}^{*}(\tau,\tilde{P})-Z_{\text{game}}(\tau)+\begin{bmatrix}\mathcal{I}_{1u}&\mathcal{I}_{1w}\end{bmatrix}\,{\mathcal{G}}^{\dagger}\,\begin{bmatrix}\mathcal{I}_{1u}^{*}\\
\mathcal{I}_{1w}^{*}\end{bmatrix},

(142)

where
Z_{\text{game}}
carries the game-structured cost (with

-R_{w}
in the disturbance block).

Theorem 6.18

(Causal conditions: periodic game)
.

Assume a constant period
\Delta_{k}\equiv T_{p}
; that the flow operator

\mathcal{F}(\cdot,X)
and the flow cost
Z(\cdot)
(with the game
block structure of Section
6.1.1
,
R_{u}\succ 0
,

R_{w}\succ 0
,
Q\succeq 0
) are
T_{p}
-periodic in
t
; that the jump
operator
\mathcal{J}(k,\cdot)
and the jump cost
Z_{k}
are
independent of
k
; and that Isaacs’ condition (
120
)
holds uniformly. Under the game analogues of
(H1)–(H3)

for the saddle-point closed-loop:

(a)

(Existence and uniqueness.)
There exists a unique

T_{p}
-periodic piecewise-
C^{1}
solution
P_{\infty}
of the
periodic I-HJI-GRE.

(b)

(Sufficiency.)
The causal saddle-point policy from

(\mathcal{K}_{u}^{s}\times\mathcal{K}_{w}^{s})(\tau,P_{\infty}(\tau))

achieves
J_{\infty}^{\circ}(X_{1}^{0})=\langle P_{\infty}(0),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
.

(c)

(Necessity.)
Any causal policy achieving

J_{\infty}^{\circ}
must use
P_{\infty}
.

Proof.

Existence and uniqueness (a).

The finite-horizon solutions
P_{T}
form a non-decreasing bounded
sequence by the nesting argument of Theorem
6.15

(horizon-
T
game with nonnegative terminal weight
P_{T^{\prime}}(t_{0}+T)\succeq 0

dominates the zero-terminal game). Hence
P_{T}(0^{-})

is non-decreasing and bounded (
0\preceq P_{T}\preceq\alpha_{2}I
). By the minimax stability assumption and
Arzelà-Ascoli, the sequence converges uniformly to the unique
periodic I-HJI-GRE solution
P_{\infty}
.

Sufficiency (b).

The
T_{p}
-periodic extension of
P_{\infty}
satisfies

\mathcal{I}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G}=0
everywhere.
Apply the key identity on
[t_{0},t_{0}+T]
with no terminal cost
along the saddle-point trajectory
X^{\circ}
(gains in

\mathcal{K}_{u}^{s}\times\mathcal{K}_{w}^{s}
), where the saddle
decomposition (
129
) makes each integrand and jump term
vanish:

\displaystyle J_{T}^{\circ}(X_{1}^{0})

\displaystyle=\langle P_{\infty}(0^{+}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle-\langle P_{\infty}(T^{-}),\,EX^{\circ}(T)E^{*}\rangle

\displaystyle\quad+\underbrace{\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\langle\mathcal{I}(\tau,P_{\infty}),\,X^{\circ}\rangle}\,\mathrm{d}t_{=0}+\underbrace{\textstyle\sum_{k}\langle\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}(k,P_{\infty}(t_{k}^{+}),P_{\infty}(t_{k}^{-})),\,X^{\circ}(t_{k}^{-})\rangle}_{=0}.

Thus along the saddle policy

J_{T}^{\circ}=\langle P_{\infty}(0^{+}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle-\langle P_{\infty}(T^{-}),\,EX^{\circ}(T)E^{*}\rangle
;
that this is indeed the game value (and not merely the cost of one
policy) follows from the lower/upper bounds of Theorem
6.7

applied on
[t_{0},t_{0}+T]
.
Since
P_{\infty}
is bounded and

\|EX^{\circ}(T)E^{*}\|_{*}\to 0
under the stability assumption,
taking
T\to\infty
gives

J_{\infty}^{\circ}(X_{1}^{0})=\langle P_{\infty}(0^{+}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
.

Necessity (c).

By Theorem
6.15
, the unique infinite-horizon I-HJI-GRE
solution
P_{\infty}^{ih}
satisfies
J_{\infty}^{\circ}=\langle P_{\infty}^{ih},\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
.
Periodicity of the data implies
P_{\infty}^{ih}
is
T_{p}
-periodic
(by the same shifting argument as Theorem
3.17
(c)),
so
P_{\infty}^{ih}=P_{\infty}
.
∎

Theorem 6.19

(Sufficient causal condition: MDT game)
.

Let
T_{\min}>0
, assume Isaacs’ condition (
120
), and
assume that the flow and jump data depend only on the timer

\tau=t-t_{k}
, that the flow data are time-invariant beyond

T_{\min}
, and that the jump data are
k
-independent:

\mathcal{F}(\tau,\cdot)\equiv\mathcal{F}_{\infty}(\cdot),\quad Z(\tau)\equiv Z(T_{\min})\ \ (\tau\geq T_{\min}),\qquad\mathcal{J}(k,\cdot)\equiv\mathcal{J}(\cdot),\quad Z_{k}\equiv Z_{\mathrm{jp}}\ \ \forall k,

(143)

where the flow cost
Z
and the jump cost
Z_{\mathrm{jp}}
carry the
game block structure of Section
6.1.1
(with
-R_{w}
in
the disturbance block,
R_{u}\succ 0
,
R_{w}\succ 0
,
Q\succeq 0
).
Suppose there exists
P_{s}:[0,T_{\min}]\to\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
satisfying:

(i)

Forward HJI-GRE on
[0,T_{\min}]
:

P_{s}
solves the forward HJI-ODE (
142
) on

(0,T_{\min})
, i.e.,

\mathcal{I}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G}=0
with Isaacs’
condition (
120
)
for
\tau\in(0,T_{\min})
.

(ii)

Jump HJI-GRE at
\tau=T_{\min}
:

Isaacs’ condition (
120
) for

\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}(k,P_{s}(0^{+}),P_{s}(T_{\min}))

and
\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{j}}=0
.

(iii)

Geromel–Colaneri condition:
the frozen HJI
matrix

\mathcal{I}^{0}(P_{s}(T_{\min})):=\mathcal{F}_{\infty}^{*}(P_{s}(T_{\min}))+Z(T_{\min})

(144)

satisfies Isaacs’ condition (
120
) and the frozen
game Riccati inequality

\mathcal{I}^{0}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G}^{0}\preceq 0
, where

\mathcal{G}^{0}
is the Isaacs block of
\mathcal{I}^{0}
. Under
(
143
),
\mathcal{I}^{0}
coincides with

\mathcal{I}(\tau,P_{s}(T_{\min}))
for every
\tau\geq T_{\min}
.

Then the causal saddle-point policy from

(\mathcal{K}_{u}^{s}\times\mathcal{K}_{w}^{s})(\tau,P_{s}(\tau))

for
\tau\leq T_{\min}
and frozen at
\tau=T_{\min}
for

\tau>T_{\min}
achieves

J_{T}^{\circ}(X_{1}^{0})\leq\langle P_{s}(0),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
for all

\Delta_{k}\geq T_{\min}
.

Proof.

Extension.

Define
\bar{P}(\tau):=P_{s}(\tau)
for
\tau\leq T_{\min}
and

\bar{P}(\tau):=P_{s}(T_{\min})
for
\tau>T_{\min}
.

Verification (minimizer’s upper bound).

The minimizer plays its causal gain
K_{u}^{s}(\tau,\bar{P}(\tau))
(frozen at

\tau=T_{\min}
for
\tau>T_{\min}
), forcing
W_{u}=0
pointwise; the
maximizer is arbitrary. By the saddle decomposition (
129
)
each integrand is then

\langle\mathcal{I}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G},\,X_{1}\rangle+\operatorname{tr}(W_{w}^{*}\mathcal{I}_{ww}W_{w})
,
and since
\mathcal{I}_{ww}\preceq 0
(Isaacs) the second term is
\leq 0
.

Flow,
\tau\in(0,T_{\min})
:

condition (i) gives
\mathcal{I}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G}=0
, so the
integrand is
\leq 0
.

Flow,
\tau>T_{\min}
:

\dot{\bar{P}}=0
and, by (
143
),

\mathcal{I}(\tau,\bar{P})=\mathcal{I}^{0}(P_{s}(T_{\min}))
, whose Schur
complement
\mathcal{I}^{0}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G}^{0}\preceq 0
by
condition (iii); hence the integrand

\langle\mathcal{I}^{0}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G}^{0},\,X_{1}\rangle+\operatorname{tr}(W_{w}^{*}\mathcal{I}^{0}_{ww}W_{w})\leq 0
.

Jump at every
t_{k}
with
\Delta_{k}\geq T_{\min}
:

\bar{P}(\Delta_{k}^{-})=P_{s}(T_{\min})
, so by condition (ii)

\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{j}}=0
and the jump
term is
\leq 0
.

Cost bound.

Apply the game key identity on
[t_{0},t_{0}+T]
with
\bar{P}

(minimizer at
K_{u}^{s}
, any maximizer):

\displaystyle J_{T}(X,X_{1}^{0})

\displaystyle=\langle\bar{P}(0),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle-\underbrace{\langle\bar{P}(T),\,EX(T)E^{*}\rangle}_{\geq 0}

\displaystyle\quad+\underbrace{\int_{0}^{T_{\min}}\langle\mathcal{I},\,X\rangle}\,\mathrm{d}t_{\leq 0}+\underbrace{\int_{T_{\min}}^{T}\langle\mathcal{I}^{0},\,X\rangle}\,\mathrm{d}t_{\leq 0}+\underbrace{\textstyle\sum_{k}\langle\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}},\,X(t_{k}^{-})\rangle}_{\leq 0}.

All correction terms are
\leq 0
, so
\max_{X_{w}}J_{T}\leq\langle P_{s}(0),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle

under the causal minimizer policy, hence
J_{T}^{\circ}\leq\langle P_{s}(0),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle

for all
T>0
.
The policy is causal since conditions (i)–(iii) are verified on

\tau\in[0,T_{\min}]
only.
∎

Theorem 6.20

(Sufficient causal condition: RDT game)
.

Let
0<T_{\min}\leq T_{\max}
, assume Isaacs’
condition (
120
), and assume the flow and jump data
depend only on the timer
\tau=t-t_{k}
and are
k
-independent, the
flow cost
Z(\tau)
and jump cost
Z_{\mathrm{jp}}
carrying the game
block structure of Section
6.1.1
(with
-R_{w}
in the
disturbance block,
R_{u}\succ 0
,
R_{w}\succ 0
,
Q\succeq 0
). Unlike the
MDT case, no time-invariance beyond
T_{\min}
is required. Suppose
there exists
P_{s}:[0,T_{\max}]\to\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
satisfying:

(i)

\mathcal{I}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G}=0
with Isaacs’
condition (
120
) on

(0,T_{\max})
(forward HJI-ODE (
142
)).

(ii)

Isaacs’ condition (
120
) for

\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}(k,P_{s}(0^{+}),P_{s}(\tau))

and
\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{j}}=0
for all
\tau\in[T_{\min},T_{\max}]
.

Then the causal saddle-point policy from

(\mathcal{K}_{u}^{s}\times\mathcal{K}_{w}^{s})(\tau,P_{s}(\tau))
achieves

J_{T}^{\circ}(X_{1}^{0})=\langle P_{s}(0),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle-\langle P_{s}(T),\,EX^{\circ}(T)E^{*}\rangle\leq\langle P_{s}(0),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle

(145)

for every
\Delta_{k}\in[T_{\min},T_{\max}]
, with equality as

T\to\infty
.

Proof.

For any
\Delta_{k}\in[T_{\min},T_{\max}]
: condition (i) gives

\mathcal{I}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G}=0
on

(0,T_{\max})
, so the forward HJI-ODE equality holds everywhere in

[0,\Delta_{k}]
; condition (ii) gives the jump Schur equality at

\tau=\Delta_{k}
. Along the saddle-point trajectory
X^{\circ}

(both players in
\mathcal{K}_{u}^{s}\times\mathcal{K}_{w}^{s}
), the saddle
decomposition (
129
) makes every flow and jump term
vanish, and the game key identity on
[t_{0},t_{0}+T]
gives

J_{T}^{\circ}=\langle P_{s}(0),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle-\langle P_{s}(T),\,EX^{\circ}(T)E^{*}\rangle+\underbrace{\int_{0}^{T}\langle\mathcal{I},\,X^{\circ}\rangle}\,\mathrm{d}t_{=0}+\underbrace{\textstyle\sum_{k}\langle\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}},\,X^{\circ}(t_{k}^{-})\rangle}_{=0},

giving (
145
). Under the saddle-point stability
assumption,
\langle P_{s}(T),\,EX^{\circ}(T)E^{*}\rangle\to 0
as
T\to\infty
, so
the bound is sharp. No Geromel–Colaneri extension is needed.
∎

6.2.6
Continuous- and discrete-time corollaries (infinite horizon)

Corollary 6.21

(Continuous-time game: infinite horizon)
.

With
N_{T}=0
, Theorem
6.15
gives the unique bounded

C^{1}
solution
P_{\infty}
of the continuous-time infinite-horizon HJI equation,
recovering the stochastic
H_{\infty}
algebraic Riccati
equation
[
53
]
.

Corollary 6.22

(Discrete-time game: infinite horizon)
.

With
\mathcal{F}=0
,
Z=0
on flow, and
t_{k}=k_{0}+k
, the
infinite-horizon game value is

J_{\infty}^{\circ}(X_{1}^{0})=\langle P_{\infty}(k_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
, where
P_{\infty}
is
the unique bounded positive solution of the
algebraic

discrete-time HJI-GRE (
137
) (without terminal
condition), obtained as the monotone non-decreasing limit of the

N
-step solutions, and the discrete-time saddle-point controllers are
given by (
138
) evaluated at
P_{\infty}
.

6.3
Connection to
H_{\infty}
and full-information games

The standard
H_{\infty}
(bounded
L_{2}
-gain) condition corresponds
to the cost block
Z_{ww}=-\gamma^{2}I
(purely diagonal in the
maximizing block), giving

\mathcal{I}_{ww}(t,P(t))=-\gamma^{2}I+E_{w}\mathcal{F}^{*}(t,P(t))E_{w}^{*}

(from the flow adjoint, since
E_{w}E^{*}=0
).
Isaacs’ condition
\mathcal{I}_{ww}\preceq 0
then reads

\gamma^{2}I\succeq E_{w}\mathcal{F}^{*}(t,P(t))E_{w}^{*}
,
which is precisely a norm bound on the maximizing-block component
of
\mathcal{F}^{*}
.
With
Z_{uw}=0
(no cross coupling in
Z
), one has

\mathcal{I}_{uw}=0
so
\mathcal{G}=\mathrm{diag}(\mathcal{I}_{uu},\mathcal{I}_{ww})
and the separation condition of
Remark
6.6
holds: the optimal minimizer depends
only on
X_{1}
.
The I-HJI-GRE (
122
) then recovers the

H_{\infty}
Riccati equation of
[
53
]
.

When the maximizer’s blocks
X_{33}
and
X_{23}
are

measured
and available to the minimizer, the problem becomes
a
full-information game
: the minimizer’s decision variable
is the full triple
(X_{12},X_{22},X_{23})
, constrained only by

X\succeq 0
. This case admits the same I-HJI-GRE conditions, and
the saddle-point gains
(K_{u}^{s},K_{w}^{s})
from (
125
)
can be implemented directly from the current matrix
X
. The
separation condition
\mathcal{I}_{uw}=0
is no longer required,
since the minimizer has direct access to the maximizing blocks.

6.4
Long-term average game value

We now develop the zero-sum game counterpart of
Section
3.3
. The setup parallels that subsection: the
dynamics are augmented by a Hermitian forcing on the state block as
in Definition
3.25
, and the average-cost rate
replaces the integrated saddle-point value as the object of interest.
The minimax–maximin structure of Section
6
is retained,
so that the long-term average game value is again given by a linear
pairing of the HJI-GRE solution with the forcing data, under Isaacs’
condition.

6.4.1
Setting and assumptions

The forced impulsive system is that of Definition
3.25
.
The augmented decision variable
X\in\mathbb{H}^{n+m}_{\succeq 0}
has the three-block
partition (
112
) with the minimizer controlling

(X_{12},X_{22})
and the maximizer controlling

(X_{13},X_{23},X_{33})
. The cost is the integrated linear cost of
Section
6.1.1
without terminal penalty:

J_{T}(X,X_{1}^{0}):=\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\langle Z(t),\,X(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\sum_{k:\,t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T}\langle Z_{k},\,X(t_{k}^{-})\rangle,

(146)

where
Z=\bigl[\begin{smallmatrix}Q&S_{u}&S_{w}\\
S_{u}^{*}&R_{u}&R_{uw}\\
S_{w}^{*}&R_{uw}^{*}&-R_{w}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr]
has the game block
structure of Section
6.1.1
with
R_{u}\succ 0
,

R_{w}\succ 0
,
Q\succeq 0
.

Definition 6.23

(Long-term average game value)
.

The
long-term average game value
is the saddle-point rate

\rho^{\circ}(X_{1}^{0}):=\limsup_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}\!\left[\min_{X_{12},X_{22}}\,\max_{X_{13},X_{23},X_{33}}\,J_{T}(X,X_{1}^{0})\right]

(147)

under the dynamics (
46
) and
EX(t_{0})E^{*}=X_{1}^{0}
.

The game-theoretic counterparts of (A1)–(A3) of
Section
3.3.1
are:

(A1
∘
)

Uniform stabilizability
of the saddle-point homogeneous closed-loop:

under the saddle-point feedback of the homogeneous game (i.e., with

\mathcal{W}=0,\mathcal{W}_{k}=0
), the closed-loop second-moment
satisfies
\|EX^{\circ}(t)E^{*}\|_{*}\leq\beta e^{-\alpha(t-s)}\|EX^{\circ}(s)E^{*}\|_{*}
.

(A2
∘
)

Uniform coercivity:

EZ(t)E^{*}\succeq\delta I
uniformly for some
\delta>0
.

(A3
∘
)

Uniform boundedness and
Isaacs’ condition.

Z,Z_{k},\mathcal{F},\mathcal{J}
uniformly bounded;

{\mathcal{G}}^{\dagger},{(\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{j}})}^{\dagger}

uniformly bounded on bounded sets of
P
; Isaacs’ condition
(Definition
6.2
) holds uniformly in
t
;
\mathcal{W}(t),\mathcal{W}_{k}
uniformly bounded; inter-jump times bounded above and
below.

Under (A1
∘
)–(A3
∘
), the homogeneous infinite-horizon
I-HJI-GRE has a unique bounded piecewise-
C^{1}
solution

P_{\infty}\succeq 0
by Theorem
6.15
.

6.4.2
Forced HJI key identity and finite-horizon decomposition

Since the HJI flow operator (
115
) is structurally identical
to the composite flow operator (
10
) with the
game-structured cost
Z
, and similarly for the jump
operator (
117
), the forced key identity of
Proposition
3.27
applies verbatim with

\mathcal{C}_{Z}\leftarrow\mathcal{I}
and

\mathcal{D}_{Z}\leftarrow\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}
:

\displaystyle J_{T}(X,X_{1}^{0})

\displaystyle=\langle P(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle-\langle P(t_{0}+T),\,EX(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\rangle

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\langle\mathcal{I}(t,P(t)),\,X(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\sum_{k:\,t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T}\langle\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}},\,X(t_{k}^{-})\rangle

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\langle P(t),\,\mathcal{W}(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\sum_{k:\,t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T}\langle P(t_{k}^{+}),\,\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle.

(148)

This is the only ingredient needed for the average-cost theory.

Theorem 6.24

(Finite-horizon average game value decomposition)
.

Let
T>0
and let
P_{T}
be the piecewise-
C^{1}
solution of the
finite-horizon I-HJI-GRE (Definition
6.4
) with

P_{T}(t_{0}+T)=0
, assumed to exist under Isaacs’ condition. Then
the finite-horizon saddle value
J_{T}^{\circ}(X_{1}^{0})
satisfies

J_{T}^{\circ}(X_{1}^{0})=\langle P_{T}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle+\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\langle P_{T}(t),\,\mathcal{W}(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\!\!\sum_{k:\,t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T}\!\!\langle P_{T}(t_{k}^{+}),\,\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle,

(149)

and is attained at the saddle-point policy
(K_{u}^{s},K_{w}^{s})
from

(\mathcal{K}_{u}^{s}\times\mathcal{K}_{w}^{s})(t,P_{T}(t))
on flow and the
analogous saddle gains at each jump. The horizon-
T
average game
value is
J_{T}^{\circ}/T
.

Proof.

Apply (
6.4.2
) with
P=P_{T}
and
P_{T}(t_{0}+T)=0
, so
the second boundary term vanishes. The I-HJI-GRE gives Isaacs’
condition and
\mathcal{I}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G}=0
,

\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{j}}=0

(Definition
6.4
), so by the saddle
decomposition (
129
) (proof of Theorem
6.7
)
the saddle of
\langle\mathcal{I}(t,P_{T}(t)),\,X(t)\rangle
in the free blocks is

0
, attained at the saddle policy, and likewise for

\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}
.
Since the last two terms in (
6.4.2
) are independent
of the free blocks
(X_{12},X_{22},X_{13},X_{23},X_{33})
, the minimax
saddle is attained at the same saddle policy as in the homogeneous
game, and the resulting saddle value is (
149
).
∎

6.4.3
Infinite-horizon: sufficient condition

Theorem 6.25

(Sufficient condition, infinite-horizon average game value)
.

Suppose there exists a bounded piecewise-
C^{1}
function

P_{\infty}:[t_{0},\infty)\to\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
satisfying the infinite-horizon
I-HJI-GRE and Isaacs’ condition uniformly, and define

\rho^{\circ}:=\limsup_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}\!\left[\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\langle P_{\infty}(t),\,\mathcal{W}(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\!\!\sum_{k:\,t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T}\!\!\langle P_{\infty}(t_{k}^{+}),\,\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle\right].

(150)

Then
:

(a)

\rho^{\circ}(X_{1}^{0})=\rho^{\circ}
for every
X_{1}^{0}
;

(b)

the saddle is attained at the saddle-point policy

(K_{u}^{s},K_{w}^{s})
from
(\mathcal{K}_{u}^{s}\times\mathcal{K}_{w}^{s})(t,P_{\infty}(t))
on flow and the analogous gains at each jump;

(c)

P_{\infty}
is the unique bounded solution of the
infinite-horizon I-HJI-GRE.

Proof.

The argument is identical to that of Theorem
3.29

with
\mathcal{C}_{Z}\leftarrow\mathcal{I}
,

\mathcal{D}_{Z}\leftarrow\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}
, and the

\sup_{X_{w}}\inf_{X_{u}}
direction replacing
\inf_{X}
. Specifically:

Lower bound (for the minimizer).

Fix any maximizer choice
X_{w}
. Apply (
6.4.2
) with

P=P_{\infty}
. By Isaacs’ condition and the saddle-point
decomposition of Theorem
6.7
, the

\mathcal{I},\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}
contributions are non-negative
when minimized over
X_{u}
(with equality at the saddle). Hence

\inf_{X_{u}}J_{T}(X,X_{1}^{0})\geq\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle-\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}+T),\,EX(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\rangle+R_{T}(P_{\infty}),

with
R_{T}(P):=\int\langle P,\,\mathcal{W}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\sum\langle P(t_{k}^{+}),\,\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle
.
Taking the supremum over
X_{w}
and dividing by
T
, the boundary
terms vanish in the limit (boundedness of
P_{\infty}
and of

EX^{\circ}E^{*}
under the saddle-point closed-loop stability
(A1
∘
)), and
R_{T}(P_{\infty})/T\to\rho^{\circ}
by hypothesis.

Attainment.

At the saddle policy

(K_{u}^{s},K_{w}^{s})\in(\mathcal{K}_{u}^{s}\times\mathcal{K}_{w}^{s})(t,P_{\infty}(t))
,
the
\mathcal{I},\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}
contributions vanish
identically, so (
6.4.2
) gives

J_{T}(X^{\circ},X_{1}^{0})=\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle-\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}+T),\,EX^{\circ}(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\rangle+R_{T}(P_{\infty})
,
and the same limit computation gives the average game value

\rho^{\circ}
.

Uniqueness
follows from Theorem
6.14
.
∎

6.4.4
Necessary condition and existence (infinite horizon)

Theorem 6.26

(Necessary condition and existence)
.

Under (A1
∘
)–(A3
∘
) and Isaacs’ condition, there exists
a unique bounded piecewise-
C^{1}
solution
P_{\infty}\succeq 0
of the
infinite-horizon I-HJI-GRE, and

\rho^{\circ}(X_{1}^{0})\;=\;\limsup_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}\!\left[\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\!\langle P_{\infty}(t),\,\mathcal{W}(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\!\!\sum_{k:\,t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T}\!\!\langle P_{\infty}(t_{k}^{+}),\,\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle\right]

(151)

independently of
X_{1}^{0}
. The right-hand side is finite whenever the
jump count grows at most linearly, i.e.,

\limsup_{T\to\infty}T^{-1}\#\{k:t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T\}<\infty
.
The saddle is attained at the trajectory
X^{\circ}
with gains

(K_{u}^{s},K_{w}^{s})\in(\mathcal{K}_{u}^{s}\times\mathcal{K}_{w}^{s})(t,P_{\infty}(t))

on flow and the analogous gains at each jump.

Proof.

Existence and bounds on
P_{\infty}
.

Theorem
6.15
applied to the homogeneous game provides a
unique bounded piecewise-
C^{1}
solution
P_{\infty}\succeq 0
.

Key identity with
P_{\infty}
.

For any admissible trajectory
X
of (
46
), the
forced HJI key identity (
6.4.2
) with
P=P_{\infty}
on

[t_{0},t_{0}+T]
reads

J_{T}(X,X_{1}^{0})=\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle-\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}+T),\,EX(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\rangle+\!\!\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\!\!\langle\mathcal{I}(t,P_{\infty}(t)),\,X\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t

+\sum_{k:\,t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T}\langle\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}},\,X(t_{k}^{-})\rangle+R_{T}(P_{\infty}),

with

R_{T}(P_{\infty}):=\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\!\langle P_{\infty}(t),\,\mathcal{W}(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\sum_{k:\,t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T}\!\langle P_{\infty}(t_{k}^{+}),\,\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle
. Under
Isaacs’ condition and the
I-HJI-GRE give
\mathcal{I}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G}=0
,
so, with the maximizer playing
K_{w}^{s}
(forcing
W_{w}=0
), the
saddle decomposition (
129
) gives

\langle\mathcal{I}(t,P_{\infty}),\,X\rangle=\operatorname{tr}(W_{u}^{*}\mathcal{I}_{uu}W_{u})\geq 0

a.e. on flow and
\langle\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}},\,X(t_{k}^{-})\rangle\geq 0
at every
jump, for any minimizer response
X_{u}
, with equality along the
saddle-point trajectory
X^{\circ}
. Hence, under the maximizer
policy
K_{w}^{s}
,

J_{T}(X,X_{1}^{0})\;\geq\;\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle-\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}+T),\,EX(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\rangle+R_{T}(P_{\infty}),

(152)

with equality at
X^{\circ}
.

Boundary term is
O(1)
in
T
.

The analogue of Lemma
3.30
for the saddle-point
closed-loop dynamics bounds
\|EX(t)E^{*}\|\leq C_{X}<\infty
uniformly in

t
along any admissible trajectory under (A1
∘
); the proof uses
the geometric factor
\rho^{j}
from the dual decay and never invokes a
bound on
\Delta_{k}
. Since
P_{\infty}
is uniformly bounded,

\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}+T),\,EX(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\rangle\leq\alpha_{2}C_{X}
uniformly in
T
.

Conclusion.

Dividing (
152
) by
T
and taking

\limsup_{T\to\infty}
, both
T^{-1}\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}),\,X_{1}^{0}\rangle
and

T^{-1}\langle P_{\infty}(t_{0}+T),\,EX(t_{0}+T)E^{*}\rangle
vanish, leaving

\limsup_{T}T^{-1}J_{T}(X,X_{1}^{0})\geq\limsup_{T}T^{-1}R_{T}(P_{\infty})
for
any minimizer response
X_{u}
against the maximizer policy
K_{w}^{s}
.
minimizing over
X_{u}
and using that
K_{w}^{s}
is one admissible maximizer
policy (
\max_{X_{w}}\min_{X_{u}}\geq\min_{X_{u}}(\cdot,K_{w}^{s})
) gives

\rho^{\circ}(X_{1}^{0})\geq\limsup_{T}T^{-1}R_{T}(P_{\infty})
. The saddle-point
trajectory
X^{\circ}
saturates (
152
), so the
reverse inequality holds and

\rho^{\circ}(X_{1}^{0})=\limsup_{T}T^{-1}R_{T}(P_{\infty})
, which
is (
151
). The right-hand side does not depend
on
X_{1}^{0}
.

Finiteness.

|R_{T}(P_{\infty})|\leq\alpha_{2}\bigl(T\sup_{t}\|\mathcal{W}(t)\|+\#\{k:t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T\}\cdot\sup_{k}\|\mathcal{W}_{k}\|\bigr)
, so

T^{-1}|R_{T}(P_{\infty})|
is bounded above whenever

\limsup_{T}T^{-1}\#\{k:t_{k}\leq t_{0}+T\}<\infty
.
∎

6.4.5
Equivalence and dual DLMI (infinite horizon)

Corollary 6.27

(Equivalence)
.

Under Isaacs’ condition, the infinite-horizon average game value is
well-posed with
\rho^{\circ}
given by (
150
) if
and only if the infinite-horizon I-HJI-GRE has a bounded
piecewise-
C^{1}
solution
P_{\infty}\succeq 0
. The solution is unique.

Define the average-forcing pairing
\Phi(P)
as
in (
59
). Since the game Riccati-inequality
feasibility set

\{P:\text{Isaacs~\eqref{eq:Isaacs:gen}},\,\mathcal{I}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G}\succeq 0,\,\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{j}}\succeq 0,\,P\text{ bounded}\}
has
P_{\infty}
as its largest element
(Theorem
6.17
), the natural direction for the dual
characterization is the supremum.

Theorem 6.28

(Dual game Riccati inequality, infinite-horizon average game value)
.

Under (A1
∘
)–(A3
∘
),

\rho^{\circ}=\sup_{P}\;\Phi(P)\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\begin{gathered}\textnormal{Isaacs cond.~\eqref{eq:Isaacs:gen} for }\mathcal{I},\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}};\\
\mathcal{I}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G}\succeq 0,\;\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{j}}\succeq 0,\;P\text{ bounded};\end{gathered}

(153)

the supremum is attained at
P_{\infty}
. As in
Theorem
6.17
, the indefinite Isaacs block makes this a
game Riccati inequality (with Schur-complement term), not a plain LMI.

Proof.

By Theorem
6.17
, every feasible
P
satisfies

P\preceq P_{\infty}
pointwise. Since
\mathcal{W}\succeq 0
and

\mathcal{W}_{k}\succeq 0
,
\Phi
is order-preserving, so

\Phi(P)\leq\Phi(P_{\infty})
for every feasible
P
, hence

\sup_{P}\Phi(P)\leq\Phi(P_{\infty})
. By
Theorem
6.26
,
\Phi(P_{\infty})=\rho^{\circ}
, so the
supremum is bounded above by
\rho^{\circ}
and is attained at

P_{\infty}
. Conversely, applying (
6.4.2
) with a
feasible
P
and dividing by
T
gives, after the minimax over

(X_{u},X_{w})
,
\rho^{\circ}\geq\Phi(P)
for every feasible
P
, so

\rho^{\circ}\geq\sup_{P}\Phi(P)
. Hence equality.
∎

6.4.6
Dwell-time conditions

The three dwell-time scenarios of
Sections
3.3.6
–
5.3.7
apply to the
zero-sum game with
\mathcal{C}_{Z}\leftarrow\mathcal{I}
and

\mathcal{D}_{Z}\leftarrow\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}
throughout, and with
the saddle-point feedback
(\mathcal{K}_{u}^{s}\times\mathcal{K}_{w}^{s})

replacing the optimal feedback
\mathcal{K}_{c}
. The forward HJI-GRE
of (
142
) plays the role of the forward
Riccati (
39
), and the Geromel–Colaneri
condition is the frozen game Riccati inequality

\mathcal{I}^{0}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G}^{0}\preceq 0
with Isaacs’
condition (
120
) on
\mathcal{I}^{0}
(
144
),
as in Theorem
6.19
.

Theorem 6.29

(Causal LTAC under periodic, MDT, RDT game)
.

Assume Isaacs’ condition (
120
) uniformly and the
hypotheses of Theorems
6.18
,
6.19
,
and
6.20
respectively (in particular, the periodicity
(periodic case), the time-invariance beyond
T_{\min}
with the frozen
LMI (
144
) (MDT case), and the timer-dependence (RDT case)
of the flow operator
\mathcal{F}
, the jump operator
\mathcal{J}
,
the costs
Z,Z_{k}
(with the game block structure
of Section
6.1.1
),
and
of the forcing data

\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W}_{k}
) together with
(A1
∘
)–(A3
∘
) and the bounded jump-rate conditions of
Theorems
3.35
–
3.38
.
Let
P_{\infty}(\tau)
or
P_{s}(\tau)
denote the causal storage
function from the respective game theorem. Then the causal
long-term average game value satisfies:

(a)

Periodic

\Delta_{k}\equiv T_{p}
:

\rho^{\circ}=T_{p}^{-1}\bigl[\int_{0}^{T_{p}}\langle P_{\infty}(\tau),\,\mathcal{W}(\tau)\rangle d\tau+\langle P_{\infty}(0^{+}),\,\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle\bigr]
.

(b)

MDT

\Delta_{k}\geq T_{\min}
:

\rho^{\circ}\leq\bar{\nu}\int_{0}^{T_{\min}}\!\langle P_{s}(\tau),\,\mathcal{W}\rangle d\tau+(1-\bar{\nu}T_{\min})\langle P_{s}(T_{\min}),\,\mathcal{W}\rangle+\bar{\nu}\langle P_{s}(0^{+}),\,\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle
.

(c)

RDT

\Delta_{k}\in[T_{\min},T_{\max}]
:

\rho^{\circ}\leq\bar{\nu}\int_{T_{\min}}^{T_{\max}}\!(\int_{0}^{\Delta}\langle P_{s}(\tau),\,\mathcal{W}\rangle d\tau)\mu(d\Delta)+\bar{\nu}\langle P_{s}(0^{+}),\,\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle
.

The periodic case is exact and the saddle is attained at

(\mathcal{K}_{u}^{s}\times\mathcal{K}_{w}^{s})(\tau,P_{\infty}(\tau))
on flow;
MDT and RDT give upper bounds for the minimizer under any maximizer
strategy and any admissible dwell-time sequence (or timer-occupation
measure
\mu
).

Proof.

The proofs of Theorems
3.35
–
3.38

apply verbatim with the substitutions

\mathcal{C}_{Z}\leftarrow\mathcal{I}
,

\mathcal{D}_{Z}\leftarrow\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{j}}
, and the
minimax direction (rather than minimization) in the saddle-point step.
Isaacs’ condition uniformly ensures the saddle-point decomposition
applies on the bounded timer interval, and the Geromel–Colaneri
condition
\mathcal{I}^{0}\,\widetilde{/}\,\mathcal{G}^{0}\preceq 0
from
Theorem
6.19

ensures the integral contribution on the tail
\tau>T_{\min}
is
non-positive (so the upper-bound inequality is preserved). All
other steps (timer occupation, jump-rate average) are identical.
∎

6.4.7
Continuous- and discrete-time corollaries

Corollary 6.30

(Continuous-time average game value)
.

Set
N_{T}=0
. Under (A1
∘
)–(A3
∘
),

\rho^{\circ}=\lim_{T}T^{-1}\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\langle P_{\infty}(t),\,\mathcal{W}(t)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t
,
where
P_{\infty}
is the unique bounded
C^{1}
solution of the
continuous-time infinite-horizon HJI equation
(Corollary
6.21
). In the LTI case
(
\mathcal{F},Z,\mathcal{W}
constant),
P_{\infty}
is constant and

\rho^{\circ}=\mathrm{trace}(P_{\infty}\mathcal{W})
.

Corollary 6.31

(Discrete-time average game value)
.

Apply with
\mathcal{F}=0
,
Z=0
on flow,
t_{k}=k_{0}+k
, and

\mathcal{W}=0
on flow. Then

\rho^{\circ}=\lim_{N}N^{-1}\sum_{k=k_{0}}^{k_{0}+N-1}\langle P_{\infty}(k+1),\,\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle
,
where
P_{\infty}
is the unique bounded positive solution of the
algebraic discrete-time HJI-GRE (
137
)
(Corollary
6.22
) and the discrete-time saddle-point
controllers are given by (
138
) at
P_{\infty}
. In the
LTI case,
\rho^{\circ}=\mathrm{trace}(P_{\infty}\mathcal{W})
.

Remark 6.32

(
H_{\infty}
-type interpretation)
.

With the cost block
Z_{ww}=-\gamma^{2}I
as in
the
H_{\infty}
remark in Section
6.3
, the saddle game value rate
\rho^{\circ}
has
the meaning of the worst-case disturbance-to-output energy gain per
unit time, augmented by the
\gamma
-bound on the noise channel. In
the LTI case with
\mathcal{W}=B_{w}B_{w}^{*}
,
\rho^{\circ}=\mathrm{trace}(P_{\infty}B_{w}B_{w}^{*})
is the standard steady-state
H_{\infty}
"
\gamma
-cost"
rate. minimizing
\gamma^{2}
subject to the
DLMI (
153
) for
\rho^{\circ}\leq\gamma^{2}
recovers the
matrix-valued analogue of the classical

H_{\infty}
-optimal cost rate problem.

7
Discussion

Every result in this paper rests on three tools that are independent
of the system class and of the sign of
M
or
P
: the key identity
(Sections
3.1
–
5.2
),
Lemma
2.4
(Extended Schur), and
Lemma
2.5
(Schur inner-product decomposition).
These apply identically to the flow integral, to each jump summand,
and hence to the full impulsive setting.

We have developed a comprehensive optimal control, optimal
steering, dissipativity, and game-theoretic framework for linear
Hermitian matrix-valued impulsive dynamical systems. All results
are obtained under LTV assumptions and are general enough to cover
state-vector continuous-time and discrete-time systems subject to input
noise, Markovian and deterministic switching, and random and
deterministic jumps in the state—including sampled-data systems as a
special case. Consequently, existing results on the optimal control,
the dissipativity analysis, and the minimax control of such systems can
be recovered as corollaries of those obtained here. The dwell-time
conditions likewise generalize existing ones in the literature.

Several directions remain open. A natural and important extension
is to
stochastic
Hermitian matrix-valued systems: replacing
the deterministic flow
E\dot{X}E^{*}=\mathcal{F}(t,X)
by an
Itô-type matrix-valued SDE that includes a diffusion
term
[
20
]

and
stochastic jumps (not considered
here), where both the jump times and the post-jump state have
distributions depending on the pre-jump state. Such a setting
would extend the present deterministic impulsive framework to the
genuinely stochastic case over Hermitian matrices; it is
qualitatively distinct from the Markov jump linear system
framework of
[
25
,
27
]
, where it is the
second-order moment of the vector state that evolves according to
a deterministic impulsive flow as studied in
[
16
]
.
Obtaining necessary causal conditions for MDT (as
Theorem
3.17
does for the periodic case) remains
an open problem, as does extending the framework to switched
impulsive systems with mode-dependent dynamics. The discussion
below addresses a third, structurally distinct direction.

The results of this paper rely on three structural properties of
the cone
\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
(using the notation of this paper; the cone

\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}=\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
of positive semidefinite Hermitian
matrices is also written
\mathcal{S}_{n}^{+}
in the positive-systems
literature): it is a proper cone (closed, convex, pointed, and with
non-empty interior); it is self-dual (
(\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0})^{*}=\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
, a
consequence of the trace inner product being non-degenerate on

\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
); and the dynamics are cone-preserving (
\mathcal{F}
and

\mathcal{J}
leave
\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
invariant). Remarkably, these are
the only structural properties used by the three tools underlying
all proofs: the key identity (whose derivation uses only linearity
of
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{J}
in
X
), the Extended Schur
lemma (Lemma
2.4
), and the Schur inner-product
decomposition (Lemma
2.5
).

It is therefore natural to ask whether the entire framework, or
large parts of it, extend to systems evolving on an arbitrary
proper cone
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
with dynamics that
preserve
\mathcal{K}
and costs that are linear on
\mathcal{K}
.
This generalization is precisely the setting studied in the recent
work on
optimal control on positive cones

(Pates and Rantzer
[
49
]
, Vladu, Megretski, and
Rantzer
[
62
]
), where the Bellman equation for LQ-type
problems can be solved explicitly by a linear function under a
critical structural condition on the cone. Stability analysis for
cone-preserving systems has been studied in, e.g.,
[
57
]
, where linear Lyapunov or storage functions play the
role that inner products with elements of
\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
play here.

The parallel is more than formal. In the
\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
-cone case,
the dual variable
P
in the key identity is itself an element of
the dual cone
(\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0})^{*}=\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
, the linear cost

\langle P,\,X\rangle=\mathrm{trace}(PX)
is the natural duality pairing between

P\in\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
and
X\in\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
, and all LMI/Riccati conditions
are cone constraints in
\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
. For a general proper cone

\mathcal{K}
, the analogous objects are: the dual cone

\mathcal{K}^{*}
, the pairing
\langle\lambda,x\rangle
for

\lambda\in\mathcal{K}^{*}
and
x\in\mathcal{K}
, and the
corresponding linear inequalities on
\mathcal{K}^{*}
. It appears
plausible that the finite-horizon optimal control, the
dissipativity, and the dwell-time results of this paper extend
verbatim to this general-cone setting, with
\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
replaced by

\mathcal{K}
, inner products replaced by the duality pairing, and
Schur complements replaced by the appropriate cone-linear
factorization. We leave the systematic development of this
direction for future work; it would unify the
\mathbb{H}^{n}_{\succeq 0}
-cone
(Hermitian matrix-valued) theory of this paper with the
positive-orthant theory of
[
49
,
62
]
and the
cone-invariant theory of
[
57
]
under a single
abstract framework for cone-preserving impulsive systems.
